Banach space theoretical construction of (primitive) spectra of C ∗ C^{*} -algebras and the Naimark problem revisited Thanks: This work was supported by JSPS KAKENHI Grant Number JP19K14561.
Ryotaro Tanaka

Abstract
. The Naimark problem asks whether C ∗ C^{*} -algebras with singleton spectra are necessarily elementary. The separable case was solved affirmatively in 1953 by Rosenberg. In 2004, Akemann and Weaver gave a counterexample to the Naimark problem for non-separable C ∗ C^{*} -algebras in the setting of ZFC + ♢ ℵ 1 +~\diamondsuit_{\aleph_{1}} , where ♢ ℵ 1 \diamondsuit_{\aleph_{1}} is Jensen’s diamond principle. From this, at least, the affirmative answer to the Naimark problem can no longer be expected although a counterexample is not constructed in ZFC alone yet. In this paper, we study the difference between elementary C ∗ C^{*} -algebras and those with singleton spectra, and find a property P P written in the language of closure operators such that a C ∗ C^{*} -algebra is elementary if and only if it has the singleton spectrum and the property P P . Banach space theoretical construction of (primitive) spectra of C ∗ C^{*} -algebras plays important roles in the theory. Characterizations of type I or CCR or (sub)homogeneous C ∗ C^{*} -algebras are also given. These results are applied to a geometric nonlinear classification problem for C ∗ C^{*} -algebras.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 46L05; Secondary 46B20, 46B80

1.
Introduction

The Naimark problem was a long-standing problem in the representation theory of
C^{*}
-algebras formulated in terms of spectra, where the spectrum
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
of a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
is defined as the set of unitary equivalence classes of irreducible representations of
\mathfrak{A}
.

The Naimark problem
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Are the following statements equivalent?

(i)

\mathfrak{A}
is
elementary
, that is, there exists a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
such that
\mathfrak{A}
is
*
-isomorphic to the algebra
K(\mathcal{H})
of all compact operators on
\mathcal{H}
.

(ii)

\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
is a singleton.

The implication (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) was observed by Naimark
[
29
]
, and the Naimark problem was asked in
[
30
]
based on his work. In 1953, Rosenberg
[
34
]
quickly gave an affirmative answer to the separable (in fact, separably representable) case. However, the non-separable case had remained open for about fifty years. A great progress on the Naimark problem was relatively recently, in 2004, achieved by Akemann and Weaver
[
2
]
. They provide a counterexample to the non-separable case with the aid of Jensen’s diamond principle
\diamondsuit_{\aleph_{1}}
, and noted that the existence of a counterexample to the Naimark problem which is generated by
\aleph_{1}
elements is independent of ZFC (Zermelo-Fraenkel set theory with the axiom of choice); see
[
2
, Theorem 5 and Corollary 7]
. In this direction, very recently, Calderón and Farah
[
11
]
also obtained a counterexample with the aid of
\diamondsuit^{\rm Cohen}~+
CH, where
\diamondsuit^{\rm Cohen}
is a weakening of
\diamondsuit_{\aleph_{1}}
and CH is the continuum hypothesis. Moreover,
\diamondsuit^{\rm Cohen}~+
CH is relatively consistent with the negation of
\diamondsuit_{\aleph_{1}}
; see
[
11
, Appendix B]
. The both ideas of constructing counterexamples to the Naimark problem were based on the Kishimoto-Ozawa-Sakai theorem on the homogeneity of the pure state space of a separable simple
C^{*}
-algebra
[
26
]
; see also the earlier work by Futamura, Kataoka and Kishimoto
[
14
]
. Since
\diamondsuit_{\aleph_{1}}
is relatively consistent with ZFC, the implication (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) cannot be proved in ZFC. However, this does not directly mean that the mentioned implication is false. It is still not known if there exists a counterexample to the Naimark problem in ZFC alone; see
[
11
, Concluding remarks]
. It should be mentioned that partial positive results in the setting of graph
C^{*}
-algebras were given by Suri and Tomforde
[
40
]
. A modified version of the Naimark problem was asked in 2006 by Sakai
[
37
]
and the long history of the Naimark problem toward its modification was summarized in his comprehensive survey
[
38
]
.

The main objective of the present paper is to study the difference between (i) and (ii) in the Naimark problem for non-separable
C^{*}
-algebras, and to find a missing piece for characterizing non-separable elementary
C^{*}
-algebras related to spectra. More precisely, we investigate a property
P
written in the language of closure operators such that (i) is equivalent to (ii) +
P
as well as
P
does not imply (ii). Naturally, if
\hat{A}
is a singleton, it contains very little information as topological space. Hence, we have to consider another natural structure of a
C^{*}
-algebra including the information of its spectrum. A candidate for this is the concept of geometric structure spaces that was introduced in
[
42
]
as closure spaces modeled after (primitive) spectra of
C^{*}
algebras for classifying Banach spaces with respect to their structure determined by Birkhoff-James orthogonality. It was shown in
[
42
, Theorem 5.2]
that the geometric structure space of an abelian
C^{*}
-algebra is homeomorphic to its spectrum. Meanwhile, the geometric structure space of a
C^{*}
-algebra forms a topological space if and only if the algebra is abelian; see
[
46
, Theorem 3.5]
. Thus, geometric structure spaces of
C^{*}
-algebras are rarely topologizable and our work should be carried out in the class of closure spaces that is much wider than that of topological spaces.

The first milestone of our study is Theorem
5.11
which gives a Banach space theoretical way of constructing (primitive) spectra of
C^{*}
-algebras. This will be achieved by applying certain transformations (defined for general closure spaces) to geometric structure spaces of
C^{*}
-algebras. The necessary theories of closure spaces and Banach spaces for this purpose are developed in Sections
3
and
4
, respectively. It turns out in Corollary
5.13
that (primitive) spectra of
C^{*}
-algebras are invariant under (closure space) homeomorphisms between geometric structure spaces of them. Hence, the geometric structure space of a
C^{*}
-algebra remenbers the information of its spectrum in this sense. Another main result of Section
5
, Theorem
5.17
, is a useful tool for reducing our argument to each irreducible representation. Based on this, we develop nonlinear theory for irreducible
C^{*}
-algebras in Section
6
. The machinery is constructed by normal parts of geometric structure spaces and finite-homeomorphisms between them. In Theorem
6.9
, we characterize continuity of such mappings induced by pairs of bounded linear (or conjugate-linear) isomorphisms between complex Hilbert spaces on which given irreducible
C^{*}
-algebras act. Moreover, Theorem
6.18
, the most technical part of the present paper, describes the possible forms of continuous finite-homeomorphisms between normal parts of geometric structure spaces of irreducible
C^{*}
-algebras acting on infinite-dimensional complex Hilbert spaces. We revisit the Naimark problem in Section
7
. The main result is Theorem
7.7
, which characterizes non-separable elementary
C^{*}
-algebras in terms of geometric structure spaces. Since the geometric structure space and spectrum of a
C^{*}
-algebra can be recognized as Banach space structures, this gives a Banach space theoretical characterization of elementary
C^{*}
-algebras among all
C^{*}
-algebras. Similar characterizations of type I or CCR or (sub)homogeneous
C^{*}
-algebras are also given in Theorem
7.9
. The results in Sections
6
and
7
are applied to a nonlinear classification problem for
C^{*}
-algebras considered in Section
8
which asks whether
C^{*}
-algebras are Jordan
*
-isomorphic if and only if they have homeomorphic geometric structure spaces. Partial affirmative answers to this problem are given in Theorems
8.12
and
8.15
. The paper concludes with two geometric nonlinear classification problems for
C^{*}
-algebras related to the above-mentioned one.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Categories

A
category

C
consists of
objects

\mathrm{ob}(\textbf{C})
,
hom-set

\textbf{C}(X,Y)
of objects (whose elements are called
morphisms
), and
composition
of morphisms
\textbf{C}(X,Y)\times\textbf{C}(Y,Z)\ni(f,g)\mapsto g\circ f\in\textbf{C}(X,Z)
. These data must satisfy the following conditions:

(i)

If
f:X\to Y
,
g:Y\to Z
and
h:Z\to W
are morphisms, then
h\circ(g\circ f)=(h\circ g)\circ f
.

(ii)

For each object
X
, there exists a morphism
\mathrm{id}_{X}:X\to X
such that
\mathrm{id}_{X}\circ f=f
and
g\circ\mathrm{id}_{X}=g
whenever
f:Y\to X
and
g:X\to Z
are morphisms.

A morphism
f:X\to Y
is called an
isomorphism
if there exists a morphism
g:Y\to X
such that
g\circ f=\mathrm{id}_{X}
and
f\circ g=\mathrm{id}_{Y}
, in which case,
g
is the
inverse
of
f
and written as
f^{-1}
. If there exists an isomorphism
f:X\to Y
, then objects
X
and
Y
are
isomorphic
.

Let
C
is a category. A
subcategory

D
of
C
is a category such that
\mathrm{ob}(\textbf{D})\subset\mathrm{ob}(\textbf{C})
and
\textbf{D}(X,Y)\subset\textbf{C}(X,Y)
for each objects
X,Y
of
D
. A subcategory
D
of
C
is said to be
full
if
\textbf{D}(X,Y)=\textbf{C}(X,Y)
for each objects
X,Y
of
D
. We note that a full subcategory is uniquely determined by
\mathrm{ob}(\textbf{D})
.

Let
C
and
D
be categories. A
functor

F
from
C
to
D
is mappings
X\mapsto F(X)
from
\mathrm{ob}(\textbf{C})
to
\mathrm{ob}(\textbf{D})
and
f\mapsto F(f)
from
\textbf{C}(X,Y)
to
\textbf{D}(F(X),F(Y))
such that
F(g\circ f)=F(g)\circ F(f)
and
F(\mathrm{id}_{X})=\mathrm{id}_{F(X)}
. The
identity functor

\mathrm{id}_{\textbf{C}}
on
C
is the functor given by
F(X)=X
and
F(f)=f
. Let
F
be a functor from
C
to
D
. If there exists a functor
G
from
D
to
C
such that
G\circ F=\mathrm{id}_{\textbf{C}}
and
F\circ G=\mathrm{id}_{\textbf{D}}
, then
F
is called an
isomorphism of categories
. The categories
C
and
D
are
isomorphic
if there exists an isomorphism of categories from
C
to
D
. See, for example,
[
17
]
for the general theory of categories.

2.2.
Banach spaces

The scalar field of a Banach space is denoted by
\mathbb{K}
. A Banach space over
\mathbb{R}
(or over
\mathbb{C}
) is called a
real
(or
complex
) Banach space. For a Banach space
X
, let
B_{X}=\{x\in X:\|x\|\leq 1\}
and
S_{X}=\{x\in X:\|x\|=1\}
. The sets
B_{X}
and
S_{X}
are called the unit ball and unit sphere of
X
, respectively. If
X
is a complex Banach space, then its complex conjugate is denoted by
\overline{X}
, that is,
\overline{X}
is the Banach space having the same underlying vector space, addition and norm as
X
and the conjugate scalar multiplication
(c,x)\mapsto\overline{c}x
. If
\mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert space, then so is
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
. The inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{a}
on
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
is given by
\langle x,y\rangle_{a}=\langle y,x\rangle
for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}
. The (continuous) dual space of
X
is denoted by
X^{*}
. Although the symbols
A^{*}
and
\rho^{*}
are also used for the adjoint of elements
A
and
\rho
of a
C^{*}
-algebra and its dual, the meanings will be clear from the context. A subset
C
of a Banach space is said to be
convex
if
(1-t)x+ty\in C
whenever
x,y\in C
and
t\in[0,1]
. A subset
D
of a convex set
C
is called a
face
of
C
if
x,y\in C
and
(1-t)x+ty\in D
for some
t\in(0,1)
implies that
x,y\in D
. If further
D\neq C
, then
D
is said to be
proper
. A
maximal face
of a convex set
C
is a proper face of
C
that is not contained in other proper faces of
C
. If a singleton
\{x\}
is a face of
C
, then it is called an
extreme point
of
C
. The set of all extreme points of
C
is denoted by
\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(C)
. An important example of a face of
B_{X}
is an
exposed face
which can be represented as
f^{-1}(\{1\})\cap B_{X}
for some
f\in S_{X^{*}}
. It is well-known that each maximal face of
B_{X}
is exposed. Let
X
and
Y
are Banach spaces over the same scalar field. Then, a mapping
T:X\to Y
is called an
isometry
if
\|Tx\|=\|x\|
for each
x\in X
. If an isometry
T
maps
X
onto
Y
, then it is called an
isometric isomorphism
. If there exists an isometric isomorphism
T:X\to Y
, then
X
is
isometrically isomorphic
to
Y
. For basics of Banach spaces needed in the present paper, see
[
28
]
.

2.3.
C^{*}
-algebras

A
C^{*}
-algebra is a complex Banach algebra with involution that satisfies the Naimark axiom
\|A^{*}A\|=\|A\|^{2}
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. A
C^{*}
-algebra is said to be
unital
if it contains the multiplicative unit
\mathbf{1}
. For a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, let
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathfrak{A}
if
\mathfrak{A}
is unital, and let
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
be its unitization of
\mathfrak{A}
if
\mathfrak{A}
is non-unital, where the unitization of a non-unital
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
is the vector space
\mathfrak{A}\oplus\mathbb{C}
endowed with the
C^{*}
-norm

\|(A,c)\|=\sup\{\|AB+cB\|:B\in B_{\mathfrak{A}}\}.

The multiplicative unit of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
is
(0,1)
and an element
(A,c)
of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
is denoted by
A+c\mathbf{1}
. If
\mathfrak{A}
is a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of a unital
C^{*}
-algebra, then it is natural to identify
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
with the
C^{*}
-algebra generated by
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathbf{1}
. The spectrum
\mathop{\mathrm{sp}}\nolimits(A)
of
A\in\mathfrak{A}
is considered in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
. Let
A
be an element of a
C^{*}
-algebra. Then,
A
is said to be
self-adjoint
if
A^{*}=A
, and
positive
, denoted by
A\geq 0
, if it is self-adjoint and
\mathop{\mathrm{sp}}\nolimits(A)
consists of non-negative real numbers. A mapping
\varphi
from a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
into another
\mathfrak{B}
is called a
*
-homomorphism
if it is linear,
\varphi(A^{*})=\varphi(A)^{*}
and
\varphi(AB)=\varphi(A)\varphi(B)
for each
A,B\in\mathfrak{A}
. It is known that
*
-homomorphisms between
C^{*}
-algebras are automatically bounded and norm-one. An injective
*
-homomorphism is called a
*
-isomorphism
. We note that each
*
-isomorphism is an isometry. If there exists a
*
-isomorphism from
\mathfrak{A}
onto
\mathfrak{B}
, then
\mathfrak{A}
is
*
-isomorphic
to
\mathfrak{B}
. For basics of
C^{*}
-algebras, for example, the readers are referred to
[
8
,
10
,
12
,
23
,
24
,
32
]
.

2.4.
States and representations

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. For each
\rho\in\mathfrak{A}^{*}
, define another element
\rho^{*}\in\mathfrak{A}^{*}
by
\rho^{*}(A)=\overline{\rho(A^{*})}
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. A functional
\rho\in\mathfrak{A}^{*}
is said to be
hermitian
if
\rho^{*}=\rho
, and
positive
if
\rho(A)\geq 0
whenever
A\in\mathfrak{A}
and
A\geq 0
. Each positive functional is hermitian. The
state space

\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{A})
of
\mathfrak{A}
is given by

\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{A})=\left\{\rho\in\mathfrak{A}^{*}:\|\rho\|=\lim_{\lambda}\rho(E_{\lambda})=1\right\}

for any fixed approximate identity
(E_{\lambda})_{\lambda}
in
\mathfrak{A}
; see
[
12
, Lemmas I.9.5 and I.9.9]
. An element of
\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{A})
is called a
state
of
\mathfrak{A}
. If
\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{A}))
, then
\rho
is said to be
pure
. The set of all pure states of
\mathfrak{A}
, that is,
\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{A}))
, is called the
pure state space
of
\mathfrak{A}
. We note that
\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{A})
is a (possibly not weakly
∗
closed) face of
B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}}
and each pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
belongs to
\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
. As is well-known, each (pure) state of a non-unital
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
extends uniquely to a (pure) state of its unitization
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
; see
[
10
, Proposition II.6.2.5 and Subsection II.6.3]
.

Let
B(\mathcal{H})
denote the
C^{*}
-algebra of all bounded linear operators on a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. A
*
-homomorphism from a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
into some
B(\mathcal{H})
is called a
representation
of
\mathfrak{A}
. An injective representation of
\mathfrak{A}
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
(that is, a
*
-isomorphism from
\mathfrak{A}
into
B(\mathcal{H})
) is called a
faithful
representation of
\mathfrak{A}
. Let
\pi:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H})
is a representation of
\mathfrak{A}
. Then,
\pi
is said to be
irreducible
if the
C^{*}
-subalgebra
\pi(\mathfrak{A})
of
B(\mathcal{H})

acts irreducibly
on
\mathcal{H}
, that is, there is no nontrivial subspace of
\mathcal{H}
invariant under
\pi(\mathfrak{A})
. If
\pi_{1}:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H}_{1})
and
\pi_{2}:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H}_{2})
are representations of
\mathfrak{A}
, then
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are
unitarily equivalent
if exists an isometric isomorphism
U:\mathcal{H}_{1}\to\mathcal{H}_{2}
such that
U\pi_{1}(A)U^{*}=\pi_{2}(A)
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
.

By the Gelfand-Naimark-Segal (GNS) construction, each state
\rho
of a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
generates a representation
\pi_{\rho}:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})
and a unit cyclic vector
x_{\rho}\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
for
\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})
such that
\rho(A)=\langle\pi_{\rho}(A)x_{\rho},x_{\rho}\rangle
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. As an application of GNS construction, we can always find a faithful representation
\pi:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H})
. In this sense, a
C^{*}
-algebra is considered as a norm-closed
*
-subalgebra of some
B(\mathcal{H})
. In the rest of this paper, “Hilbert spaces” always mean
complex
Hilbert spaces if we work with
C^{*}
-algebras. It is known that the representation
\pi_{\rho}
is irreducible if and only if
\rho
is pure.

2.5.
Spectra and Primitive spectra

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Then, an ideal
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathfrak{A}
is said to be
primitive
if
\mathcal{I}=\ker\pi
for some irreducible representation
\pi
of
\mathfrak{A}
. The
primitive spectrum

\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{A})
of
\mathfrak{A}
is the set of all primitive ideals of
\mathfrak{A}
equipped with the hull-kernel topology, where the hull-kernel closure of
S\subset\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{A})
is given by
\{\mathcal{I}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{A}):\bigcap\{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}:\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\in S\}\subset\mathcal{I}\}
. The
spectrum

\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
is defined as the set of all unitary equivalence classes of irreducible representations of
\mathfrak{A}
that equips with the pullback topology with respect to the mapping
\pi\mapsto\ker\pi
from
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
onto
\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{A})
. As was noted in
[
32
, Theorem 3.13.2]
, each irreducible representation is unitarily equivalent to
\pi_{\rho}
for some pure state
\rho
of
\mathfrak{A}
. Spectra and primitive spectra of
C^{*}
-algebras are basic tools in the representation theory of
C^{*}
-algebras. One of the most successful applications of (primitive) spectra to the theory of operator algebras is characterizations of Type I
C^{*}
-algebras which is known as the Glimm-Sakai theorem.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Then,
A\in\mathfrak{A}
is said to be
abelian
if the norm-closure of
A\mathfrak{A}A
(hereditary
C^{*}
-algebra) is abelian. If every quotient of
\mathfrak{A}
contains a nonzero abelian element, then
\mathfrak{A}
is
of internally type I
. Meanwhile,
\mathfrak{A}
is
of bidual type I
if
\mathfrak{A}^{**}
is a type I von Neumann algebra. By a deep result by Glimm
[
15
]
and Sakai
[
35
,
36
]
, these two definitions of type I
C^{*}
-algebras are mutually equivalent and they are characterized in terms of irreducible representations. Let
\pi:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H})
be an irreducible representation. Then,
\pi
is a
CCR representation
if
K(\mathcal{H})=\pi(\mathfrak{A})
, and
GCR representation
if
K(\mathcal{H})\subset\pi(\mathfrak{A})
. If every irreducible representation of
\mathfrak{A}
is CCR (or GCR), then
\mathfrak{A}
is called a
CCR
(or
GCR
)
C^{*}
-algebra. The following is (a part of) the Glimm-Sakai theorem.

The Glimm-Sakai theorem
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Then, the following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathfrak{A}
is of internally type I.

(ii)

\mathfrak{A}
is of bidual type I.

(iii)

\mathfrak{A}
is GCR.

Moreover, (i) to (iii) implies the following:

(iv)

The mapping
\pi\mapsto\ker\pi
from
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
onto
\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{A})
is injective.

If
\mathfrak{A}
is separable, then (i) to (iv) are equivalent.

The separable case was due to Glimm, and the general case was completed by Sakai. We note that the separability is required to obtain (i) to (iii) from (iv). Indeed, if
\mathfrak{A}
is a non-separable simple
C^{*}
-algebra, then the implication (iv) to (iii) is (essentially the same as) the Naimark problem.

3.
Closure spaces

We begin with drawing the framework of our study. For a set
K
, the symbol
2^{K}
stands for the power set of
K
. First, we introduce the concept of (Fréchet (V)) closure operators, which is a basis for the theory developed in the present paper; see
[
33
,
39
]
for backgrounds.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
K
be a set. Then,
c:2^{K}\to 2^{K}
is called a
(Fréchet (V)) closure operator
if it satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

c(\emptyset)=\emptyset
.

(ii)

S\subset c(S)
for each
S\subset K
.

(iii)

c(c(S))=c(S)
for each
S\subset K
.

(iv)

c(S_{1})\subset c(S_{2})
whenever
S_{1}\subset S_{2}\subset K
.

The ordered pair
(K,c)
is called a
(Fréchet (V)) closure space
.

Remark 3.2
.

There is another well-known concept of closure operators. Let
K
be a set. Then,
c:2^{K}\to 2^{K}
is called a
preclosure operator
or
Čech closure operator
if it satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

c(\emptyset)=\emptyset
.

(ii)

S\subset c(S)
for each
S\subset K
.

(iii)

c(S_{1}\cup S_{2})=c(S_{1})\cup C(S_{2})
for each subsets
S_{1},S_{2}
of
K
.

Although preclosure operators are also called closure operators in the literature, in this paper, the term “a closure opeartor” always means a Fréchet (V) closure operator.

In a closure space, we can consider closed sets in a natural manner.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
(K,c)
be a closure space, and let
S\subset K
. Then,
S
is said to be
closed
if
c(S)=S
. Let
\mathfrak{C}(K,c)
denote the family of all closed subsets of
K
, that is,
\mathfrak{C}(K,c)=\{S\subset K:c(S)=S\}
.

Remark 3.4
.

Let
(K,c)
be a closure space. Then, the identity

c(S)=\bigcap\{C\in\mathfrak{C}(K,c):S\subset C\}

holds for each
S\subset K
. Indeed, if
C\in\mathfrak{C}(K,c)
and
S\subset C
, then
c(S)\subset c(C)=C
. The converse follows from the fact that
S\subset c(S)\in\mathfrak{C}(K,c)
.

Remark 3.5
.

Let
(K,c)
be a closure space. Then,
\emptyset,K\in\mathfrak{C}(K,c)
by
c(\emptyset)=\emptyset
and
K\subset c(K)\subset K
.

Let
K
be a set, and let
c:2^{K}\to 2^{K}
. Then, as is well-known, the family
\{S\subset K:c(S)=S\}
satisfies the axioms of closed sets if and only if it satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

c(\emptyset)=\emptyset
.

(ii)

S\subset c(S)
for each
S\subset K
.

(iii)

c(c(S))=c(S)
for each
S\subset K
.

(iv)

c(S_{1}\cup S_{2})=c(S_{1})\cup c(S_{2})
for each subsets
S_{1},S_{2}
of
K
.

This set of conditions is known as the Kuratowski closure axioms.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
(K,c)
be a closure space. Then,
(K,c)
is said to be
topologizable
if the closure operator
c
satisfies the Kuratowski closure axioms. If
(K,c)
is topologizable, then it is called a
topological (closure) space
and
K
is equipped with the topology induced by
\mathfrak{C}(K,c)
.

Remark 3.7
.

If
(K,c)
is a closure space, and if
S_{1},S_{2}
are subsets of
K
, then the monotonicity of
c
implies that
c(S_{1}\cup S_{2})\supset c(S_{1})\cup c(S_{2})
. Hence,
(K,c)
is topologizable if and only if
c(S_{1}\cup S_{2})\subset c(S_{1})\cup c(S_{2})
for each subsets
S_{1},S_{2}
of
K
. It should be mentioned that this was already noted in the restricted case of geometric structure spaces; see
[
42
, Lemma 3.6]
.

The concept of closure spaces generalizes that of topological spaces. Hence, it is natural to consider appropriate morphisms between them.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
(K,c)
and
(L,d)
be closure spaces, and let
f:K\to L
. Then,
f
is said to be
continuous
if
f(c(S))\subset d(f(S))
for each
S\subset K
. If
f
is bijecitive, and if both
f
and
f^{-1}
are continuous, then
f
is called a
homeomorphism
. The closure spaces
(K,c)
and
(L,d)
are said to be
homeomorphic
, denoted by
(K,c)\simeq(L,d)
, if there exists a homeomorphism between
(K,c)
and
(L,d)
.

Remark 3.9
.

Let
(K,c)
and
(L,d)
be closure spaces, and let
f:K\to L
. Then,
f
is continuous if and only if
f^{-1}(S^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{C}(K,c)
whenever
S^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}(L,d)
, and
f
is a homeomorphism if and only if
f(c(S))=d(f(S))
for each
S\subset K
. Although these facts were essentially already noted in
[
44
, Remarks 3.10 and 3.11]
, we realize them here for the reader’s convenience.

Suppose that
f
is continuous and
S^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}(Y,d)
. Then, it follows from

f(c(f^{-1}(S^{\prime})))\subset d(f(f^{-1}(S^{\prime})))\subset d(S^{\prime})=S^{\prime}

that
c(f^{-1}(S^{\prime}))\subset f^{-1}(S^{\prime})
. Hence,
f^{-1}(S^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{C}(K,c)
. Conversely, if
f^{-1}(S^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{C}(K,c)
whenever
S^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}(L,d)
, and if
S\subset K
, then we infer that
d(f(S))\in\mathfrak{C}(L,d)
and
f^{-1}(d(f(S))\in\mathfrak{C}(K,c)
. Since
S\subset f^{-1}(f(S))\subset f^{-1}(d(f(S)))
, we obtain

c(S)\subset c(f^{-1}(d(f(S))))=f^{-1}(d(f(S))),

which implies that
f(c(S))\subset d(f(S))
. This shows that
f
is continuous.

Next, we assume that
f
is a homeomorphism. Then, it follows from
f(c(S))\subset d(f(S))
and
f^{-1}(d(f(S)))\subset c(f^{-1}(f(S))=c(S)
that
f(c(S))=d(f(S))
for each
S\subset K
. Conversely, suppose that
f(c(S))=d(f(S))
for each
S\subset K
. If
S^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}(L,d)
, then

f(c(f^{-1}(S^{\prime})))=d(f(f^{-1}(S^{\prime})))=d(S^{\prime})=S^{\prime}

and
c(f^{-1}(S^{\prime}))=f^{-1}(S^{\prime})
. Therefore,
f
is continuous. Meanwhile, if
S\in\mathfrak{C}(K,c)
, then
d(f(S))=f(c(S))=f(S)
, that is,
(f^{-1})^{-1}(S)=f(S)\in\mathfrak{C}(L,d)
. Thus,
f^{-1}
is also continuous.

Remark 3.10
.

Let
(K,c)
be a closure space. Then, the identity mapping
\mathop{\textrm{id}}_{K}
on
K
is a homeomorphism on
K
. This is realized by the equation
\mathop{\textrm{id}}_{K}(c(S))=c(S)=c(\mathop{\textrm{id}}_{K}(S))
for each
S\subset K
.

The following proposition is what is needed for defining the category of closure spaces.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
(K,c)
,
(L,d)
and
(M,e)
be closure spaces, and let
f:K\to L
and
g:L\to M
. If
f
and
g
are continuous, then
g\circ f:K\to M
is continuous.

Proof.

Let
S\subset K
. Then, it follows that

(g\circ f)(c(S))=g(f(c(S)))\subset g(d(f(S)))\subset e(g(f(S)))=e((g\circ f)(S)).

Hence,
g\circ f
is continuous.
∎

Now, we introduce the category of closure spaces.

Definition 3.12
.

The category of (Fréchet (V)) closure spaces, denoted as
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Cl}_{\textrm{\rm F}}}
, is the category whose objects are (Fréchet (V)) closure spaces. The morphisms between closure spaces
(K,c)
and
(L,d)
are the continous mappings, and the composition of morphisms is the composition of mappings. The full subcategory of
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Cl}_{\textrm{\rm F}}}
whose objects are topological closure spaces is denoted by
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Top}_{\textrm{\rm F}}}
.

Remark 3.13
.

The isomorphisms in
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Cl}_{\textrm{\rm F}}}
are just the homeomorphisms.

As the following proposition says,
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Top}_{\textrm{\rm F}}}
has some desirable properties. Recall that a full subcategory of a category is said to be
strictly full
if it is isomorphism-closed. Let
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Top}}
denote the category of topological spaces.

Proposition 3.14
.

\mathop{\textrm{\bf Top}_{\textrm{\rm F}}}
is strictly full subcategory of
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Cl}_{\textrm{\rm F}}}
. Moreover,
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Top}_{\textrm{\rm F}}}
and
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Top}}
are isomorphic.

Proof.

The proof of the former statement is essentially the same as that of
[
42
, Theorem 3.11]
. Let
(K,c),(L,d)
are closure spaces. Suppose that
(K,c)
is topologizable, and that
f:K\to L
be a homeomorphism. Since
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Top}_{\textrm{\rm F}}}
is full, it is sufficient to show that
L
is topologizable. Let
S^{\prime}_{1},S^{\prime}_{2}\subset L
. Then, it follows from

\displaystyle f^{-1}(d(S^{\prime}_{1}\cup S^{\prime}_{2}))=c(f^{-1}(S^{\prime}_{1}\cup S^{\prime}_{2}))

\displaystyle=c(f^{-1}(S^{\prime}_{1})\cup f^{-1}(S^{\prime}_{2}))

\displaystyle=c(f^{-1}(S^{\prime}_{1}))\cup c(f^{-1}(S^{\prime}_{2}))

\displaystyle=f^{-1}(d(S^{\prime}_{1}))\cup f^{-1}(d(S^{\prime}_{2}))

that

\displaystyle d(S^{\prime}_{1}\cup S^{\prime}_{2})=f(f^{-1}(d(S^{\prime}_{1}))\cup f^{-1}(d(S^{\prime}_{2})))

\displaystyle=f(f^{-1}(d(S^{\prime}_{1})))\cup f(f^{-1}(d(S^{\prime}_{2})))

\displaystyle=d(S^{\prime}_{1})\cup d(S^{\prime}_{2}).

This shows that
L
is topologizable.

Now, we note that each topological space
(K,\mathfrak{T})
can be viewed as a closure space by setting
c_{\mathfrak{T}}(S)=\overline{S}^{\mathfrak{T}}
for each
S\subset K
. Moreover,
(K,c_{\mathfrak{T}})
is topologizable and
\mathfrak{T}=\{K\setminus S:S\in\mathfrak{C}(K,c_{\mathfrak{T}})\}
. Meanwhile, if
(K,c)
is a topological closure space, then
\mathcal{I}_{c}=\{K\setminus S:S\in\mathfrak{C}(K,c)\}
is a topology on
K
and
c=c_{\mathfrak{T}_{c}}
. Remark also that
f:(K,\mathfrak{T})\to(L,\mathfrak{U})
is continuous if and only if
f:(K,c_{\mathfrak{T}})\to(L,c_{\mathfrak{U}})
is continuous.

Define functors
F
from
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Top}}
to
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Top}_{\textrm{\rm F}}}
and
G
from
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Top}_{\textrm{\rm F}}}
to
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Top}}
by
F((K,\mathfrak{T}))=(K,c_{\mathfrak{T}})
for each topological space
(K,\mathfrak{T})
,
F(f)=f
for each continuous mapping
f
between topological spaces,
G((K,c))=(K,\mathfrak{T}_{c})
for each topological closure space
(K,c)
, and
G(f)=f
for each continuous mapping
f
between topological closure spaces. Then,
FG
and
GF
are the identity functors on
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Top}_{\textrm{\rm F}}}
and
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Top}}
, respectively. Thus,
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Top}}
and
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Top}_{\textrm{\rm F}}}
are (covariantly) isomorphic.
∎

As was shown in Proposition
3.14
, each topological space
(K,\mathfrak{T})
naturally corresponds to the topological closure space
(K,c_{\mathfrak{T}})
. In the rest of this paper, we identify
(K,\mathfrak{T})
with
(K,c_{\mathfrak{T}})
unless otherwise stated.

Next, we consider subspaces of a closure space.

Definition 3.15
.

Let
(K,c)
be a closure space, and let
A\subset K
. Define a mapping
c_{A}:2^{A}\to 2^{A}
by
c_{A}(S)=c(S)\cap A
for each
S\subset A
. Then, the ordered pair
(A,c_{A})
is called a
subspace
of
(K,c)
.

It will turn out that subspaces of closure spaces are indeed closure spaces.

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
(K,c)
be a closure space, and let
A\subset K
. Then,
(A,c_{A})
is a closure space. Moreover, the natural injection
\iota_{A}:A\to B
is continuous whenever
A\subset B\subset K
, where
\iota_{A}
is defined by
\iota_{A}(t)=t
for each
t\in A
.

Proof.

Since
c(\emptyset)=\emptyset
, we have
c_{A}(\emptyset)=\emptyset
. If
S\subset A
, then it follows from
S\subset c(S)
that

S=S\cap A\subset c(S)\cap A=c_{A}(S).

We also note that
c(c_{A}(S))\subset c(S)\cap c(A)
since
c_{A}(S)=c(S)\cap A\subset c(S)\cap c(A)
and

c(c(S)\cap c(A))\subset c(c(S))\cap c(c(A))=c(S)\cap c(A),

which implies that

c_{A}(c_{A}(S))=c(c_{A}(S))\cap A\subset c(S)\cap c(A)\cap A=c(S)\cap A=c_{A}(S).

Finally, if
S_{1}\subset S_{2}\subset A
, then

c_{A}(S_{1})=c(S_{1})\cap A\subset c(S_{2})\cap A=c_{A}(S_{2})

by
c(S_{1})\subset c(S_{2})
. Therefore,
c_{A}
is a closure operator on
A
.

The continuity of
\iota_{A}
follows from the fact that

\iota_{A}(c_{A}(S))=c_{A}(S)=c(S)\cap A\subset c(S)\cap B=c_{B}(S)=c_{B}(\iota_{A}(S))

for each
S\subset A
.
∎

Remark 3.17
.

Let
(K,c)
be a closure space, and let
A\subset K
. If
A
is closed, then
c(S)\subset c(A)=A
whenever
S\subset A
. Hence,

c_{A}(S)=c(S)\cap A=c(S)

for each
S\subset A
, that is,
c_{A}=c|2^{A}
.

As in the case of topological spaces, continuous functions between closure spaces restrict to continuous functions between subspaces.

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
(K,c)
and
(L,d)
be closure spaces, and let
A\subset K
. If
f:K\to L
is continuous (or a homeomorphism), then
f|A:A\to f(A)
is continuous (or a homeomorphism).

Proof.

Let
S\subset A
. If
f
is continuous, then

f(c_{A}(S))=f(c(S)\cap A)\subset f(c(S))\cap f(A)\subset d(f(S))\cap f(A)=c_{f(A)}(f(S)).

Hence,
f|A:A\to f(A)
is continuous. In the case that
f
is homeomorphism, we obtain

f(c_{A}(S))=f(c(S)\cap A)=f(c(S))\cap f(A)=d(f(S))\cap f(A)=c_{f(A)}(f(S)),

which implies that
f|A:A\to f(A)
is a homeomorphism.
∎

The following lemma gives a way of constructing the
pullback
closure operator.

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
K
be a set, let
(L,d)
be a closure space, and let
f:K\to L
. Define a mapping
c:2^{K}\to 2^{K}
by
c(S)=f^{-1}(d(f(S)))
for each
S\subset K
. Then, the following hold:

(i)

c
is a closure operator on
K
and
f
is continuous with respect to the closure operators
c
and
d
.

(ii)

If
(L,d)
is topologizable, then so is
(K,c)
.

(iii)

If
f
is surjective, then
f(c(S))=d(f(S))
for each
S\subset K
. In particular,
f
is a closed mapping with respect to the closure operators
c
and
d
.

(iv)

If
f
is bijective, then it is a homeomorphism with respect to the closure operators
c
and
d
.

Proof.

(i) First, we note that
f(c(S))=f(f^{-1}(d(f(S))))\subset d(f(S))
for each
S\subset K
. From this, it is sufficient to prove that
c
is a closure operator on
K
. Since
d(\emptyset)=\emptyset
, we have
c(\emptyset)=f^{-1}(d(f(\emptyset)))=\emptyset
. If
S\subset K
, then
S\subset f^{-1}(f(S))\subset f^{-1}(d(f(S))=c(S)
and

c(c(S))=f^{-1}(d(f(c(S))))\subset f^{-1}(d(d(f(S))))=f^{-1}(d(f(S)))=c(S).

Moreover,

c(S_{1})=f^{-1}(d(f(S_{1})))\subset f^{-1}(d(f(S_{2})))=c(S_{2})

whenever
S_{1}\subset S_{2}\subset K
. This shows that
c
is a closure operator on
K
.

(ii) Suppose that
(L,d)
is topologizable, and that
S_{1},S_{2}
are subsets of
K
. Then, it follows that

\displaystyle c(S_{1}\cup S_{2})=f^{-1}(d(f(S_{1}\cup S_{2})))

\displaystyle=f^{-1}(d(f(S_{1})))\cup f^{-1}(d(f(S_{2})))

\displaystyle=c(S_{1})\cup c(S_{2}).

Hence,
(K,c)
is topologizable.

(iii) If
f
is surjective, then
f(f^{-1}(S^{\prime}))=S^{\prime}
for each
S^{\prime}\subset L
. Therefore,
f(c(S))=d(f(S))
for each
S\subset K
. In particular, if
S
is closed, then
f(S)=f(c(S))=d(f(S))
, that is,
f(S)
is closed.

(iv) If
f
is bijective, then it is continuous by (i) and closed by (iii). Thus,
f
is a homeomorphism.
∎

The preceding lemma is useful for constructing new closure spaces from a given closure space. The following is the basis for our construction.

Definition 3.20
.

Let
(K,c)
be a closure space. Define binary relations on
K
by declaring that
t_{1}\rightleftarrows t_{2}
if
t_{1}=t_{2}
or
\{t_{1},t_{2}\}\not\in\mathfrak{C}(K,c)
, and that
t_{1}\sim t_{2}
if there exists a finite subset
\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\}
of
K
such that
t_{1}\rightleftarrows s_{1}\rightleftarrows\cdots\rightleftarrows s_{n}\rightleftarrows t_{2}
.

Remark 3.21
.

Let
(K,c)
be a closure space, and let
t,t_{1},t_{2}\in K
. Then, the following hold:

(i)

t\rightleftarrows t
.

(ii)

If
t_{1}\rightleftarrows t_{2}
, then
t_{2}\rightleftarrows t_{1}
.

(iii)

If
t_{1}\rightleftarrows t_{2}
, then
t_{1}\sim t_{2}
.

The statements (i) and (ii) are obvious from the definition. Moreover, if
t_{1}\rightleftarrows t_{2}
, then
\{t_{1}\}
is a finite subset of
\mathfrak{S}(X)
and
t_{1}\rightleftarrows t_{1}\rightleftarrows t_{2}
, which implies that
t_{1}\sim t_{2}
. This proves (iii).

In particular, if “
\rightleftarrows
” is transitive, then it becomes an equivalence relation on
K
, in which case, “
\rightleftarrows
” is equivalent to “
\sim
.”

Remark 3.22
.

Let
(K,c)
be a closure space. If
\{t_{1},t_{2}\}\in\mathfrak{C}(K,c)
for each
t_{1},t_{2}\in K
, then “
\sim
” is equivalent to “
=
.” The converse is also true if
(K,c)
is
T_{1}
. Indeed, if
\{t_{1},t_{2}\}\in\mathfrak{C}(K,c)
for each
t_{1},t_{2}\in K
, and if
t_{1}\rightleftarrows t_{2}
, then
t_{1}=t_{2}
. This shows that
t_{1}\sim t_{2}
if and only if
t_{1}=t_{2}
. Conversely, if
(K,c)
is
T_{1}
, and if
\{t_{1},t_{2}\}\not\in\mathfrak{C}(K,c)
for some
t_{1},t_{2}\in K
, then
t_{1}\sim t_{2}
. We note that
t_{1}\neq t_{2}
since each singleton is closed.

It will turn out that the relation “
\sim
” is an equivalence relation.

Proposition 3.23
.

Let
(K,c)
be a closure space. Then, “
\sim
” is an equivalence relation on
K
.

Proof.

It follows from Remark
3.21
that
t\sim t
for each
t\in K
and
t_{1}\sim t_{2}
implies that
t_{2}\sim t_{1}
. Now, suppose that
t_{1}\sim t_{2}
and
t_{2}\sim t_{3}
. Then, there exist finite subsets
\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\}
and
\{s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{m}\}
of
K
such that
t_{1}\rightleftarrows s_{1}\rightleftarrows\cdots\rightleftarrows s_{n}\rightleftarrows t_{2}
and
t_{2}\rightleftarrows s^{\prime}_{1}\rightleftarrows\cdots\rightleftarrows s^{\prime}_{m}\rightleftarrows t_{3}
. In particular,
\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n},t_{2},s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{m}\}
is a finite subset of
K
and

t_{1}\rightleftarrows s_{1}\rightleftarrows\cdots\rightleftarrows s_{n}\rightleftarrows t_{2}\rightleftarrows s^{\prime}_{1}\rightleftarrows\cdots\rightleftarrows s^{\prime}_{m}\rightleftarrows t_{3}.

Therefore,
t_{1}\sim t_{3}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Using the equivalence relation “
\sim
” on a given closure space, we obtain two natural way of constructing new closure spaces. In the case of topological spaces, the most natural candidate of topologies on a quotient set is the quotient topology. However, we will go to another way to obtain closure spaces which are more compatible with nonlinear geometry.

Definition 3.24
.

Let
(K,c)
be a closure space, let
K^{\mathfrak{G}}=K/\!\sim~=\{C(t):t\in K\}
and
K^{\mathfrak{P}}=\{c(C(t)):t\in K\}
. Define mappings
c^{\mathfrak{G}}:2^{K^{\mathfrak{G}}}\to 2^{K^{\mathfrak{G}}}
and
c^{\mathfrak{P}}:2^{K^{\mathfrak{P}}}\to 2^{K^{\mathfrak{P}}}
by

c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T)=\left\{c(C(t)):c(C(t))\subset c(\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in T\})\right\}

for each
T\subset K^{\mathfrak{P}}
, and

c^{\mathfrak{G}}(S)=\left\{C(t):c(C(t))\in c^{\mathfrak{P}}(\{c(C(s)):C(s)\in S\})\right\}

for each
S\subset K^{\mathfrak{G}}
. The ordered pairs
(K^{\mathfrak{G}},c^{\mathfrak{G}})
and
(K^{\mathfrak{P}},c^{\mathfrak{P}})
are called
\mathfrak{G}
-transform
and
\mathfrak{P}
-transform
of
(K,c)
, respectively. Define a mapping
\delta_{K}:K^{\mathfrak{G}}\to K^{\mathfrak{P}}
by
\delta_{K}(C(t))=c(C(t))
for each
t\in K
.

We present the first properties of
\mathfrak{G}
- and
\mathfrak{P}
-transforms of closure spaces.

Proposition 3.25
.

Let
(K,c)
be a closure space. Then,
(K^{\mathfrak{G}},c^{\mathfrak{G}})
and
(K^{\mathfrak{P}},c^{\mathfrak{P}})
are closure spaces. Moreover,
c^{\mathfrak{G}}(S)=\delta_{K}^{-1}(c^{\mathfrak{P}}(\delta_{K}(S)))
for each
S\subset K^{\mathfrak{G}}
.

Proof.

The identity
c^{\mathfrak{G}}(S)=\delta_{K}^{-1}(c^{\mathfrak{P}}(\delta_{K}(S)))
for each
S\subset K^{\mathfrak{G}}
follows from the definitions of
c^{\mathfrak{G}}
and
c^{\mathfrak{P}}
. Combining this with Lemma
3.19
, it is sufficient to show that
c^{\mathfrak{P}}
is a closure operator on
K^{\mathfrak{P}}
. First, we note that
\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in\emptyset\}=\emptyset
, which together with the fact that
t\in C(t)\subset c(C(t))
for each
t
implies
c^{\mathfrak{P}}(\emptyset)=\emptyset
. Moreover, if
T_{1}\subset T_{2}\subset K^{\mathfrak{P}}
, then

\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in T_{1}\}\subset\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in T_{2}\}

and
c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T_{1})\subset c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T_{2})
by the monotonicity of
c
. Now, let
T\subset K^{\mathfrak{P}}
. If
c(C(t))\in T
, and if
s\in C(t)
, then
C(s)=C(t)
and
c(C(s))=c(C(t))\in T
. This shows that
C(t)\subset\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in T\}
. Hence,
c(C(t))\subset c(\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in T\})
and
c(C(t))\in c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T)
, that is,
T\subset c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T)
, which in turn implies that

c(\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in T\})\subset c(\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T)\}).

Meanwhile, if
c(C(t))\in c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T)
, then

t\in C(t)\subset c(C(t))\subset c(\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in T\})

by the definition of
c^{\mathfrak{P}}
. Therefore,

\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T)\}\subset c(\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in T\})

and
c(\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T)\})\subset c(\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in T\})
, that is,

c(\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in T\})=c(\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T)\}).

This proves that
c^{\mathfrak{P}}(c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T))=c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T)
.
∎

Remark 3.26
.

Let
(K,c)
be a closure space. Then,
(K^{\mathfrak{G}},c^{\mathfrak{G}})
is always
T_{0}
, that is, whenever
c(C(t_{1}))\neq c(C(t_{2}))
, there exists an
F\in\mathfrak{C}(K^{\mathfrak{P}},c^{\mathfrak{P}})
such that
c(C(t_{1}))\in F
and
c(C(t_{2}))\not\in F
, or
c(C(t_{2}))\in F
and
c(C(t_{2}))\not\in F
. Indeed, if
s,t\in K
and
c(C(s))=c(C(t))
, then
s\in C(s)\subset c(C(s))=c(C(t))
, which implies that
\{s\in K:c(C(s))=c(C(t))\}\subset c(C(t))
. Hence,

c^{\mathfrak{P}}(\{c(C(t))\})\subset\{c(C(t^{\prime})):c(C(t^{\prime}))\subset c(C(t))\}

for each
t\in K
. In particular, if
t_{1},t_{2}\in K
,
c(C(t_{1}))\in c^{\mathfrak{P}}(\{c(C(t_{2}))\})
, and
c(C(t_{2}))\in c^{\mathfrak{P}}(\{c(C(t_{1}))\})
, then
c(C(t_{1}))=c(C(t_{2}))
. This shows that
(K^{\mathfrak{P}},c^{\mathfrak{P}})
is
T_{0}
.

Meanwhile,
(K^{\mathfrak{G}},c^{\mathfrak{G}})
is
T_{0}
if and only if it is homeomorphic to
(K^{\mathfrak{P}},c^{\mathfrak{P}})
, in which case,
\delta_{K}:K^{\mathfrak{G}}\to K^{\mathfrak{P}}
is a homeomorphism. Indeed, if
(K^{\mathfrak{G}},c^{\mathfrak{G}})
is
T_{0}
, and if
C(t_{1})\neq C(t_{2})
, then there exists an
F\in\mathfrak{C}(K^{\mathfrak{G}},c^{\mathfrak{G}})
such that
C(t_{1})\in F
and
C(t_{2})\not\in F
, or
C(t_{1})\not\in F
and
C(t_{2})\in F
. Replacing
C(t_{1})
with
C(t_{2})
if necessary, we may assume that
C(t_{1})\in F
and
C(t_{2})\not\in F
. Since
c^{\mathfrak{G}}(F)=F
, it follows that
c^{\mathfrak{G}}(\{C(t_{1})\})\subset F
, which implies that

\displaystyle C(t_{2})\not\in c^{\mathfrak{G}}(\{C(t_{1})\})

\displaystyle=\{C(t):c(C(t))\in c^{\mathfrak{P}}(\{c(C(t_{1}))\})\}.

Combining this with
c(C(t_{1}))\in c^{\mathfrak{P}}(\{c(C(t_{1}))\})
, we have
c(C(t_{1}))\neq c(C(t_{2}))
, that is,
\delta_{K}
is injective. Therefore,
\delta_{K}:K^{\mathfrak{G}}\to K^{\mathfrak{P}}
is a homeomorphism by Lemma
3.19
(iv) and Proposition
3.25
. The converse immediately follows from the first paragraph.

Althought the morphisms in
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Cl}_{\textrm{\rm F}}}
are the continuous mappings, in the context of
\mathfrak{G}
- and
\mathfrak{P}
-transforms of closure spaces, a weaker concept of continuity plays important roles.

Definition 3.27
.

Let
(K,c)
and
(L,d)
be closure spaces, and let
f:K\to L
. Then,
f
is said to be
finitely continuous
if
f(c(S))\subset d(f(S))
for each finite subset
S
of
K
. A finitely continuous bijection with finitely continuous inverse is called a
finite-homeomorphism
.

We naturally expect that homeomorphisms between closure spaces preserve the equivalence of elements. In fact, as the following lemma says, finite-homeomorphisms already have this property.

Lemma 3.28
.

Let
(K,c)
and
(L,d)
be closure spaces, and let
f:K\to L
be finitely continuous injection. Then,
t_{1}\rightleftarrows t_{2}
implies
f(t_{1})\rightleftarrows f(t_{2})
. Consequently,
t_{1}\sim t_{2}
implies
f(t_{1})\sim f(t_{2})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
t_{1}\rightleftarrows t_{2}
and
t_{1}\neq t_{2}
. Then,
\{t_{1},t_{2}\}\not\in\mathfrak{C}(K,c)
. Since
f
is finitely continuous, it follows that

f(c(\{t_{1},t_{2}\}))\subset d(f(\{t_{1},t_{2}\})).

If
\{f(t_{1}),f(t_{2})\}\in\mathfrak{C}(L,d)
, then
f(c(\{t_{1},t_{2}\}))\subset f(\{t_{1},t_{2}\})
, which together with the injectivity of
f
implies that

c(\{t_{1},t_{2}\})=f^{-1}(f(c(\{t_{1},t_{2}\})))\subset f^{-1}(f(\{t_{1},t_{2}\}))=\{t_{1},t_{2}\}.

However, this contradicts
\{t_{1},t_{2}\}\not\in\mathfrak{C}(K,c)
. Hence,
\{f(t_{1}),f(t_{2})\}\not\in\mathfrak{C}(L,d)
and
f(t_{1})\rightleftarrows f(t_{2})
.
∎

We conclude this section with the following result. It will turn out that the
\mathfrak{G}
- and
\mathfrak{P}
-transforms of a closure space are invariant under homeomorphisms.

Theorem 3.29
.

Let
(K,c)
and
(L,d)
be closure spaces, and let
f:K\to L
be a homeomorphism. Define mappings
f^{\mathfrak{G}}:K^{\mathfrak{G}}\to L^{\mathfrak{G}}
and
f^{\mathfrak{P}}:K^{\mathfrak{P}}\to L^{\mathfrak{P}}
by
f^{\mathfrak{G}}(C(t))=C(f(t))
and
f^{\mathfrak{P}}(c(C(t)))=d(C(f(t)))
for each
t\in K
. Then,
f^{\mathfrak{G}}
and
f^{\mathfrak{P}}
are homeomorphisms with inverses
(f^{-1})^{\mathfrak{G}}
and
(f^{-1})^{\mathfrak{P}}
. Moreover,
\delta_{L}\circ f^{\mathfrak{G}}=f^{\mathfrak{P}}\circ\delta_{K}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.28
, we have
f(C(t))=C(f(t))
for each
t\in K
. From this, the mapping
f^{\mathfrak{G}}
is well-defined and bijective. Moreover since
f(c(S))=d(f(S))
for each
S\subset K
, it follows that
f(c(C(t)))=d(f(C(t)))=d(C(f(t)))
for each
t\in K
, which implies that
f^{\mathfrak{P}}
is well-defined and bijective. By the definitions of
f^{\mathfrak{G}}
and
f^{\mathfrak{P}}
, we obtain
(f^{\mathfrak{G}})^{-1}=(f^{-1})^{\mathfrak{G}}
,
(f^{\mathfrak{P}})^{-1}=(f^{-1})^{\mathfrak{P}}
and
\delta_{L}\circ f^{\mathfrak{G}}=f^{\mathfrak{P}}\circ\delta_{K}
.

Suppose that
T\subset K^{\mathfrak{P}}
. If
c(C(t))\in c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T)
, then
c(C(t))\subset c(\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in T\})
. Since
f
is a homeomorphism, we derive that

\displaystyle d(C(f(t)))=d(f(C(t)))=f(c(C(t)))

\displaystyle\subset f(c(\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in T\}))

\displaystyle=d(f(\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in T\})).

Meanwhile, if
s\in K
and
c(C(s))\in T
, then
d(C(f(s)))=f^{\mathfrak{P}}(c(C(s)))\in f^{\mathfrak{P}}(T)
, that is,
f(s)\in\{s^{\prime}\in L:d(C(s^{\prime}))\in f^{\mathfrak{P}}(T)\}
. Hence,

\displaystyle f^{\mathfrak{P}}(c(C(t)))=d(C(f(t)))

\displaystyle\subset d(f(\{s\in K:c(C(s))\in T\}))

\displaystyle\subset d(\{s^{\prime}\in L:d(C(s^{\prime}))\in f^{\mathfrak{P}}(T)\}.

This shows that
f^{\mathfrak{P}}(c(C(t)))\in d^{\mathfrak{P}}(f^{\mathfrak{P}}(T))
whenenver
c(C(t))\in c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T)
, and that
f^{\mathfrak{P}}(c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T))\subset d^{\mathfrak{P}}(f^{\mathfrak{P}}(T))
. Therefore,
f^{\mathfrak{P}}
is continuous. Applying the same argument as above to
f^{-1}
, we can conclude that
f^{\mathfrak{P}}
is a homeomorphism.

Next, suppose that
S\subset K^{\mathfrak{G}}
. By the preceding paragraph, we have

\displaystyle f^{\mathfrak{G}}(c^{\mathfrak{G}}(S))=f^{\mathfrak{G}}(\delta_{K}^{-1}(c^{\mathfrak{P}}(\delta_{K}(S))))

\displaystyle=f^{\mathfrak{G}}(\delta_{K}^{-1}(c^{\mathfrak{P}}(((f^{\mathfrak{P}})^{-1}\circ f^{\mathfrak{P}}\circ\delta_{K})(S))))

\displaystyle=f^{\mathfrak{G}}(\delta_{K}^{-1}(c^{\mathfrak{P}}(((f^{\mathfrak{P}})^{-1}\circ\delta_{L}\circ f^{\mathfrak{G}})(S))))

\displaystyle=f^{\mathfrak{G}}(\delta_{K}^{-1}((f^{\mathfrak{P}})^{-1}(d^{\mathfrak{P}}((\delta_{L}\circ f^{\mathfrak{G}})(S)))))

\displaystyle=f^{\mathfrak{G}}((f^{\mathfrak{P}}\circ\delta_{K})^{-1}(d^{\mathfrak{P}}((\delta_{L}\circ f^{\mathfrak{G}})(S))))

\displaystyle=f^{\mathfrak{G}}((\delta_{L}\circ f^{\mathfrak{G}})^{-1}(d^{\mathfrak{P}}((\delta_{L}\circ f^{\mathfrak{G}})(S))))

\displaystyle=f^{\mathfrak{G}}((f^{\mathfrak{G}})^{-1}(\delta_{L}^{-1}(d^{\mathfrak{P}}((\delta_{L}\circ f^{\mathfrak{G}})(S)))))

\displaystyle=\delta_{L}^{-1}(d^{\mathfrak{P}}(\delta_{L}(f^{\mathfrak{G}}(S))))

\displaystyle=d^{\mathfrak{G}}(f^{\mathfrak{G}}(S)).

Thus,
f^{\mathfrak{G}}
is also a homeomorphism.
∎

4.
Geometric spectra and geometric primitive spectra

In this section, we focus on special closure spaces that are naturally induced by facial structure of unit balls of Banach spaces.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
X
be a (real or complex) nontrivial Banach space, and let
I_{F}=\bigcup\{\ker f:f\in\Phi^{*}(F)\}
for each maximal face
F
of
B_{X}
, where

\Phi^{*}(F)=\{f\in B_{X^{*}}:f(F)=\{1\}\}.

Then, the set

\mathfrak{S}(X)=\{I_{F}:\text{$F$ is a maximal face of $B_{X}$}\}

equipped with the mapping
S\mapsto S^{=}
given by

S^{=}=\left\{I\in\mathfrak{S}(X):\bigcap\{I^{\prime}:I^{\prime}\in S\}\subset I\right\}

for each
S\subset\mathfrak{S}(X)
is called the
geometric structure space
of
X
. Let
\mathfrak{C}(X)=\{S\subset\mathfrak{S}(X):S^{=}=S\}
.

See
[
42
, Definition 3.4]
for the original definition of
\mathfrak{S}(X)
, and
[
42
, Theorem 4.15]
for the equivalence of two definitions of geometric structure spaces. It was shown in
[
42
, Proposition 3.5]
that
\mathfrak{S}(X)
is a closure space whenever
X
is a Banach space. Although the scalar field should be specified in categories whose objects are Banach spaces, in the context of closure spaces, it is not necessary to distinguish the real and complex cases. More precisely, it is appropriate to categorize the scalar-specified versions as a subcategory of (homeomorphs of) geometric structure spaces of
all
(real or complex) Banach spaces.

Definition 4.2
.

The full subcategory of
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Cl}_{\textrm{\rm F}}}
whose objects are homeomorphs of geometric structure spaces of nontrivial Banach spaces is denoted by
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Gss}}
. The full subcategory of
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Gss}}
whose objects are homeomorphs of geometric structure spaces of nontrivial Banach spaces over
\mathbb{K}
is denoted by
\mathbb{K}
-
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Gss}}
.

Remark 4.3
.

From the definition,
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Gss}}
is a strictly full subcategory of
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Cl}_{\textrm{\rm F}}}
, and
\mathbb{K}
-
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Gss}}
is a strictly full subcategory of
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Gss}}
.

Remark 4.4
.

It was shown in
[
42
, Theorem 4.11]
that the geometric structure space
\mathfrak{S}(X)
of a Banach space
X
is always
T_{1}
. Namely, each singleton in
\mathfrak{S}(X)
is closed. Hence, in particular, not
T_{1}
topological spaces cannot be objects in
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Gss}}
.

The main purpose of this section is to obtain more visible forms of
\mathfrak{G}
- and
\mathfrak{P}
-transforms of geometric structure spaces of Banach spaces. As in the preceding section, the symbol “
\sim
” stands for the equivalence relation on a closure space defined in Definition
3.20
.

Before developing the theory, we give an example which explains why “
\sim
” was adopted nevertheless “
\rightleftarrows
” is much simpler.

Example 4.5
.

There exists a Banach space
X
such that “
\rightleftarrows
” is not transitive on
\mathfrak{S}(X)
. Let
X
be the space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
endowed with the norm
\|\cdot\|
defined by

\|(a,b,c)\|=\|(\|(a,b)\|_{\infty},c)\|_{2}=(\max\{|a|^{2},|b|^{2}\}+|c|^{2})^{1/2}

for each
(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Then, the dual norm of
\|\cdot\|
is given by

\|(a,b,c)\|^{*}=\|(\|(a,b)\|_{1},c)\|_{2}=((|a|+|b|)^{2}+|c|^{2})^{1/2}

for each
(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. To be precise,
X^{*}
is isometrically isomorphic to
(\mathbb{R}^{3},\|\cdot\|^{*})
under the isometric isomorphism

X^{*}\ni f\mapsto(f(1,0,0),f(0,1,0),f(0,0,1))\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

Let
F_{1}=\{(a,b,c)\in B_{X}:a=1\}
and
F_{2}=\{(a,b,c)\in B_{X}:b=1\}
. Then,
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are exposed faces of
B_{X}
with respect to the norm-one functionals
f_{1}=(1,0,0)
and
f_{2}=(0,1,0)
. Moreover, if
f=(a,b,c)\in\Phi^{*}(F_{1})
, then it follows from
(1,0,0)\in F_{1}
that
a=f_{1}(1,0,0)=1
. Combining this with
\|(a,b,c)\|^{*}=1
, we obtain
f=(1,0,0)
. Hence,
\Phi^{*}(F_{1})=\{f_{1}\}
. From this, in particular,
F_{1}
is a maximal face of
B_{X}
. Indeed, if
F
is a maximal face of
B_{X}
containing
F_{1}
, then
\Phi^{*}(F)\subset\Phi^{*}(F_{1})=\{f_{1}\}
, which together with the maximality of
F
implies that
F=f_{1}^{-1}(1)\cap B_{X}=F_{1}
. Similarly, it turns out that
\Phi^{*}(F_{2})=\{f_{2}\}
and
F_{2}
is a maximal faces of
B_{X}
.

Next, let
F_{3}=\{(a,b,c)\in B_{X}:a=c=1/\sqrt{2}\}
,
F_{4}=\{(a,b,c)\in B_{X}:b=c=1/\sqrt{2}\}
,
f_{3}=(1/\sqrt{2},0,1/\sqrt{2})
, and
f_{4}=(0,1/\sqrt{2},1/\sqrt{2})
. Then,
\|f_{3}\|=\|f_{4}\|=1
. Moreover, by the equality condition for the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have
F_{j}=f_{j}^{-1}(\{1\})\cap B_{X}
for
j=3,4
. If
f=(a,b,c)\in\Phi^{*}(F_{3})
, then
(1/\sqrt{2},0,1/\sqrt{2})\in F_{3}
implies that
a/\sqrt{2}+c/\sqrt{2}=1
. Since
(|a|^{2}+|c|^{2})^{1/2}\leq\|(a,b,c)\|^{*}=1
, again by the equality condition for the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have
a=c=1/\sqrt{2}
and
b=0
. Therefore,
f=f_{3}
, that is,
\Phi^{*}(F_{3})=\{f_{3}\}
. An argument similar to above also shows that
\Phi^{*}(F_{4})=\{f_{4}\}
. In particular,
F_{3}
and
F_{4}
are maximal faces of
B_{X}
.

We note that
e=(0,0,1)\in S_{X}
is an extreme point of
B_{X}
which is also a point smoothness. Indeed, if
f=(a,b,c)\in S_{X^{*}}
, and if
f(0,0,1)=1
, then
c=1
and
a=b=0
, that is
f=e
. Meanwhile, if
(a,b,c)\in B_{X}
satisfies
e(a,b,c)=1
, then
c=1
and
a=b=0
, that is,
(a,b,c)=e
. Hence,
\{e\}=e^{-1}(\{1\})\cap B_{X}
. This means that
\{e\}
is a one-point face (that is, an extreme point) of
B_{X}
. In particular,
\{e\}
is a maximal face of
B_{X}
with
\Phi^{*}(\{e\})=\{e\}
.

From the above, it follows that
\ker f_{j},\ker e\in\mathfrak{S}(X)
for
j=1,2,3,4
. Now, we note that
\ker f_{1}\cap\ker e\subset\ker f_{3}
and
\ker f_{2}\cap\ker e\subset\ker f_{4}
. Hence,

\ker f_{3}\in\{\ker f_{1},\ker e\}^{=}\setminus\{\ker f_{1},\ker e\}

and

\ker f_{4}\in\{\ker f_{2},\ker e\}^{=}\setminus\{\ker f_{2},\ker e\},

that is,
\{\ker f_{1},\ker e\}\not\in\mathfrak{C}(X)
and
\{\ker f_{2},\ker e\}\not\in\mathfrak{C}(X)
. Thus,
\ker f_{1}\rightleftarrows\ker e
and
\ker e\rightleftarrows\ker f_{2}
. Meanwhile, if
F
is a maximal face of
B_{X}
that contains
x=(a,b,c)\in S_{X}
with
c\neq 0
, then
I_{F}\not\in\{\ker f_{1},\ker f_{2}\}^{=}
. Indeed, such an
x
satisfies
\max\{|a|^{2},|b|^{2}\}=1-|c|^{2}<1
, which implies that
c^{\prime}\neq 0
whenever
f=(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime})\in\Phi^{*}(F)
; for otherwise,
|f(x)|=|aa^{\prime}+bb^{\prime}|\leq\max\{|a|,|b|\}(|a^{\prime}|+|b^{\prime}|)<1
by
f\in S_{X^{*}}
. Therefore,

(0,0,1)\in(\ker f_{1}\cap\ker f_{2})\setminus\bigcup\{\ker f:f\in\Phi^{*}(F)\},

which proves that
I_{F}\not\in\{\ker f_{1},\ker f_{2}\}^{=}
. Now, let
I_{F}\in\{\ker f_{1},\ker f_{2}\}^{=}
, where
F
is a maximal face of
B_{X}
. Then,
F
is contained in the subspace
M=\{(a,b,c)\in X:c=0\}
which is naturally isometrically isomorphic to
\ell_{\infty}^{2}
. Since
F(\subset M)
is also a maximal face of
B_{M}
and
S_{M}=F_{1}\cup(-F_{1})\cup F_{2}\cup(-F_{2})
, it follows that
F\in\{F_{1},-F_{1},F_{2},-F_{2}\}
. Hence,
I_{F}=\ker f_{1}
if
F\in\{F_{1},-F_{1}\}
, while
I_{F}=\ker f_{2}
if
F\in\{F_{2},-F_{2}\}
. This proves that
\{\ker f_{1},\ker f_{2}\}\in\mathfrak{C}(X)
, that is,
\ker f_{1}\not\rightleftarrows\ker f_{2}
.

Now, we proceed to develop the theory. First, we give two ways of constructing new closure spaces from the geometric structure space of a given Banach space.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
X
be a nontrivial Banach space, and let

J_{C(I)}=\bigcap\{I^{\prime}:I^{\prime}\in C(I)\}

for each
I\in\mathfrak{S}(X)
. The
geometric spectrum

\mathfrak{GS}(X)
and
geometric primitive spectrum

\mathfrak{PS}(X)
of
X
are defined as the sets
\mathfrak{GS}(X)=\{C(I):I\in\mathfrak{S}(X)\}
and

\mathfrak{PS}(X)=\{J_{C(I)}:I\in\mathfrak{S}(X)\}.

endowed with the mappings
S\mapsto S^{\equiv}
on
2^{\mathfrak{GS}(X)}
and
S\mapsto S^{\cong}
on
2^{\mathfrak{PS}(X)}
given by

\displaystyle S^{\equiv}

\displaystyle=\left\{C(I)\in\mathfrak{GS}(X):\bigcap\{J_{C(I^{\prime})}:C(I^{\prime})\in S\}\subset J_{C(I)}\right\}

\displaystyle T^{\cong}

\displaystyle=\left\{J\in\mathfrak{PS}(X):\bigcap\{J^{\prime}:J^{\prime}\in T\}\subset J\right\}

for each
S\subset\mathfrak{GS}(X)
and each
T\subset\mathfrak{PS}(X)
. Let
\mathfrak{GC}(X)=\{S\subset\mathfrak{GS}(X):S^{\equiv}=S\}
and
\mathfrak{PC}(X)=\{T\subset\mathfrak{PS}(X):T^{\cong}=T\}
. Define a mapping
\gamma_{X}:\mathfrak{GS}(X)\to\mathfrak{PS}(X)
by
\gamma_{X}(C(I))=J_{C(I)}
for each
C(I)\in\mathfrak{GS}(X)
.

It will turn out that geometric spectra and geometric primitive spectra of Banach spaces are closure spaces.

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
X
be a nontrivial Banach space. Then,
\mathfrak{GS}(X)
and
\mathfrak{PS}(X)
are closure spaces. Moreover,
S^{\equiv}=\gamma_{X}^{-1}(\gamma_{X}(S)^{\cong})
for each
S\subset\mathfrak{GS}(X)
.

Proof.

Let
S\subset\mathfrak{GS}(X)
and
C(I)\in\mathfrak{GS}(X)
. Then, we have

\bigcap\{J^{\prime}:J^{\prime}\in\gamma_{X}(S)\}=\bigcap\{J_{C(I^{\prime})}:C(I^{\prime})\in S\}\subset J_{C(I)}=\gamma_{X}(C(I))

if and only if
\gamma_{X}(C(I))\in\gamma_{X}(S)^{\cong}
, which implies that

S^{\equiv}=\{C(I)\in\mathfrak{GS}(X):\gamma_{X}(C(I))\in\gamma_{X}(S)^{\cong}\}=\gamma_{X}^{-1}(\gamma_{X}(S)^{\cong}).

Hence, in the light of Lemma
3.19
, it is sufficient to show that
\mathfrak{PS}(X)
is a closure space. First, we note that
\bigcap\{J:J\in\emptyset\}=X
, and
J_{C(I)}\subset I\neq X
for each
I\in\mathfrak{S}(X)
since
I\cap F=\emptyset
provided that
I=I_{F}
for some maximal face
F
of
B_{X}
. Therefore,
\emptyset^{\cong}=\emptyset
. If
J\in T
, then
\bigcap\{J^{\prime}:J^{\prime}\in T\}\subset J
, which implies that
J\in T^{\cong}
. This shows that
T\subset T^{\cong}
. Since
\bigcap\{J^{\prime}:J^{\prime}\in T\}\subset J
whenever
J\in T^{\cong}
, it follows that
\bigcap\{J^{\prime}:J^{\prime}\in T\}\subset\bigcap\{J^{\prime}:J^{\prime}\in T^{\cong}\}
. Combining this with (ii), we obtain
\bigcap\{J^{\prime}:J^{\prime}\in T\}=\bigcap\{J^{\prime}:J^{\prime}\in T^{\cong}\}
, that is,
(T^{\cong})^{\cong}=T^{\cong}
. Finally, if
T_{1}\subset T_{2}\subset\mathfrak{PS}(X)
, then
\bigcap\{J^{\prime}:J^{\prime}\in T_{2}\}\subset\bigcap\{J^{\prime}:J^{\prime}\in T_{1}\}
. Therefore,
T_{1}^{\cong}\subset T_{2}^{\cong}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

Let
X
be a Banach space such that “
\sim
” and “
=
” are equivalent on
\mathfrak{S}(X)
. Then,
C(I)=\{I\}
and
J_{C(I)}=I
for each
I\in\mathfrak{S}(X)
. Hence,
\mathfrak{S}(X)
coincides with
\mathfrak{PS}(X)
, and
\gamma_{X}:\mathfrak{GS}(X)\to\mathfrak{PS}(X)
is bijective. In particular,
\mathfrak{GS}(X)
is homeomorphic to
\mathfrak{S}(X)
by Lemma
3.19
(iv).

We give some examples of geometric spectra and geometric primitive spectra of Banach spaces.

Example 4.9
.

Let
X
be a Banach space. Suppose that
\mathfrak{S}(X)
is topologizable. Then, each finite subset of
\mathfrak{S}(X)
is closed, which together with Remark
3.22
implies that “
\sim
” and “
=
” are equivalent on
\mathfrak{S}(X)
. Therefore,
\mathfrak{S}(X),\mathfrak{GS}(X)
and
\mathfrak{PS}(X)
are homeomorphic to each other by Remark
4.8
.

Example 4.10
.

Let
X
be a reflexive smooth Banach space with
\dim X\geq 2
. As in the proof of
[
42
, Theorem 3.8]
, each two-point set in
\mathfrak{S}(X)
is not closed. Hence, we have
I_{1}\sim I_{2}
for any choice of
I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathfrak{S}(X)
, that is,
\mathfrak{GS}(X)=\{\mathfrak{S}(X)\}
. Moreover since
J_{\mathfrak{S}(X)}=\bigcap\{I:I\in\mathfrak{S}(X)\}=\{0\}
by
[
44
, Lemma 4.1]
, we obtain
\mathfrak{PS}(X)=\{\{0\}\}
. Consequently,
\mathfrak{GS}(X)
and
\mathfrak{PS}(X)
are topologizable, and they are both homeomorphic to a one-point topological space endowed with the trivial topology.

Example 4.11
.

The geometric spectrum and geometric primitive spectrum of
\ell_{1}
depend on the scalar field
\mathbb{K}
. Recall that
\mathfrak{S}(\ell_{1})=\{\ker f_{u}:u\in\mathcal{U}\}
by
[
42
, Theorem 5.8]
, where

\mathcal{U}=\{(u_{n})_{n}\in\ell_{\infty}:\text{$|u_{n}|=1$ for each $n\in\mathbb{N}$}\}

and
f_{u}\in\ell_{1}^{*}
is defined by
f_{u}((a_{n})_{n})=\sum_{n}a_{n}u_{n}
for each
u=(u_{n})_{n}\in\mathcal{U}
and each
(a_{n})_{n}\in\ell_{1}
.

(I) Let
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
. Suppose that
u=(u_{n})_{n}
and
v=(v_{n})_{n}
are elements of
\mathcal{U}
such that
\ker f_{u}\neq\ker f_{v}
. Replacing
u
(or
v
) with
-u
(or
-v
) if necessary, we may assume that
u_{1}=v_{1}=1
. Since
\ker f_{u}\neq\ker f_{v}
, there exists an
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
u_{n}\neq v_{n}
. Let
\ker f_{w}\in\{\ker f_{u},\ker f_{v}\}^{=}
. It follows from
\ker f_{u}\cap\ker f_{v}\subset\ker f_{w}
that
f_{w}=\alpha f_{u}+\beta f_{v}
for some
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, in which case,
w=\alpha u+\beta v
. In particular, we have
|\alpha+\beta|=|w_{1}|=1
and
|\alpha-\beta|=|w_{n}|=1
. Since
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, it turns out that
\alpha\beta=0
. Therefore,
f_{w}\in\{f_{u},-f_{u},f_{v},-f_{v}\}
, that is,
\ker f_{w}\in\{\ker f_{u},\ker f_{v}\}
. This shows that
\{\ker f_{u},\ker f_{v}\}\in\mathfrak{C}(\ell_{1})
, which together with Remark
3.22
implies that “
\sim
” and “
=
” are equivalent on
\mathfrak{S}(\ell_{1})
. Thus, in the real case,
\mathfrak{S}(\ell_{1})
coincides with
\mathfrak{PS}(\ell_{1})
, and
\mathfrak{GS}(\ell_{1})
is homeomorphic to
\mathfrak{S}(\ell_{1})
. It is worth mentioning here that
\mathfrak{S}(\ell_{1})
is not topologizable; see
[
42
, Theorem 5.8]
.

(II) Let
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{C}
. Suppose that
u=(u_{n})_{n}
and
v=(v_{n})_{n}
are elements of
\mathcal{U}
. We claim that
\ker f_{u}\rightleftarrows\ker f_{v}
if and only if the set
\{u_{n}\overline{v_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
contains at most two elements. To show this, suppose that
\{u_{n}\overline{v_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
contains at most two elements. We note that if
\{u_{n}\overline{v_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is a singleton, then
v
is a scalar multiple of
u
, in which case,
\ker u=\ker v
. Next, let
\{u_{n}\overline{v_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}=\{e^{i\theta_{1}},e^{i\theta_{2}}\}
. Replacing
u
with
e^{i\theta_{1}}u
, we may assume that
\{u_{n}\overline{v_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}=\{1,e^{i\theta}\}
and
1\neq e^{i\theta}
. Set
N_{1}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:u_{n}=v_{n}\}
and
N_{2}=\mathbb{N}\setminus N_{1}
. If
(a_{n})_{n}\in\ker f_{u}\cap\ker f_{v}
, then
0=f_{u}((a_{n})_{n})=\sum_{n}a_{n}u_{n}
and

0=f_{v}((a_{n})_{n})=\sum_{n}a_{n}v_{n}=\sum_{n\in N_{1}}a_{n}u_{n}+e^{-i\theta}\sum_{n\in N_{2}}a_{n}u_{n},

which implies that
\sum_{n\in N_{1}}a_{n}u_{n}=\sum_{n\in N_{2}}a_{n}u_{n}=\sum_{n\in N_{2}}a_{n}v_{n}=0
. Now, let

w_{n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}u_{n}&(n\in N_{1})\\
e^{i\theta/2}v_{n}&(n\in N_{2})\end{array}\right..

Then,
w=(w_{n})_{n}\in\mathcal{U}
and

\ker f_{w}\in\{\ker f_{u},\ker f_{v}\}^{=}\setminus\{\ker f_{u},\ker f_{v}\}.

Hence,
\{\ker f_{u},\ker f_{v}\}\not\in\mathfrak{C}(\ell_{1})
and
\ker f_{u}\rightleftarrows\ker f_{v}
.

Conversely, suppose that
\{u_{n}\overline{v_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
contains three elements. In this case, we have
\ker f_{u}\neq\ker f_{v}
; for otherwise,
\{u,v\}
is linearly dependent, and
\{u_{n}\overline{v_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
becomes a singleton. As in the preceding paragraph, it may be assumed that
\{u_{n}\overline{v_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}=\{1,e^{i\theta_{1}},e^{i\theta_{2}}\}
with
0<\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}<2\pi
. Let
w\in\mathcal{U}
be such that
\ker f_{w}\in\{\ker f_{u},\ker f_{v}\}^{=}
. Then,
w=\alpha u+\beta v
for some
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
. Choose
n,n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
u_{n}=v_{n}
, and
u_{n_{j}}=e^{i\theta_{j}}v_{n_{j}}
for
j=1,2
. It follows that
|\alpha+\beta|=|w_{n}|=1
and
|e^{i\theta_{j}}\alpha+\beta|=|w_{n_{j}}|=1
for
j=1,2
, which implies that
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\nolimits\alpha\overline{\beta}=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\nolimits e^{i\theta_{1}}\alpha\overline{\beta}=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\nolimits e^{i\theta_{2}}\alpha\overline{\beta}
. If
\alpha\overline{\beta}=re^{i\theta}\neq 0
, then we obtain
\theta<\theta+\theta_{1}<\theta+\theta_{2}<\theta+2\pi
. Meanwhile,
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\nolimits e^{i\varphi_{1}}=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\nolimits e^{i\varphi_{2}}
implies that
e^{i\varphi_{1}}=\overline{e^{i\varphi_{2}}}
. Therefore,
\alpha\overline{\beta}=0
. This proves that
\{\ker f_{u},\ker f_{v}\}\in\mathfrak{C}(X)
. Combining this with
\ker f_{u}\neq\ker f_{v}
, we derive that
\ker f_{u}\not\rightleftarrows\ker f_{v}
.

Next, we claim that
\ker f_{u}\sim\ker f_{v}
if and only if
\{u_{n}\overline{v_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is a finite set. Indeed, if
\ker f_{u}\sim\ker f_{v}
, then there exists a finite subset
\{w^{(1)},\ldots,w^{(m)}\}\subset\mathcal{U}
such that
\ker f_{u}\rightleftarrows\ker f_{w^{(1)}}\rightleftarrows\cdots\rightleftarrows\ker f_{w^{(m)}}\rightleftarrows\ker v
. By what we have proved in above, it follows that each of

\displaystyle\left\{u_{n}\overline{w^{(1)}_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right\},

\displaystyle\left\{w^{(1)}_{n}\overline{w^{(2)}_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right\},\ldots,\left\{w^{(m-1)}_{n}\overline{w^{(m)}_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right\},

\displaystyle\left\{w^{(m)}_{n}\overline{v_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}

contains at most two elements. Moreover, we have

u_{n}\overline{v_{n}}=\left(u_{n}\overline{w^{(1)}_{n}}\right)\left(w^{(1)}_{n}\overline{w^{(2)}_{n}}\right)\cdots\left(w^{(m-1)}_{n}\overline{w^{(m)}_{n}}\right)\left(w^{(m)}_{n}\overline{v_{n}}\right)

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus,
\{u_{n}\overline{v_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
contains at most
2^{m+1}
elements.

For the converse, let
\{u_{n}\overline{v_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}=\{e^{i\theta_{1}},\ldots,e^{i\theta_{m}}\}
with
0\leq\theta_{1}<\cdots<\theta_{m}<2\pi
, and let
N_{j}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:u_{n}\overline{v_{n}}=e^{i\theta_{j}}\}
. Set

w^{(j)}_{n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}u_{n}&(n\not\in N_{1}\cup\cdots\cup N_{j})\\
v_{n}&(n\in N_{1}\cup\cdots\cup N_{j})\end{array}\right.

for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
and each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
N_{1}\cup\cdots\cup N_{m}=\mathbb{N}
, we have
w^{(m)}=v
. Moreover, it follows that

\left\{u_{n}\overline{w^{(1)}_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}=\{1,e^{i\theta_{1}}\}

and

\left\{w^{(j)}_{n}\overline{w^{(j+1)}_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}=\{1,e^{i\theta_{j+1}}\}

for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\}
. This means that

\ker f_{u}\rightleftarrows\ker f_{w^{(1)}}\rightleftarrows\cdots\rightleftarrows\ker f_{w^{(m)}}=\ker v,

that is,
\ker f_{u}\sim\ker f_{v}
.

Now, let
u\in\mathcal{U}
. Then, we know that

C(\ker f_{u})=\{\ker f_{v}:\text{$v\in\mathcal{U}$ and $\{u_{n}\overline{v_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}$ is a finite set}\}.

Suppose that
(a_{n})_{n}\in J_{C(\ker f_{u})}
. For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let

v^{(m)}_{n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}u_{n}&(n\neq m)\\
-u_{n}&(n=m)\end{array}\right..

Since

\left\{u_{n}\overline{v^{(m)}_{n}}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}=\{-1,1\},

it follows that
\ker u\sim\ker v^{(m)}
. From this, we obtain
0=f_{u}((a_{n})_{n})=\sum_{n}a_{n}u_{n}
and

0=f_{v^{(m)}}((a_{n})_{n})=\sum_{n}a_{n}v_{n}=-a_{m}u_{m}+\sum_{n\neq m}a_{n}u_{n},

which together with
|u_{m}|=1
implies that
a_{m}=0
. Thus,
(a_{n})_{n}=0
. This shows that
J_{C(\ker u)}=\{0\}
for each
\ker u\in\mathcal{U}
, that is,
\mathfrak{PS}(\ell_{1})=\{\{0\}\}
and
\mathfrak{GC}(\ell_{1})=\{\emptyset,\mathfrak{GS}(\ell_{1})\}
in the complex case. In particular, if
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{C}
, then
\mathfrak{PS}(\ell_{1})
and
\mathfrak{GS}(\ell_{1})
are both topologizable and have the trivial topology.

Remark 4.12
.

The complex
\ell_{1}
-space gives another example of a (complex) Banach space
X
such that “
\rightleftarrows
” is not transitive on
\mathfrak{S}(X)
.

Next, we clarify the positions of geometric (primitive) spectra of Banach spaces as closure spaces. For this purpose, three auxiliary results will be needed.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
X
be a Banach space, and let
I\in\mathfrak{S}(X)
. Then,

C(I)^{=}=\{I^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}(X):J_{C(I)}\subset I^{\prime}\}

and
J_{C(I)}=\bigcap\{I^{\prime}:I^{\prime}\in C(I)^{=}\}
. In particular,
C(I_{1})^{=}=C(I_{2})^{=}
if and only if
J_{C(I_{1})}=J_{C(I_{2})}
for
I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathfrak{S}(X)
.

Proof.

From the definitions of
C(I)^{=}
and
J_{C(I)}
, we have

C(I)^{=}=\{I^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}(X):J_{C(I)}\subset I^{\prime}\},

which implies that
J_{C(I)}\subset\bigcap\{I^{\prime}:I^{\prime}\in C(I)^{=}\}
. Meanwhile, since
C(I)\subset C(I)^{=}
, it follows that

J_{C(I)}=\bigcap\{I^{\prime}:I^{\prime}\in C(I)\}\supset\bigcap\{I^{\prime}:I^{\prime}\in C(I)^{=}\}.

Therefore,
J_{C(I)}=\bigcap\{I^{\prime}:I^{\prime}\in C(I)^{=}\}
.
∎

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
X
be a Banach space. Then,

\bigcap\{J:J\in T\}=\bigcap\{I\in\mathfrak{S}(X):J_{C(I)}\in T\}

for each
T\subset\mathfrak{PS}(X)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
x\in\bigcap\{J:J\in T\}
, and that
J_{C(I)}\in T
. Then,
x\in J_{C(I)}\subset I
, which shows that

\bigcap\{J:J\in T\}\subset\bigcap\{I\in\mathfrak{S}(X):J_{C(I)}\in T\}.

Conversely, suppose that
x\in\bigcap\{I\in\mathfrak{S}(X):J_{C(I)}\in T\}
. If
J\in T
, then
J=J_{C(I)}
for some
I\in\mathfrak{S}(X)
, in which case,
J_{C(I^{\prime})}=J_{C(I)}\in T
whenever
I^{\prime}\in C(I)
. Hence,
x\in\bigcap\{I^{\prime}:I^{\prime}\in C(I)\}=J_{C(I)}=J
, which implies that
x\in\bigcap\{J:J\in T\}
. Therefore,

\bigcap\{J:J\in T\}\supset\bigcap\{I\in\mathfrak{S}(X):J_{C(I)}\in T\}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
X
be a Banach space, let
I\in\mathfrak{S}(X)
, and let
T\subset\mathfrak{PS}(X)
. Then,

J_{C(I)}\in T^{\cong}
if and only if
C(I)^{=}\subset\{I^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}(X):J_{C(I^{\prime})}\in T\}^{=}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
J_{C(I)}\in T^{\cong}
. If
I_{0}\in C(I)
. then we have

\bigcap\{I^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}(X):J_{C(I^{\prime})}\in T\}=\bigcap\{J:J\in T\}\subset J_{C(I)}\subset I_{0}

by Lemma
4.14
, that is,
I_{0}\in\{I^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}(X):J_{C(I^{\prime})}\in T\}^{=}
. Hence,

C(I)^{=}\subset\{I^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}(X):J_{C(I^{\prime})}\in T\}^{=}.

For the converse, we assume that
C(I)^{=}\subset\{I^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}(X):J_{C(I^{\prime})}\in T\}^{=}
. If
I_{0}\in C(I)
, then
I_{0}\in\{I^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}(X):J_{C(I^{\prime})}\in T\}^{=}
, which together with Lemma
4.14
implies that

\bigcap\{J:J\in T\}=\bigcap\{I^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}(X):J_{C(I^{\prime})}\in T\}\subset I_{0}.

Thus,

\bigcap\{J:J\in T\}\subset\bigcap\{I_{0}:I_{0}\in C(I)\}=J_{C(I)},

that is,
J_{C(I)}\in T^{\cong}
.
∎

The following is the main result in this section.

Theorem 4.16
.

Let
X
be a Banach space. Then,
\mathfrak{GS}(X)
coincides with the
\mathfrak{G}
-transform of
\mathfrak{S}(X)
and the mapping
C(I)^{=}\mapsto J_{C(I)}
is a homeomorphism from the
\mathfrak{P}
-transform of
\mathfrak{S}(X)
onto
\mathfrak{PS}(X)
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathfrak{S}(X)^{\mathfrak{G}},c^{\mathfrak{G}})
and
(\mathfrak{S}(X)^{\mathfrak{P}},c^{\mathfrak{P}})
be the
\mathfrak{G}
- and
\mathfrak{P}
-transforms of
\mathfrak{S}(X)
, respectively, and let
\delta(C(I))=C(I)^{=}
for each
I\in\mathfrak{S}(X)
. Then,
\mathfrak{S}(X)^{\mathfrak{G}}=\{C(I):I\in\mathfrak{S}(X)\}=\mathfrak{GS}(X)
,
\mathfrak{S}(X)^{\mathfrak{P}}=\{C(I)^{=}:I\in\mathfrak{S}(X)\}
,

c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T)=\{C(I)^{=}:C(I)^{=}\subset\{I^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}(X):C(I^{\prime})^{=}\in T\}^{=}\}

for each
T\subset\mathfrak{S}(X)^{\mathfrak{P}}
, and
c^{\mathfrak{G}}(S)=\delta^{-1}(c^{\mathfrak{P}}(\delta(S)))
for each
S\subset\mathfrak{S}(X)^{\mathfrak{G}}
. Define a mapping
\Phi:\mathfrak{S}(X)^{\mathfrak{P}}\to\mathfrak{PS}(X)
by
\Phi(C(I)^{=})=J_{C(I)}
for each
I\in\mathfrak{S}(X)
. Then,
\Phi
is well-defined and bijective by Lemma
4.13
.

Let
T\subset\mathfrak{S}(X)^{\mathfrak{P}}
and
I\in\mathfrak{S}(X)
. Then,
C(I)^{=}\in c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T)
if and only if

C(I)^{=}\subset\{I^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}(X):C(I^{\prime})^{=}\in T\}^{=}=\{I^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}(X):J_{C(I^{\prime})}\in\Phi(T)\}^{=},

which occurs if and only if
\Phi(C(I)^{=})=J_{C(I)}\in\Phi(T)^{\cong}
by Lemma
4.15
. Therefore,
\Phi(c^{\mathfrak{P}}(T))=\Phi(T)^{\cong}
. This shows that
\Phi
is a homeomorphism.

Finally, let
S\subset\mathfrak{S}(X)^{\mathfrak{G}}=\mathfrak{GS}(X)
. Since
\Phi\circ\delta=\gamma_{X}
, it follows from the preceding paragraph that

\displaystyle S^{\equiv}=\gamma_{X}^{-1}(\gamma_{X}(S)^{\cong})

\displaystyle=(\Phi\circ\delta)^{-1}((\Phi\circ\delta)(S)^{\cong})

\displaystyle=(\Phi\circ\delta)^{-1}(\Phi(c^{\mathfrak{P}}(\delta(S))))

\displaystyle=\delta^{-1}(\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(c^{\mathfrak{P}}(\delta(S)))))

\displaystyle=\delta^{-1}(c^{\mathfrak{P}}(\delta(S)))

\displaystyle=c^{\mathfrak{G}}(S).

Thus, the closure operators on
\mathfrak{GS}(X)
and
\mathfrak{S}(X)^{\mathfrak{G}}
coincide.
∎

Combining the preceding theorem with Theorem
3.29
, we immediately have the following result.

Corollary 4.17
.

Let
X
and
Y
be Banach spaces, and let
\Phi:\mathfrak{S}(X)\to\mathfrak{S}(Y)
be a homeomorphism. Define
\Phi^{\mathfrak{GS}}(C(I))=C(\Phi(I))
and
\Phi^{\mathfrak{PS}}(J_{C(I)})=J_{C(\Phi(I))}
for each
I\in\mathfrak{S}(X)
. Then,
\Phi^{\mathfrak{GS}}:\mathfrak{GS}(X)\to\mathfrak{GS}(Y)
and
\Phi^{\mathfrak{PS}}:\mathfrak{PS}(X)\to\mathfrak{PS}(Y)
are homeomorphisms. Moreover,
\gamma_{Y}\circ\Phi^{\mathfrak{GS}}=\Phi^{\mathfrak{PS}}\circ\gamma_{X}
on
\mathfrak{GS}(X)
. In particular,
\gamma_{X}:\mathfrak{GS}(X)\to\mathfrak{PS}(X)
is injective if and only if
\gamma_{Y}:\mathfrak{GS}(Y)\to\mathfrak{PS}(Y)
is.

Remark 4.18
.

Let
X
and
Y
be Banach spaces. Then, by the preceding theorem,
\mathfrak{S}(X)\simeq\mathfrak{S}(Y)
implies that
\mathfrak{GS}(X)\simeq\mathfrak{GS}(Y)
and
\mathfrak{PS}(X)\simeq\mathfrak{PS}(Y)
. We remark that the converse to this is false in general since all reflexive smooth Banach spaces
X
with
\dim X\geq 2
have the same geometric (primitive) spectra. In particular, it should be emphasized that
\mathfrak{GS}(X)
and
\mathfrak{PS}(X)
no longer remember the dimension of
X
in general.

We conclude this section with a useful tool for obtaining homeomorphisms between geometric structure spaces from isometric isomorphisms between underlying Banach spaces. Recall that an element
x
of a Banach space over
\mathbb{K}
is said to be
Birkhoff-James orthogonal
to another
y
, denoted by
x\perp_{BJ}y
, if
\|x+\lambda y\|\geq\|x\|
for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{K}
. This is known as a generalization of the usual orthogonality in Hilbert spaces, and was named after Birkhoff and James since Birkhoff
[
9
]
first introduced it and James
[
20
,
21
]
greatly developed its theory. For nice surveys on Birkhoff-James orthogonality, see
[
3
,
4
,
7
]
.

Let
X
and
Y
be Banach spaces, and let
T:X\to Y
. Then,
T
is called a
Birkhoff-James orthogonality preserver
if it is bijective and
x\perp_{BJ}y
is equivalent to
Tx\perp_{BJ}Ty
. The following result comes from
[
42
, Theorem 3.10]
, which is useful for constructing homeomorphisms between geometric structure spaces of Banach spaces.

Theorem 4.19
.

Let
X
and
Y
be Banach spaces, and let
T:X\to Y
be a Birkhoff-James orthogonality preserver. Define a mapping
\Phi_{T}:\mathfrak{S}(X)\to\mathfrak{S}(Y)
by
\Phi_{T}(I)=T(I)
for each
I\in\mathfrak{S}(X)
. Then,
\Phi_{T}
is a homeomorphism with
\Phi_{T}^{-1}=\Phi_{T^{-1}}
.

Since isometric isomorphism between Banach spaces are obviously Birkhoff-James orthogonality preservers, we can apply the preceding theorem to such mappings.

5.
The case of
C^{*}
-algebras

This section is devoted to the case of
C^{*}
-algebras. Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Then, as was shown in
[
46
, Proposition 3.2]
, the geometric structure space of
\mathfrak{A}
is given by

\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})=\{\ker\rho:\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})\}.

Based on this information, we aim to identify the
\mathfrak{G}
- and
\mathfrak{P}
-transforms of
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
via
\mathfrak{GS}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{PS}(\mathfrak{A})
.

Remark 5.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, and let
\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
. Then, as was noted (without a detailed proof) in
[
1
, 5.4]
, there exist a pure state
\tau
of
\mathfrak{A}
and a unitary element
U
of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
such that
\rho U=\tau
, where
\rho U\in\mathfrak{A}^{*}
is defined by
(\rho U)(A)=\rho(UA)
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. We realize this fact for the reader’s convenience.

Let
\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
, and let
F=\{\tau\in B_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}^{*}}:\tau|\mathfrak{A}=\rho\}
. Then,
F\neq\emptyset
by the Hahn-Banach theorem, and
F
is a weakly
∗
closed face of
B_{A^{*}}
. Hence, by the Krein-Milman theorem, there exists a
\overline{\rho}\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(F)\subset\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}^{*}})
such that
\overline{\rho}|\mathfrak{A}=\rho
. If there exist a pure state
\tau
of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
and a unitary
U\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
such that
\overline{\rho}U=\tau
, then
\rho U=\tau|\mathfrak{A}
and
\rho=(\tau|\mathfrak{A})U^{*}
since
\mathfrak{A}
is an ideal of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
, in which case,
\rho|\mathfrak{A}
is a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
. Indeed, we have
\|\tau|\mathfrak{A}\|=\|\rho\|=1
by

\|\tau|\mathfrak{A}\|=\|\rho U\|\leq\|\rho\|=\|(\tau|\mathfrak{A})U^{*}\|\leq\|\tau|\mathfrak{A}\|.

If
\tau|\mathfrak{A}=(1-t)\tau_{1}+t\tau_{2}
for some state
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
of
\mathfrak{A}
and some
t\in(0,1)
, then
\tau_{j}
is extended uniquely to a state
\overline{\tau_{j}}
of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
for
j=1,2
. To be precise, let
\overline{\tau_{j}}(A+c\mathbf{1})=\tau_{j}(A)+c
for each
A+c\mathbf{1}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
and
j=1,2
. It follows that

\displaystyle((1-t)\overline{\tau_{1}}+t\overline{\tau_{2}})(A+c\mathbf{1})

\displaystyle=(1-t)(\tau_{1}(A)+c)+t(\tau_{2}(A)+c)

\displaystyle=((1-t)\tau_{1}+t\tau_{2})(A)+c

\displaystyle=\tau(A)+\tau(c\mathbf{1})

\displaystyle=\tau(A+c\mathbf{1})

for each
A+c\mathbf{1}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
, that is,
\tau=(1-t)\overline{\tau_{1}}+t\overline{\tau_{2}}
. Since
\tau
is a pure state of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
, we have
\tau=\overline{\tau_{1}}=\overline{\tau_{2}}
and
\tau|A=\tau_{1}=\tau_{2}
. This shows that
\tau|\mathfrak{A}
is a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
. Hence, it is sufficient to prove the claim for unital
C^{*}
-algebras.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{A}
is unital, and that
\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
. Let
\pi_{u}:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathcal{H}
be the universal representation of
\mathfrak{A}
, and let
\mathcal{R}
be the weak-operator closure of
\pi_{u}(\mathfrak{A})
in
B(\mathcal{H})
. We note that each element of
\pi_{u}(\mathfrak{A})^{*}
is normal and extends, uniquely and without changing norm, to a normal element of
\mathcal{R}^{*}
. Let
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{R}^{*}
denote the normal extension of
\rho\circ\pi_{u}^{-1}\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\pi_{u}(\mathfrak{A})^{*}})
. By the polar decomposition, there exist a normal state
\tau_{0}
of
\mathcal{R}
and a partial isometry
V\in\mathcal{R}
such that
\rho_{0}V=\tau_{0}
and
\rho_{0}=\tau_{0}V^{*}
. We note that
\tau_{0}|\pi_{u}(\mathfrak{A})
is a pure state of
\pi_{u}(\mathfrak{A})
. Indeed, we have
\|\tau_{0}|\pi_{u}(\mathfrak{A})\|=\|\tau_{0}\|=1
by the Kaplansky density theorem since
\tau
is normal. Moreover, if
\tau_{0}=(1-t)\tau_{1}+t\tau_{2}
on
\pi_{u}(\mathfrak{A})
for some states
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
of
\pi_{u}(\mathfrak{A})
and some
t\in(0,1)
, then
\tau_{0}=(1-t)\tau_{1}+t\tau_{2}
on
\mathcal{R}
by the weak-operator density of
\pi_{u}(\mathfrak{A})
in
\mathcal{R}
, where
\tau_{j}
is extended uniquely to a normal linear functional for
j=1,2
. It follows from
\tau_{0}(V^{*}V)=\rho_{0}(V)=\tau_{0}(\mathbf{1})=1
that
\tau_{1}(V^{*}V)=\tau_{2}(V^{*}V)=1
, which implies that
\tau_{j}=\tau_{j}V^{*}V
for
j=1,2
. Now, we note that
\rho_{0}=\tau_{1}V^{*}=\tau_{2}V^{*}
since
\rho_{0}=\tau_{0}V^{*}=(1-t)(\tau_{1}V^{*})+t(\tau_{2}V^{*})
and
\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\pi_{u}(\mathfrak{A})^{*}})
. Therefore,
\tau_{0}=\rho V=\tau_{1}=\tau_{2}
by
\tau_{j}V^{*}V=\tau_{j}
for
j=1,2
. This shows that
\tau_{0}|\pi_{u}(\mathfrak{A})
is a pure state of
\pi_{u}(\mathfrak{A})
, and hence,
\tau=\tau_{0}\circ\pi_{u}
is a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
. Since
\pi_{u}
is a direct sum of all representations obtained from states of
\mathfrak{A}
by the GNS construction, the representation
(\pi_{\tau},\mathcal{H}_{\tau})
is a summand of
(\pi_{u},\mathcal{H})
