. In particular, we can regard
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}
as a subspace of
\mathcal{H}
in a natural manner. Let
x\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\tau}}
be such that
\tau(A)=\langle\pi_{\tau}(A)x,x\rangle
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, and let
P
be the projection from
\mathcal{H}
onto
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}
. Then, we obtain
Px=x
and

\tau_{0}(\pi_{u}(A))=\tau(A)=\langle\pi_{\tau}(A)x,x\rangle=\langle\pi_{u}(A)x,x\rangle

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. Combining this with the weak-operator density of
\pi_{u}(A)
in
\mathcal{R}
, we derive that
\tau_{0}(A)=\langle Ax,x\rangle
for each
A\in\mathcal{R}
. Moreover since
P\in\pi_{u}(A)^{\prime}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\rho(A)=\rho_{0}(\pi_{u}(A))=\tau_{0}(V^{*}\pi_{u}(A))

\displaystyle=\langle V^{*}\pi_{u}(A)x,x\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\pi_{u}(A)x,PVPx\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\pi_{\tau}(A)x,PVPx\rangle

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. We note that
\|PVPx\|=1
by
\|\rho\|=1
. Since
\tau
is a pure state, the representation
\pi_{\tau}
is irreducible. Thus, there exists a unitary element
U\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
\pi_{\tau}(U)PVPx=x
, in which case,

\rho(U^{*}A)=\langle\pi_{\tau}(U^{*}A)x,PVPx\rangle=\langle\pi_{\tau}(A)x,\pi_{\tau}(U)PVPx\rangle=\tau(A)

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, that is,
\rho U^{*}=\tau
. This proves the claim.

Remark 5.2
.

The converse to the claim in the preceding remark is also true. Namely, if
\mathfrak{A}
is a
C^{*}
-algebra, then
\rho U\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
whenever
\rho
is a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
and
U
is a unitary element of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
. Indeed since
\mathfrak{A}
is an ideal of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
, the mapping
\tau\mapsto\tau U
is an isometric isomorphism on
\mathfrak{A}^{*}
. Combining this with the fact that
\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
, we obtain
\rho U\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
. In summary, the identity

\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})=\{\rho U:\text{$\rho$ is a pure state of $\mathfrak{A}$ and $U\in\mathcal{U}$}\}

holds, where
\mathcal{U}
is the unitary group of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
.

The following lemma readily follows from Remark
5.2
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Then,

\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})=\{\ker\rho U:\text{$\rho$ is a pure state of $\mathfrak{A}$ and $U\in\mathcal{U}$}\},

where
\mathcal{U}
is the unitary group of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
.

To identify
\mathfrak{GS}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{PS}(\mathfrak{A})
for
C^{*}
-algebras
\mathfrak{A}
, we give a characterization of the equivalence relation on
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
in terms of representations. To this end, some auxiliary results will be needed. Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, and let
\rho
be a state of
\mathfrak{A}
. Then,
\pi_{\rho}:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})
denotes the
*
-representation of
\mathfrak{A}
obtained from
\rho
by the GNS construction. If
x_{\rho}\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}
satisfies
\rho(A)=\langle\pi_{\rho}(A)x_{\rho},x_{\rho}\rangle
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, then

\langle(\pi_{\rho}(A)+c\mathbf{1})x_{\rho},x_{\rho}\rangle=\langle\pi_{\rho}(A)x_{\rho},x_{\rho}\rangle+c=\rho(A)+c=\overline{\rho}(A+c\mathbf{1})

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
and each
c\in\mathbb{C}
, where
\overline{\rho}
is a unique extension of
\rho
to
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
. Hence, we can extend
\pi_{\rho}
to
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
by setting
\pi_{\rho}(A+c\mathbf{1})=\pi_{\rho}(A)+c\mathbf{1}
for each
A+c\mathbf{1}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
, in which case,
\overline{\rho}(A+c\mathbf{1})=\langle\pi_{\rho}(A+c\mathbf{1})x_{\rho},x_{\rho}\rangle
for each
A+c\mathbf{1}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
. In the following, if
\mathfrak{A}
is non-unital, the symbol
\pi_{\rho}
stands for this extended representation of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
. We note that
\pi_{\rho}
is an irreducible representation of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
whenever
\rho
is a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
since
\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})\subset\pi_{\rho}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}})
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, and let
\rho
be a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
. Define an element
\rho_{x,y}
of
\mathfrak{A}^{*}
by

\rho_{x,y}(A)=\langle\pi_{\rho}(A)x,y\rangle

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
and each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
. Then,
\rho_{x,y}\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
for each
x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}
.

Proof.

Let
x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}
, and let
x_{\rho}\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}
be such that
\rho(A)=\langle\pi_{\rho}(A)x_{\rho},x_{\rho}\rangle
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. Since the representation
\pi_{\rho}
of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
is irreducible, there exist unitary elements
U,V
of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
such that
\pi(U)x_{\rho}=x
and
\pi(V)x_{\rho}=y
. It follows that

(U\rho V^{*})(A)=\rho(V^{*}AU)=\langle\pi_{\rho}(V^{*}AU)x_{\rho},x_{\rho}\rangle=\langle\pi_{\rho}(A)x,y\rangle=\rho_{x,y}(A)

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, that is,
U\rho V^{*}=\rho_{x,y}
. Combining this with the fact that the mapping
\tau\mapsto U\tau V^{*}
is an isometric isomorphism on
\mathfrak{A}^{*}
, we infer that
\rho_{x,y}\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
.
∎

Let
\rho
be a pure state of a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
. Then, it follows from

\rho_{x,y}(A)=\langle\pi_{\rho}(A)x,y\rangle=\overline{\langle\pi_{\rho}(A^{*})y,x\rangle}=\overline{\rho_{y,x}(A^{*})}

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
that
\rho_{x,y}(A)=0
if and only if
\rho_{y,x}(A^{*})=0
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, and let
\rho
be a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
. Then, the following hold:

(i)

Suppose that
x,y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\setminus\{0\}
. Then,
\ker\rho_{x,y_{1}}=\ker\rho_{x,y_{2}}
if and only if
\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
is linearly dependent.

(ii)

Suppose that
x_{1},x_{2},y\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\setminus\{0\}
. Then,
\ker\rho_{x_{1},y}=\ker\rho_{x_{2},y}
if and only if
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
is linearly dependent.

Proof.

(i) Suppose that
\ker\rho_{x,y_{1}}=\ker\rho_{x,y_{2}}
. Then,
\rho_{x,y_{2}}=c\rho_{x,y_{1}}=\rho_{x,\overline{c}y_{1}}
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. Since
\pi_{\rho}
is irreducible, we have
\pi_{\rho}(A)x=y_{2}-cy_{1}
for some
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, which implies that

0=\rho_{x,y_{2}}(A)-\rho_{x,cy_{1}}(A)=\langle\pi_{\rho}(A)x,y_{2}-cy_{1}\rangle=\|y_{2}-cy_{1}\|^{2}.

Hence,
y_{2}=cy_{1}
, that is,
\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
is linearly dependent. Conversely, if
\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
is linearly dependent, then
y_{2}=cy_{1}
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. It follows from
\rho_{x,y_{2}}=\rho_{x,cy_{1}}=\overline{c}\rho_{x,y_{1}}
that
\ker\rho_{x,y_{1}}=\ker\rho_{x,y_{2}}
. This proves (i).

(ii) We note that
\ker\rho_{x_{j},y}=\{A^{*}:A\in\ker\rho_{y,x_{j}}\}
and
\ker\rho_{y,x_{j}}=\{A^{*}:A\in\ker\rho_{x_{j},y}\}
for
j=1,2
. Hence, we have
\ker\rho_{x_{1},y}=\ker\rho_{x_{2},y}
if and only if
\ker\rho_{y,x_{1}}=\ker\rho_{y,x_{2}}
, which occurs if and only if
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
is linearly dependent by (i).
∎

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, and let
\rho
be a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
. Then, the following hold:

(i)

Suppose that
x,y_{1},y_{2}\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}
. If
\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
is linearly independent, then

\{\ker\rho_{x,y_{1}},\ker\rho_{x,y_{2}}\}\not\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{A}).

(ii)

Suppose that
x_{1},x_{2},y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}
. If
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
is linearly independent, then

\{\ker\rho_{x_{1},y},\ker\rho_{x_{2},y}\}\not\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{A}).

Proof.

Suppose that
\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
is linearly independent. Set
M=\langle\{y_{1},y_{2}\}\rangle
, that is,
M
is the linear span of
\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
. If
y\in S_{M}
, then
y=\lambda y_{1}+\mu y_{2}
, which implies that
\rho_{x,y}=\overline{\lambda}\rho_{x,y_{1}}+\overline{\mu}\rho_{x,y_{2}}
. Hence,
\ker\rho_{x,y}\in\{\ker\rho_{x,y_{1}},\ker\rho_{x,y_{2}}\}^{=}
. Meanwhile,
\ker\rho_{x,y}\not\in\{\ker\rho_{x,y_{1}},\ker\rho_{x,y_{2}}\}
if
\{y,y_{j}\}
is linearly independent for
j=1,2
. Therefore,
\{\ker\rho_{x,y_{1}},\ker\rho_{x,y_{2}}\}\not\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{A})
. This proves (i). An argument similar to above also shows (ii).
∎

Before characterizing the equivalence relation on
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
, we give a technical remark on elements of
\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
.

Remark 5.7
.

Let
\rho
be a pure state of a
C*
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
. If
\rho U
is also a pure state for some unitary element
U
in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
, then
\rho=\rho U
. Indeed since
\rho(A)=\langle\pi_{\rho}(A)x_{\rho},x_{\rho}\rangle
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, it follows that
\rho U=\rho_{x_{\rho},y}
for
y=\pi_{\rho}(U)^{*}x_{\rho}
. If
\pi_{\rho}(A)\geq 0
, then
\pi_{\rho}(A)^{1/2}\in\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})
since
\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})
is a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
B(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})
. Let
\pi_{\rho}(B)=\pi_{\rho}(A)^{1/2}
. It may be assumed that
B^{*}=B
since
\pi_{\rho}(2^{-1}(B+B^{*}))=\pi_{\rho}(B)
, in which case,
B^{2}\geq 0
and
\pi_{\rho}(B^{2})=\pi_{\rho}(B)^{2}=\pi_{\rho}(A)
. Since
\rho U
is positive, we obtain

\langle\pi_{\rho}(A)x_{\rho},y\rangle=\langle\pi_{\rho}(B^{2})x_{\rho},y\rangle=(\rho U)(B^{2})\geq 0.

Meanwhile,
\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})
is strong-operator dense in
B(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})
since it acts irreducibly on
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
. By the Kadison transitivity theorem, each positive element of
B(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})
is the strong-operator limit of a net of positive elements of
\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})
. Therefore,
\langle Ax_{\rho},y\rangle\geq 0
for each positive element
A
of
B(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})
. This means that the functional
A\mapsto\langle Ax_{\rho},y\rangle
is a state of
B(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})
, which implies that
x_{\rho}=y
. Thus,
\rho=\rho U
.

As a consequence of the preceding paragraph, if
\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
, then there exists a unique pure state
\tau
of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\rho=\tau U
for some unitary element
U
of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
. Indeed, if
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
are pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
, and if
\tau_{1}U_{1}=\rho=\tau_{2}U_{2}
for some unitary elements
U_{1},U_{2}
of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
, then
\tau_{1}U_{1}U_{2}^{*}=\tau_{2}
, that is,
\tau_{1}U_{1}U_{2}^{*}
is a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
, which implies that
\tau_{1}=\tau_{1}U_{1}U_{2}^{*}=\tau_{2}
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, and let
\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
. Then,
|\rho|
denotes a unique pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\rho=|\rho|U
for some unitary element
U
of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
.

If
\pi_{1}:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H}_{1})
and
\pi_{2}:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H}_{2})
are irreducible representations, then
(\pi_{1}\oplus\pi_{2})(\mathfrak{A})
acts non-degenerately on
\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}
. Indeed, if
x\oplus y
is a nonzero element of
\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}
, then
x\neq 0
or
y\neq 0
. In the case that
x\neq 0
, there exists an
A\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
\pi_{1}(A)x=x
. It follows that
((\pi_{1}\oplus\pi_{2})(A))(x,y)=(\pi_{1}(A)x,\pi_{2}(A)y)\neq 0
. We have the same consequence as above in the other case.

Recall that if
\pi:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H})
is a representation, then
\ker\pi
is a norm-closed ideal of
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{A}/\ker\pi
is
*
-isomorphic to
\pi(\mathfrak{A})
under the
*
-isomorphism
A+\ker\pi\mapsto\pi(A)
. If
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, then
[
10
, Proposition II.5.1.5]
generates an element
A^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
\pi(A)=\pi(A^{\prime})
and
\|A^{\prime}\|=\|A+\ker\pi\|=\|\pi(A)\|
. This shows that
\pi(B_{\mathfrak{A}})=B_{\pi(\mathfrak{A})}
.

Now, the equivalence relation on
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
is characterized as follows.

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, and let
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
. Then,
\ker\rho_{1}\sim\ker\rho_{2}
if and only if
\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}
and
\pi_{|\rho_{2}|}
are unitarily equivalent.

Proof.

Let
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
be unitary elements of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
such that
\rho_{j}=|\rho_{j}|U_{j}
for
j=1,2
. Suppose that
\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}
and
\pi_{|\rho_{2}|}
are not unitarily equivalent. In this case, the set
\{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\}
is linearly independent; in particular,
\ker\rho_{1}\neq\ker\rho_{2}
. Indeed, if
\{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\}
is linearly dependent, then
\rho_{1}=c\rho_{2}=c|\rho_{2}|U_{2}=|\rho_{2}|(cU)
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}
with
|c|=1
, which implies that
|\rho_{1}|=|\rho_{2}|
, a contradiction. We note that
|\rho_{j}|=|\rho_{j}|_{x_{|\rho_{j}|},x_{|\rho_{j}|}}
and
\rho_{j}=|\rho_{j}|_{x_{|\rho_{j}|},y_{j}}
for
j=1,2
, where
y_{j}=\pi_{|\rho_{j}|}(U_{j})^{*}x_{|\rho_{j}|}
.

Since
\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}
and
\pi_{|\rho_{2}|}
are irreducible and not unitarily equivalent, they are disjoint from each other, which implies that the strong-operator closure of
(\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}\oplus\pi_{|\rho_{2}|})(\mathfrak{A})
in
B(\mathcal{H}_{|\rho_{1}|}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{|\rho_{2}|})
coincides with the direct sum
B(\mathcal{H}_{|\rho_{1}|})\oplus B(\mathcal{H}_{|\rho_{2}|})
; see
[
32
, Corollary 2.2.5, Theorems 3.8.11 and 3.13.2, and 3.8.13]
. Now, let
\ker\rho\in\{\ker\rho_{1},\ker\rho_{2}\}^{=}
. Since
\ker\rho_{1}\cap\ker\rho_{2}\subset\ker\rho
, we have
\rho=c_{1}\rho_{1}+c_{2}\rho_{2}
. Let
V_{j}
be a unitary operator in
B(\mathcal{H}_{|\rho_{j}|})
such that
V_{j}(c_{j}x_{|\rho_{j}|})=|c_{j}|y_{j}
. Then,
(V_{1},V_{2})
is a unitary element of
B(\mathcal{H}_{|\rho_{1}|})\oplus B(\mathcal{H}_{|\rho_{2}|})
. Meanwhile, since

\rho_{1}(A)=\langle\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}(A)x_{|\rho_{1}|},y_{1}\rangle=\langle(\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}\oplus\pi_{|\rho_{2}|})(A)(x_{|\rho_{1}|},0),(y_{1},0)\rangle

and

\rho_{2}(A)=\langle\pi_{|\rho_{2}|}(A)x_{|\rho_{2}|},y_{2}\rangle=\langle(\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}\oplus\pi_{|\rho_{2}|})(A)(0,x_{|\rho_{2}|}),(0,y_{2})\rangle

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, it follows that

\displaystyle(c_{1}\rho_{1}+c_{2}\rho_{2})(A)

\displaystyle=\langle(\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}\oplus\pi_{|\rho_{2}|})(A)(c_{1}x_{|\rho_{1}|},0),(y_{1},0)\rangle+\langle(\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}\oplus\pi_{|\rho_{2}|})(A)(0,c_{2}x_{|\rho_{2}|}),(0,y_{2})\rangle

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. Since
(V_{1},V_{2})\in B(\mathcal{H}_{|\rho_{1}|})\oplus B(\mathcal{H}_{|\rho_{2}|})
, there exists a net
(B_{\lambda})_{\lambda}
in the unit ball of
(\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}\oplus\pi_{|\rho_{2}|})(\mathfrak{A})
that converges to
(V_{1},V_{2})
with respect to the strong-operator topology. Moreover, the unit ball of
(\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}\oplus\pi_{|\rho_{2}|})(\mathfrak{A})
coincides with
(\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}\oplus\pi_{|\rho_{2}|})(B_{\mathfrak{A}})
. Hence,
B_{\lambda}=(\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}\oplus\pi_{|\rho_{2}|})(A_{\lambda})
for some
A_{\lambda}\in B_{\mathfrak{A}}
and

\displaystyle|c_{1}|+|c_{2}|

\displaystyle=|\langle(V_{1},V_{2})(c_{1}x_{|\rho_{1}|},0),(y_{1},0)\rangle+\langle(V_{1},V_{2})(0,c_{2}x_{|\rho_{2}|}),(0,y_{2})\rangle|

\displaystyle=\lim_{\lambda}|(c_{1}\rho_{1}+c_{2}\rho_{2})(A_{\lambda})|

\displaystyle\leq\|c_{1}\rho_{1}+c_{2}\rho_{2}\|

\displaystyle\leq|c_{1}|+|c_{2}|.

This together with
\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
shows that
|c_{1}|+|c_{2}|=\|c_{1}\rho_{1}+c_{2}\rho_{2}\|=1
. If
c_{1}c_{2}\neq 0
, we derive that

\rho=|c_{1}|(|c_{1}|^{-1}c_{1}\rho_{1})+|c_{2}|(|c_{2}|^{-1}c_{2}\rho_{2}),

and
|c_{1}|^{-1}c_{1}\rho_{1}\neq|c_{2}|^{-1}c_{2}\rho_{2}
since
\{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\}
is linearly independent. However, this contradicts
\rho\in ext(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
. Thus,
c_{1}=0
or
c_{2}=0
, that is,
\rho=c_{1}\rho_{1}
or
\rho=c_{2}\rho_{2}
, which shows that
\ker\rho\in\{\ker\rho_{1},\ker\rho_{2}\}
. Hence,
\{\ker\rho_{1},\ker\rho_{2}\}\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{A})
, and
\ker\rho_{1}\not\rightleftarrows\ker\rho_{2}
.

Next, suppose that
\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}
and
\pi_{|\rho_{2}|}
are unitarily equivalent, and that
\ker\rho_{1}\neq\ker\rho_{2}
. Let
U:\mathcal{H}_{1}\to\mathcal{H}_{2}
be an isometric isomorphism such that
\pi_{|\rho_{2}|}(A)=U\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}(A)U^{*}
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. Then, it follows that

\rho_{2}(A)=\langle\pi_{|\rho_{2}|}(A)x_{|\rho_{2}|},y_{2}\rangle=\langle U\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}(A)U^{*}x_{|\rho_{2}|},y_{2}\rangle=\langle\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}(A)U^{*}x_{|\rho_{2}|},U^{*}y_{2}\rangle

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. Here, we note that either
\{x_{|\rho_{1}|},U^{*}x_{|\rho_{2}|}\}
or
\{y_{1},U^{*}y_{2}\}
is linearly independent by
\ker\rho_{1}\neq\ker\rho_{2}
. Indeed, if
\{x_{|\rho_{1}|},U^{*}x_{|\rho_{2}|}\}
and
\{y_{1},U^{*}y_{2}\}
are both linearly dependent, then
U^{*}x_{|\rho_{2}|}=cx_{|\rho_{1}|}
and
U^{*}y_{2}=dy_{1}
for some
c,d\in\mathbb{C}
with
|c|=|d|=1
, in which case,
\rho_{2}=c\overline{d}\rho_{1}
. This contradicts
\ker\rho_{1}\neq\ker\rho_{2}
. The argument will be divided into three parts.

(i) Suppose that
\{x_{|\rho_{1}|},U^{*}x_{|\rho_{2}|}\}
is linearly dependent. Then,
\{y_{1},U^{*}y_{2}\}
is linearly independent. Let
c\in\mathbb{C}
be such that
|c|=1
and
U^{*}x_{|\rho_{2}|}=cx_{|\rho_{1}|}
. Then, we have

\rho_{2}(A)=\langle\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}(A)cx_{|\rho_{1}|},U^{*}y_{2}\rangle=c|\rho_{1}|_{x_{|\rho_{1}|},U^{*}y_{2}}(A)

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, which implies that
\ker\rho_{2}=\ker|\rho_{1}|_{x_{|\rho_{1}|},U^{*}y_{2}}
. Meanwhile, Lemma
5.6
ensures that

\{\ker|\rho_{1}|_{x_{|\rho_{1}|},y},\ker\rho_{1}\}=\{\ker|\rho_{1}|_{x_{|\rho_{1}|},y},\ker|\rho_{1}|_{x_{|\rho_{1}|},y_{1}}\}\not\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{A})

and

\{\ker|\rho_{1}|_{x_{|\rho_{1}|},y},\ker\rho_{2}\}=\{\ker|\rho_{1}|_{x_{|\rho_{1}|},y},\ker|\rho_{1}|_{x_{|\rho_{1}|},U^{*}y_{2}}\}\not\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{A})

whenever
\{y,y_{1}\}
and
\{y,U^{*}y_{2}\}
are linearly independent. We note that such an element
y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{|\rho_{1}|}}
exists since
\{y_{1},U^{*}y_{2}\}
is linearly independent. This shows that
\ker\rho_{1}\sim\ker\rho_{2}
.

(ii) Suppose that
\{y_{1},U^{*}y_{2}\}
is linearly dependent. Then,
\{x_{|\rho_{1}|},U^{*}x_{|\rho_{2}|}\}
is linearly independent, and
U^{*}y_{2}=dy_{1}
for some
d\in\mathbb{C}
with
|d|=1
. In particular, we obtain

\rho_{2}(A)=\langle\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}(A)U^{*}x_{|\rho_{2}|},U^{*}y_{2}\rangle=\overline{d}|\rho_{1}|_{U^{*}x_{|\rho_{2}|},y_{1}}(A)

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, that is,
\ker\rho_{2}=\ker|\rho_{1}|_{U^{*}x_{|\rho_{2}|},y_{1}}
. Now, let
x\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{|\rho_{1}|}}
be such that
\{x,x_{|\rho_{1}|}\}
and
\{x,U^{*}x_{|\rho_{2}|}\}
are linearly independent. Then, it follows from Lemma
5.6
that

\{\ker|\rho_{1}|_{x,y_{1}},\ker\rho_{1}\}=\{\ker|\rho_{1}|_{x,y_{1}},\ker|\rho_{1}|_{x_{|\rho_{1}|},y_{1}}\}\not\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{A})

and

\{\ker|\rho_{1}|_{x,y_{1}},\ker\rho_{2}\}=\{\ker|\rho_{1}|_{x,y_{1}},\ker|\rho_{1}|_{U^{*}x_{|\rho_{2}|},y_{1}}\}\not\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{A}).

Therefore,
\ker\rho_{1}\sim\ker\rho_{2}
.

(iii) Suppose that
\{x_{|\rho_{1}|},U^{*}x_{|\rho_{2}|}\}
and
\{y_{1},U^{*}y_{2}\}
are linearly independent. In this case, we derive that

\{\ker|\rho_{1}|_{x_{|\rho_{1}|},U^{*}y_{2}},\ker\rho_{1}\}=\{\ker|\rho_{1}|_{x_{|\rho_{1}|},U^{*}y_{2}},\ker|\rho_{1}|_{x_{|\rho_{1}|},y_{1}}\}\not\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{A})

and

\{\ker|\rho_{1}|_{x_{|\rho_{1}|},U^{*}y_{2}},\ker\rho_{2}\}=\{\ker|\rho_{1}|_{x_{|\rho_{1}|},U^{*}y_{2}},\ker|\rho_{1}|_{U^{*}x_{|\rho_{2}|},U^{*}y_{2}}\}\not\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{A})

by Lemma
5.6
. This proves that
\ker\rho_{1}\sim\ker\rho_{2}
. Thus, in either case, the unitary equivalence of
\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}
and
\pi_{|\rho_{2}|}
implies
\ker\rho_{1}\sim\ker\rho_{2}
.

Now, we know the following two implications:
\ker\rho_{1}\rightleftarrows\ker\rho_{2}
implies that
\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}
and
\pi_{|\rho_{2}|}
are unitarily equivalent, and the unitary equivalence of
\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}
and
\pi_{|\rho_{2}|}
implies that
\ker\rho_{1}\sim\ker\rho_{2}
. Since the unitary equivalence of representations of
\mathfrak{A}
is transitive, we can conclude that
\ker\rho_{1}\sim\ker\rho_{2}
if and only if
\pi_{|\rho_{1}|}
and
\pi_{|\rho_{2}|}
are unitarily equivalent.
∎

Corollary 5.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, and let
\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
. Then,
\ker\rho\sim\ker|\rho|
. In particular, each element of
\mathfrak{GS}(\mathfrak{A})
contains a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
.

Based on Theorem
5.8
, we can find a natural relationship between elements of geometric primitive spectra and primitive ideals of
C^{*}
-algebras.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, and let
\rho
be a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
. Then,
C(\ker\rho)=\{\ker\rho_{x,y}:x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}\}
and
J_{C(\ker\rho)}=\ker\pi_{\rho}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\tau\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
and
\ker\tau\in C(\ker\rho)
. Let
U
be a unitary element of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
such that
\tau=|\tau|U
. Since
\ker\rho\sim\ker\tau
, the representations
\pi_{\rho}
and
\pi_{|\tau|}
are unitarily equivalent by Theorem
5.8
. Let
U_{0}:\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\to\mathcal{H}_{|\tau|}
be an isometric isomorphism such that
\pi_{|\tau|}(A)=U_{0}\pi_{\rho}(A)U_{0}^{*}
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. Then, it follows that

\displaystyle\tau(A)=|\tau|(UA)=\langle\pi_{|\tau|}(UA)x_{|\tau|},x_{|\tau|}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle U_{0}\pi_{\rho}(UA)U_{0}^{*}x_{|\tau|},x_{|\tau|}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\pi_{\rho}(A)U_{0}^{*}x_{|\tau|},\pi_{\rho}(U)^{*}U_{0}^{*}x_{|\tau|}\rangle

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. Therefore, setting
x=U_{0}^{*}x_{|\tau|}
and
y=\pi_{\rho}(U)^{*}U_{0}^{*}x_{|\tau|}
, we have
\tau=\rho_{x,y}
and
\ker\tau=\ker\rho_{x,y}
.

Conversely, suppose that
x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}
. Since
\pi_{\rho}
is an irreducible representation of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
, there exists a unitary element
U
of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
such that
\pi_{\rho}(U)x=y
, which implies that
\rho_{x,y}=\rho_{x,x}U^{*}
. In particular, we obtain
|\rho_{x,y}|=\rho_{x,x}
. Moreover, since each unit vector in
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
is cyclic for
\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})
, the representations
\pi_{\rho}
and
\pi_{\rho_{x,x}}
are unitarily equivalent by the essential uniqueness of the GNS construction. Therefore,
\ker\rho\sim\ker\rho_{x,x}\sim\ker\rho_{x,y}
by Theorem
5.8
and Corollary
5.9
. This shows that
\ker\rho_{x,y}\in C(\ker\rho)
for each
x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}
. Thus,
C(\ker\rho)=\{\ker\rho_{x,y}:x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}\}
.

Finally, we note that
A\in J_{C(\ker\rho)}
in and only if
\langle\pi_{\rho}(A)x,y\rangle=\rho_{x,y}(A)=0
for each
x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}
, which occurs if and only if
\pi_{\rho}(A)=0
. Hence,
J_{C(\ker\rho)}=\ker\pi_{\rho}
.
∎

Now, we give the main theorem in this section.

Theorem 5.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Then, the spectrum
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
of
\mathfrak{A}
is homeomorphic to
\mathfrak{GS}(\mathfrak{A})
, and the primitive spectrum
\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(A)
of
\mathfrak{A}
coincides with
\mathfrak{PS}(\mathfrak{A})
. Moreover, a natural homeomorphism
\Phi:\hat{\mathfrak{A}}\to\mathfrak{GS}(\mathfrak{A})
is given by
\Phi(\overline{\pi_{\rho}})=C(\ker\rho)
for each pure state
\rho
of
\mathfrak{A}
, where
\overline{\pi_{\rho}}
is the unitary equivalence class of
\pi_{\rho}
.

Proof.

Recall that a subset
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathfrak{A}
is a primitive ideal of
\mathfrak{A}
if and only if
\mathcal{I}=\ker\pi_{\rho}
for some pure state
\rho
of
\mathfrak{A}
. Moreover, we have
\ker\pi_{\rho}=J_{C(\ker\rho)}
by Lemma
5.10
. Hence, it follows from Corollary
5.9
that
\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{A})=\mathfrak{PS}(\mathfrak{A})
as sets. Then, it is obvious that
S^{\cong}
coincides with the hull-kernel closure of
S
in
\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{A})
.

Next, suppose that
\pi
is an irreducible representation. Then, there exists a pure state
\rho
of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\pi
is unitarily equivalent to
\pi_{\rho}
. Let
\Phi(\overline{\pi_{\rho}})=C(\ker\rho)
for each pure state
\rho
of
\mathfrak{A}
. Since
\overline{\pi_{\rho_{1}}}=\overline{\pi_{\rho_{2}}}
if and only if
C(\ker\rho_{1})=C(\ker\rho_{2})
by Theorem
5.8
, the mapping
\Phi:\hat{\mathfrak{A}}\to\mathfrak{GS}(\mathfrak{A})
is well-defined and injective. Moreover,
\Phi
is surjective by Corollary
5.9
.

Now, let
\kappa(\overline{\pi})=\ker\pi
for each
\overline{\pi}\in\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
. Then, the topology on
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
is defined as follows: A subset
F
of
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
is closed if and only if
C=\kappa^{-1}(F)
for some hull-kernel closed subset
F
of
\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{A})=\mathfrak{PS}(\mathfrak{A})
. Meanwhile, we have
C^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{GC}(\mathfrak{A})
if and only if
C^{\prime}=\gamma_{\mathfrak{A}}^{-1}(F^{\prime})
for some
F^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{PC}(\mathfrak{A})
by Lemma
3.19
(i) and Theorem
4.7
, for
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime\equiv}=\gamma_{\mathfrak{A}}^{-1}(\gamma_{\mathfrak{A}}(C^{\prime})^{\cong})
provided that
C^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{GC}(\mathfrak{A})
. We claim that
\Phi(\kappa^{-1}(S))=\gamma_{\mathfrak{A}}^{-1}(S)
for each
S\subset\mathfrak{PS}(\mathfrak{A})
. Indeed, if
C(\ker\rho)\in\Phi(\kappa^{-1}(S))
, then
C(\ker\rho)=\Phi(\overline{\pi_{\rho}})
for some
\overline{\pi_{\rho}}\in\kappa^{-1}(S)
, in which case,
\kappa(\overline{\pi_{\rho}})\in S
. Since
\kappa(\overline{\pi_{\rho}})=\ker\pi_{\rho}=J_{C(\ker\rho)}=\gamma_{\mathfrak{A}}(C(\ker\rho))
, it follows that
C(\ker\rho)\in\gamma_{\mathfrak{A}}^{-1}(S)
. Therefore,
\Phi(\kappa^{-1}(S))\subset\gamma_{\mathfrak{A}}^{-1}(S)
. Conversely, if
C(\ker\rho)\in\gamma_{\mathfrak{A}}^{-1}(S)
, then
\kappa(\overline{\pi_{\rho}})=\gamma_{\mathfrak{A}}(C(\ker\rho))\in S
, which implies that
C(\ker\rho)=\Phi(\overline{\pi_{\rho}})\in\Phi(\kappa^{-1}(S))
. This proves the claim.

Finally, if
C
is closed in
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
, then
C=\kappa^{-1}(F)
for some
F\in\mathfrak{PC}(\mathfrak{A})
by the preceding paragraph. Since
\Phi(C)=\Phi(\kappa^{-1}(F))=\gamma_{\mathfrak{A}}^{-1}(F)
, it follows that
\Phi(C)\in\mathfrak{GC}(\mathfrak{A})
. Conversely, if
\Phi(C)\in\mathfrak{GC}(\mathfrak{A})
, then
\Phi(C)=\gamma_{\mathfrak{A}}^{-1}(F)
for some
F\in\mathfrak{PC}(\mathfrak{A})
, which together with
\gamma_{\mathfrak{A}}^{-1}(F)=\Phi(\kappa^{-1}(F))
implies that
C=\kappa^{-1}(F)
, that is,
C
is closed in
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
. This shows that
\Phi
preserves closed sets in both directions. Thus,
\Phi
is a homeomorphism.
∎

Since
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A}),\mathfrak{GS}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{PS}(\mathfrak{A})
are constructed from the Banach space structure of
\mathfrak{A}
, the preceding theorem gives a Banach space theoretical construction of the (primitive) spectrum of
\mathfrak{A}
. Moreover, in the light of Theorem
4.16
, the spectrum
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
and primitive spectrum
\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{A})
of
\mathfrak{A}
are homeomorphic to
\mathfrak{G}
- and
\mathfrak{P}
-transforms of
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
, respectively.

Here, we recall that (primitive) spectra of
C^{*}
-algebras are always topologizable. Combining this with Theorem
5.11
, we immediately have the following result.

Corollary 5.12
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Then,
\mathfrak{GS}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{PS}(\mathfrak{A})
are topologizable.

The following is a consequence of Corollary
4.17
and Theorem
5.11
.

Corollary 5.13
.

Let
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}
be
C^{*}
-algebras. If
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})
are homeomorphic, then so are the (primitive) spectra of
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
.

This will be a useful tool for classifying
C^{*}
-algebras in terms of geometric structure spaces.

The rest of this section focuses on geometric structure spaces of images of irreducible representations of
C^{*}
-algebras. We begin with a simple lemma.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, and let
\rho
be a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
. Suppose that
B\in B_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}}
and
\|\rho B\|=1
. Then,
\rho B\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
.

Proof.

We note that
\rho B=\rho_{x_{\rho},y}
for
y=\pi_{\rho}(B)^{*}x_{\rho}
. Moreover, it follows from
\|B\|\leq 1
and
\|\rho B\|=1
that
\|y\|=1
. By the irreducibility of
\pi_{\rho}
, there exists a unitary element
U\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
such that
\pi_{\rho}(U)x_{\rho}=y
, which implies that
\rho B=\rho_{x_{\rho},y}=\rho U^{*}\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
.
∎

We also need the following basic lemma.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, and let
\mathcal{I}
be a closed ideal of
\mathfrak{A}
. Then, the unitization of
\mathfrak{A}/\mathcal{I}
is
*
-isomorphic to
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}/\mathcal{I}
under the
*
-isomorphism
(A+\mathcal{I})+c\mathbf{1}^{\prime}\mapsto(A+c\mathbf{1})+\mathcal{I}
from the unitization of
\mathfrak{A}/\mathcal{I}
onto
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}/\mathcal{I}
, where
\mathbf{1}
and
\mathbf{1}^{\prime}
are the units in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
and the unitization of
\mathfrak{A}/\mathcal{I}
respectively. Consequently,
(A+\mathcal{I})+c\mathbf{1}^{\prime}
is unitary in the unitization of
\mathfrak{A}/\mathcal{I}
if and only if
(A+c\mathbf{1})+\mathcal{I}
is unitary in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}/\mathcal{I}
.

Proof.

If
\mathfrak{A}
is unital, then
\mathfrak{A}/\mathcal{I}
is also unital. Hence, we consider the non-unital case. First, we note that
\mathcal{I}
is also an ideal of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
. To show this, suppose that
A\in\mathcal{I}
and
A\geq 0
. Since
\mathcal{I}
is a
C^{*}
-algebra and
\mathfrak{A}
is an ideal of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
, we have
A^{1/2}\in\mathcal{I}
,
BA=(BA^{1/2})A^{1/2}\in\mathcal{I}
and
AB=A^{1/2}(A^{1/2}B)\in\mathcal{I}
for each
B\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
. Combining this with the fact that
\mathcal{I}
coincides with the linear span of its positive elements, we have
BA\in\mathcal{I}
and
AB\in\mathcal{I}
whenever
A\in I
and
B\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
. We note that
A\in\mathfrak{A}
and
A+c\mathbf{1}\in\mathcal{I}
implies that
c=0
; for otherwise,
I=c^{-1}(B-A)\in\mathfrak{A}
for some
B\in\mathcal{I}\subset\mathfrak{A}
.

Next, let
A,B\in\mathfrak{A}
and
c,d\in\mathbb{C}
. Then,
(A+\mathcal{I})+c\mathbf{1}^{\prime}=(B+\mathcal{I})+d\mathbf{1}^{\prime}
if and only if
A-B\in\mathcal{I}
and
c=d
, which occurs if and only if
(A+c\mathbf{1})-(B+d\mathbf{1})\in\mathcal{I}
, that is,
(A+c\mathbf{1})+\mathcal{I}=(B+d\mathbf{1})+\mathcal{I}
. Hence, setting
\varphi((A+\mathcal{I})+c\mathbf{1}^{\prime})=(A+c\mathbf{1})+\mathcal{I}
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
and each
c\in\mathbb{C}
, we have a bijection
\varphi
from the unitization of
\mathfrak{A}/\mathcal{I}
onto
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}/\mathcal{I}
. Moreover, it follows that

\displaystyle\varphi((A+\mathcal{I})+c\mathbf{1}^{\prime})^{*})=\varphi((A^{*}+\mathcal{I})+\overline{c}\mathbf{1}^{\prime})=(A^{*}+\overline{c}\mathbf{1})+\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle=(A+c\mathbf{1})^{*}+\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle=\varphi((A+\mathcal{I})+c\mathbf{1}^{\prime})^{*},

and

\displaystyle\varphi(((A+\mathcal{I})+c\mathbf{1}^{\prime})((B+\mathcal{I})+d\mathbf{1}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\varphi((AB+cB+dA+\mathcal{I})+cd\mathbf{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(AB+cB+dA+cd\mathbf{1})+\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle=(A+c\mathbf{1})(B+d\mathbf{1})+\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle=((A+c\mathbf{1})+\mathcal{I})((B+d\mathbf{1})+\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\varphi((A+\mathcal{I})+c\mathbf{1}^{\prime})\varphi((B+\mathcal{I})+d\mathbf{1}^{\prime}).

Therefore,
\varphi
is a
*
-isomorphism.
∎

Applying the preceding lemma to primitive ideals, we have the following identity. Recall that, for a subset
M
of
\mathfrak{A}
, the symbol
M^{\perp}
stands for its annihilator, that is,
M^{\perp}=\{\rho\in\mathfrak{A}^{*}:M\subset\ker\rho\}
.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, and let
\pi:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H})
be an irreducible representation. Then,
\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{(\ker\pi)^{\perp}})=\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})\cap(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{(\ker\pi)^{\perp}})
. First, we assume that
\rho
is a state of
\mathfrak{A}
. If
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
are states of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\rho=(1-t)\rho_{1}+t\rho_{2}
for some
t\in(0,1)
, and if
A
is a positive element of
\ker\pi
, then
\rho_{1}(A)=\rho_{2}(A)=0
by
\rho_{1}(A)\geq 0
,
\rho_{2}(A)\geq 0
and
(1-t)\rho_{1}(A)+t\rho_{2}(A)=\rho(A)=0
. Since
\ker\pi
is a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
\mathfrak{A}
, every element of
\ker\pi
is a linear combination of (at most four) positive elements of
\ker\pi
. Therefore,
\rho_{j}\in(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
for
j=1,2
, which together with
\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{(\ker\pi)^{\perp}})
shows that
\rho=\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}
. This proves that
\rho
is a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
, and
\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})\cap(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
.

Next, let
(\varphi(\rho))(A+\ker\pi)=\rho(A)
for each
\rho\in(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
and each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. Then,
\varphi:(\ker\pi)^{\perp}\to(\mathfrak{A}/\ker\pi)^{*}
is an isometric isomorphism; see, for example,
[
28
, Theorem 1.10.17]
. Suppose that
\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{(\ker\pi)^{\perp}})
. We note that
\varphi(\rho)\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{(\mathfrak{A}/\ker\pi)^{*}})
, and hence,
\varphi(\rho)V
is a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}/\ker\pi
for some unitary element
V
of the unitization of
\mathfrak{A}/\ker\pi
. Set
V=(A_{0}+\ker\pi)+c\mathbf{1}^{\prime}
, where
A\in\mathfrak{A}
,
c\in\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbf{1}^{\prime}
is the unit of the unitization of
\mathfrak{A}/\ker\pi
. By Lemma
5.15
,
(A_{0}+c\mathbf{1})+\ker\pi
is a unitary element of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}/\ker\pi
. In particular, we may assume that
\|A_{0}+c\mathbf{1}\|=1
by
[
10
, Proposition II.5.1.5]
. Set
U=A_{0}+c\mathbf{1}\in S_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}}
. Since
U+\ker\pi
is unitary in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}/\ker\pi
, we have
\mathbf{1}-U^{*}U\in\ker\pi
and
\mathbf{1}-UU^{*}\in\ker\pi
. Moreover, it turns out that

\displaystyle V(A+\ker\pi)

\displaystyle=((A_{0}+\ker\pi)+c\mathbf{1}^{\prime})(A+\ker\pi)

\displaystyle=(A_{0}+\ker\pi)(A+\ker\pi)+c(A+\ker\pi)

\displaystyle=(A_{0}A+cA)+\ker\pi

\displaystyle=UA+\ker\pi

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, which implies that

(\varphi(\rho)V)(A+\ker\pi)=(\varphi(\rho))(UA+\ker\pi)=\rho(UA)=(\rho U)(A)

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. Hence,
\rho U
is positive. We claim that the mapping
\tau\mapsto\tau U
is an isometric isomorphism on
(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
. Since
\ker\pi
is an ideal of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
, we obtain
\tau U\in(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
whenever
\tau\in(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
. We note that
\|\tau U\|\leq\|\tau\|
since
\|U\|=1
. Similarly,
\tau U^{*}\in(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
whenever
\tau\in(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
, and
\|\tau U^{*}\|\leq\|\tau\|
by
\|U^{*}\|=\|U\|=1
. Meanwhile since
\mathbf{1}-U^{*}U\in\ker\pi
and
\mathbf{1}-UU^{*}\in\ker\pi
, it follows that
A-U^{*}UA\in\ker\pi
and
A-UU^{*}A\in\ker\pi
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, that is,
\tau=(\tau U^{*})U=(\tau U)U^{*}
whenever
\tau\in(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
. Thus, the mapping
\tau\mapsto\tau U
is an isometric isomorphism on
(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
. Consequently,
\rho U\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{(\ker\pi)^{\perp}})
as
\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{(\ker\pi)^{\perp}})
. Combining this with the the first paragraph, it turns out that
\rho U
is a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
. Now, Lemma
5.14
applies, and
\rho=(\rho U)U^{*}\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
.

The converse is obvious since
(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
is a subspace of
\mathfrak{A}^{*}
.
∎

Based on the preceding lemma, we can identify the geometric structure space of the image of an irreducible representation of a
C^{*}
-algebra. Recall that, for a bounded linear operator
T
from a Banach space
X
onto another
Y
, the (Banach space) adjoint
T^{*}
of
T
is defined by
(T^{*}f)(x)=f(Tx)=(f\circ T)(x)
for each
f\in X^{*}
and each
x\in X
.

Theorem 5.17
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, let
\pi
be an irreducible representation of
\mathfrak{A}
, and let
I\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
be such that
\ker\pi=J_{C(I)}
. Then,
\pi^{*}:\pi(\mathfrak{A})^{*}\to(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
is an isometric weak
∗
-to-(relative) weak
∗
homeomorphism, and

\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))=\{\ker\rho:\pi^{*}(\rho)\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})\}

Moreover, the mapping
\ker\rho\mapsto\ker\pi^{*}(\rho)
is a homeomorphism from
\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))
onto the subspace
C(I)^{=}
of
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
.

Proof.

We first note that
\pi^{*}(\rho)=\rho\circ\pi\in(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
whenever
\rho\in\pi(\mathfrak{A})^{*}
. Let
\psi(A+\ker\pi)=\pi(A)
and
(\varphi(\rho))(A+\ker\pi)=\rho(A)
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
and each
\rho\in(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
. Then,
\psi:\mathfrak{A}/\ker\pi\to\pi(\mathfrak{A})
is a
*
-isomorphism and
\varphi:(\ker\pi)^{\perp}\to(\mathfrak{A}/\ker\pi)^{*}
is an isometric isomorphism, which implies that
(\psi^{-1})^{*}\circ\varphi:(\ker\pi)^{\perp}\to\pi(\mathfrak{A})^{*}
is also an isometric isomorphism. We note that since
\varphi
is a (relative) weak
∗
-to-weak
∗
homeomorphism, so is
(\psi^{-1})^{*}\circ\varphi
. Moreover, setting
\overline{\rho}=((\psi^{-1})^{*}\circ\varphi)(\rho)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\overline{\rho}(\pi(A))=((\psi^{-1})^{*}\circ\varphi)(\rho))(\pi(A))

\displaystyle=((\psi^{-1})^{*}(\varphi(\rho)))(\pi(A))

\displaystyle=(\varphi(\rho))(\psi^{-1}(\pi(A)))

\displaystyle=\rho(A)

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
and each
\rho\in(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
, that is,
\pi^{*}(\overline{\rho})=\rho
for each
\rho\in(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
. This shows that
\pi^{*}=((\psi^{-1})^{*}\circ\varphi)^{-1}
, and
\pi^{*}:\pi(\mathfrak{A})^{*}\to(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
is an isometric weak
∗
-to-(relative) weak
∗
homeomorphism, which together with Lemma
5.16
implies that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))=\{\ker\rho:\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\pi(\mathfrak{A})^{*}})\}

\displaystyle=\{\ker\rho:\pi^{*}(\rho)\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{(\ker\pi)^{\perp}})\}

\displaystyle=\{\ker\rho:\pi^{*}(\rho)\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})\}.

Moreover, for an element
\tau\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
, it follows from
\ker\pi=J_{C(I)}
and Lemma
4.13
that
\tau\in(\ker\pi)^{\perp}
if and only if
\ker\tau\in C(I)^{=}
. Meanwhile, if
\ker\tau\in C(I)^{=}
, then
\tau\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
implies that
\tau=\pi^{*}(\rho)
for some
\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\pi(\mathfrak{A})^{*}})
. Therefore,
C(I)^{=}=\{\ker\pi^{*}(\rho):\ker\rho\in\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))\}
. We note that, for elements
\ker\rho_{1}
and
\ker\rho_{2}
of
\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))
, the equality
\ker\rho_{1}=\ker\rho_{2}
holds if and only if
\rho_{2}=c\rho_{1}
for some nonzero
c\in\mathbb{C}
, which occurs if and only if
\pi^{*}(\rho_{2})=c\pi^{*}(\rho_{1})
for some nonzero
c\in\mathbb{C}
, that is, the equality
\ker\pi^{*}(\rho_{1})=\ker\pi^{*}(\rho_{2})
holds. Thus, setting
\Phi(\ker\rho)=\ker\pi^{*}(\rho)
for each
\ker\rho\in\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))
yields a bijection from
\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))
onto
C(I)^{=}
.

Finally, suppose that
S\subset\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))
and
\ker\rho\in\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))
. We assume that
\ker\rho\in S^{=}
. If
A\in\bigcap\{\ker\pi^{*}(\tau):\ker\tau\in S\}
, then
\tau(\pi(A))=(\pi^{*}(\tau))(A)=0
whenever
\ker\tau\in S
, that is,
\pi(A)\in\bigcap\{\ker\tau:\ker\tau\in S\}\subset\ker\rho
. This means that
(\pi^{*}(\rho))(A)=\rho(\pi(A))=0
. Hence,

\bigcap\{\Phi(\ker\tau):\ker\tau\in S\}=\bigcap\{\ker\pi^{*}(\tau):\ker\tau\in S\}\subset\ker\pi^{*}(\rho)=\Phi(\ker\rho),

that is,
\Phi(\ker\rho)\in\Phi(S)^{=}
. Conversely, if
\ker\rho\not\in S^{=}
, then there exists a
\pi(A)\in\pi(\mathfrak{A})
such that
\pi(A)\in\bigcap\{\ker\tau:\ker\tau\in S\}
and
\pi(A)\not\in\ker\rho
, in which case, we have
(\pi^{*}(\tau))(A)=\tau(\pi(A))=0
whenever
\ker\tau\in S
, and
(\pi^{*}(\rho))(A)=\rho(\pi(A))\neq 0
. Therefore,
A\in\bigcap\{\ker\pi^{*}(\tau):\ker\tau\in S\}
and
A\not\in\ker\pi^{*}(\rho)
, that is,
\Phi(\ker\rho)=\ker\pi^{*}(\rho)\not\in\Phi(S)^{=}
. Now, we know that
\ker\rho\in S^{=}
if and only if
\Phi(\ker\rho)\in\Phi(S)^{=}
, which shows that
\Phi(S^{=})=\Phi(S)^{=}
. Thus,
\Phi
is a homeomorphism.
∎

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space, and let
x,y\in\mathcal{H}
. In the rest of this paper, let
\omega_{x,y}
denote the weak-operator continuous functional on
B(\mathcal{H})
defined by
\omega_{x,y}(A)=\langle Ax,y\rangle
for each
A\in B(\mathcal{H})
.

Corollary 5.18
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, let
\pi
be an irreducible representation of
\mathfrak{A}
, let
\mathcal{H}
be the Hilbert space on which
\pi(\mathfrak{A})
acts, and let
I\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
be such that
\ker\pi=J_{C(I)}
. Then,
\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))\supset\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi(\mathfrak{A}):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}}\}
.

Proof.

Let
\rho
be a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\pi_{\rho}
is unitarily equivalent to
\pi
, and let
U:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
be an isometric isomorphism such that
\pi(A)=U^{*}\pi_{\rho}(A)U
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. Suppose that
x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}}
. Then, it follows from

\displaystyle\omega_{x,y}(\pi(A))=\langle\pi(A)x,y\rangle=\langle U^{*}\pi_{\rho}(A)Ux,y\rangle=\rho_{Ux,Uy}(A)

that
\pi^{*}(\omega_{x,y}|\pi(\mathfrak{A}))=\rho_{Ux,Uy}\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
by Lemma
5.4
. Therefore,

\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi(\mathfrak{A})=\ker(\omega_{x,y}|\pi(\mathfrak{A}))\in\mathfrak{S}(\pi(A))

by Theorem
5.17
.
∎

Corollary 5.19
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space, and let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
B(\mathcal{H})
that acts irreducibly on
\mathcal{H}
. Then,
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})\supset\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}}\}
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to apply the preceding corollary to the identity representation of
\mathfrak{A}
.
∎

6.
Nonlinear theory for irreducible
C^{*}
-algebras

An
irreducible

C^{*}
-algebra is a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of some
B(\mathcal{H})
that acts irreducibly on
\mathcal{H}
. This section is devoted to special mappings between geometric structure spaces of irreducible
C^{*}
-algebras that play crucial roles in the rest of this paper. We begin with modifications of Lemma
5.10
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, and let
\rho
be a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
. Then,
C(\ker\rho)=\{\ker\pi_{\rho}^{*}(\omega_{x,y}|\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}\}
.

Proof.

Recall that
C(\ker\rho)=\{\ker\rho_{x,y}:x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}\}
by Lemma
5.10
. Since
\rho_{x,y}=\omega_{x,y}\circ\pi_{\rho}=\pi_{\rho}^{*}(\omega_{x,y}|\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}))
, it follows that
C(\ker\rho)=\{\ker\pi_{\rho}^{*}(\omega_{x,y}|\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}\}
.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space. Then,
\omega_{x,y}=\|x\|\|y\|\omega_{\|x\|^{-1}x,\|y\|^{-1}y}
and
\ker\omega_{x,y}=\ker\omega_{\|x\|^{-1}x,\|y\|^{-1}y}
whenever
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. Hence, the condition
x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}
is not essential in the preceding lemma, that is, the identity

\{\ker\pi_{\rho}^{*}(\omega_{x,y}|\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}\}=\{\ker\pi_{\rho}^{*}(\omega_{x,y}|\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})):x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\setminus\{0\}\}

holds.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be an irreducible
C^{*}
algebra acting on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Suppose that
x_{0},y_{0}\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. Then,
C(\ker\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A})=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}\}
.

Proof.

It may be assumed that
x_{0},y_{0}\in S_{\mathcal{H}}
. Then,
\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}},\omega_{x_{0},x_{0}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
by Corollary
5.19
. Moreover, since
\mathfrak{A}
acts irreducibly on
\mathcal{H}
, we have a unitary element
U\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}
such that
U^{*}y_{0}=x_{0}
, that is,
\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}}U=\omega_{x_{0},x_{0}}
. This means that
|\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}}|\mathfrak{A}|=\omega_{x_{0},x_{0}}|\mathfrak{A}
, and
C(\ker\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A})=\ker(\omega_{x_{0},x_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A})
by Theorem
5.8
. Set
\rho=\omega_{x_{0},x_{0}}|\mathfrak{A}
for short. Since
\rho
is a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
, by Lemma
6.1
, we obtain

C(\ker\omega_{x_{0},x_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A})=\{\ker\pi_{\rho}^{*}(\omega_{z,w}|\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})):z,w\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\setminus\{0\}\}.

Now, let
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. By the irreducibility of
\mathfrak{A}
, there exist
V_{1},V_{2}\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
V_{1}x_{0}=x
and
V_{2}y_{0}=y
. It follows from
\omega_{x,y}\neq 0
and

\displaystyle\omega_{x,y}(A)=\omega_{x_{0},x_{0}}(V_{2}^{*}AV_{1})

\displaystyle=\langle\pi_{\rho}(A)\pi_{\rho}(V_{1})x_{\rho},\pi_{\rho}(V_{2})x_{\rho}\rangle

\displaystyle=(\pi_{\rho}^{*}(\omega_{\pi_{\rho}(V_{1})x_{\rho},\pi_{\rho}(V_{2})x_{\rho}}|\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}))(A)

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
that
\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in C(\ker\omega_{x_{0},x_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A})
. Conversely, if
z,w\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\setminus\{0\}
, then the irreducibility of
\pi_{\rho}
ensures that
\pi_{\rho}(W_{1})x_{\rho}=z
and
\pi_{\rho}(W_{2})x_{\rho}=w
, which implies that

\displaystyle(\pi_{\rho}^{*}(\omega_{z,w}|\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})))(A)=\langle\pi_{\rho}(A)z,w\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\pi_{\rho}(W_{2}^{*}AW_{1})z,w\rangle

\displaystyle=\omega_{x_{0},x_{0}}(W_{2}^{*}AW_{1})

\displaystyle=\omega_{W_{1}x_{0},W_{2}x_{0}}(A)

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. In particular,
W_{1}x_{0}\neq 0
and
W_{2}x_{0}\neq 0
. Therefore, the identity

C(\ker\omega_{x_{0},x_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A})=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}\}

holds.
∎

The equivalence class of a pure vector state of an irreducible
C^{*}
-algebra plays important roles in the rest of this paper. The following definition assigns a special symbol to it.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be an irreducible
C^{*}
-algebra acting on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Then, the equivalence class
\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}\}
in
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
is called the
normal part
and denoted by
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
.

In the rest of this section, we characterize (continuous) finite-homeomorphism between normal parts of geometric structure spaces of irreducible
C^{*}
-algebras. First, we give a wide class of such mappings. To this end, some preliminary works are needed.

Remark 6.5
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space, and let
H\in B(\mathcal{H})
be such that
0\leq H\leq I
. Then, there exists a sequence of projections
(R_{n}(H))_{n}
with the following properties:

(i)

HR_{n}(H)=R_{n}(H)H\geq n^{-1}R_{n}(H)
for each
n
.

(ii)

R_{n}(H)\leq R(H)
for each
n
, where
R(H)
is the range projection of
H
.

(iii)

The sequence
(R_{n}(H))_{n}
converges to
R(H)
with respect to the strong-operator topology.

This is an elementary application of functional calculus. For each
k\in(0,1)
, let
f_{k}
be an element of
C(\mathop{\mathrm{sp}}\nolimits(H))
defined by

f_{k}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&(t\in[0,k])\\
(t-k)/(1-k)&(t\in[k,1])\end{array}\right..

Then, we obtain
tf_{k}(t)^{1/n}\geq kf_{k}(t)^{1/n}
,

t(1-f_{k}(t)^{1/n})\leq\max\{(1-k)k+k,1-f_{k}((1-k)k+k)^{1/n}\}

and
(1-t)f(t)^{1/n}\leq 1-k
for each
t\in[0,1]
and each
n
, which implies that
Hf_{k}(H)^{1/n}\geq kf_{k}(H)^{1/n}
,
H(I-f_{k}(H)^{1/n})\leq((1-k)k+k)I
and
(I-H)f_{k}(H)^{1/n}\leq(1-k)I
for sufficiently large
n
. Since
(f_{k}(H)^{1/n})_{n}
converges to the range projection
E_{k}
of
f_{k}(H)
with respect to the strong-operator topology, it follows that
HE_{k}=E_{k}H\geq kE_{k}
,
0\leq H(I-E_{k})\leq((1-k)k+k)I
and
0\leq(I-H)E_{k}\leq(1-k)I
. In particular, we have
\|H-HE_{k}\|\leq(1-k)k+k
and
\|E_{k}-E_{k}H\|\leq 1-k
. Here, we note that
E_{k}\leq R(H)
for each
k\in(0,1)
since
f_{k}(t)\leq t
for each
t\in[0,1]
and
(H^{1/n})_{n}
converges to
R(H)
with respect to the strong-operator topology. Moreover, if
x\in R(H)(\mathcal{H})
, and if
\varepsilon>0
, then
\|x-Hy\|<\varepsilon/3
for some
y\in\mathcal{H}
. It follows from

\displaystyle\|E_{k}x-R(H)x\|

\displaystyle\leq\|E_{k}x-E_{k}Hy\|+\|E_{k}Hy-Hy\|+\|Hy-R(H)x\|

\displaystyle\leq\|x-Hy\|+\|E_{k}H-H\|\|y\|+\|R(H)Hy-R(H)x\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|Hy-x\|+((1-k)k+k)\|y\|

\displaystyle<2\varepsilon/3+(2k-k^{2})\|y\|

that
\|E_{k}x-R(H)x\|<\varepsilon
for sufficiently small
k
. This shows that
\lim_{k\to 0}\|E_{k}x-R(H)x\|=0
. The desired sequence is then given by setting
R_{n}(H)=E_{n^{-1}}
for each
n
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space, and let
A,B\in B(\mathcal{H})
. If
\langle Bx,y\rangle=0
whenever
\langle Ax,y\rangle=0
, then
B=cA
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

We only consider the case
A\neq 0
since
A=0
implies
B=0
from the assumption. It may be assumed that
\|A\|=1
. Suppose that
\langle Bx,y\rangle=0
whenever
\langle Ax,y\rangle=0
. Let
A=U|A|=|A^{*}|U
be the polar decomposition of
A
, where
U
is the partial isometry with
U^{*}U=R(|A|)
and
UU^{*}=R(|A^{*}|)=R(A)
. Since
|A|=|A|R(|A|)
and
|A^{*}|=|A^{*}|R(|A^{*}|)
, it follows that

\langle A(I-R(|A|))x,y\rangle=\langle U|A|(I-R(|A|))x,y\rangle=0

and

\langle Ax,(I-R(|A^{*}|))y\rangle=\langle|A^{*}|Ux,(I-R(|A^{*}|))y\rangle=0

for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}
, which together with the assumption implies that

\langle B(I-R(|A|))x,y\rangle=\langle Bx,(I-R(|A^{*}|))y\rangle=0,

for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}
. Hence,
B=R(|A^{*}|)BR(|A|)
.

Next, recall that
|A|R_{n}(|A|)=R_{n}(|A|)|A|\geq n^{-1}R_{n}(|A|)
, which implies that
|A|R_{n}(|A|)
is invertible in
R_{n}(|A|)B(\mathcal{H})R_{n}(|A|)
. Let
C_{n}\in R_{n}(|A|)B(\mathcal{H})R_{n}(|A|)
be such that
C_{n}|A|R_{n}(|A|)=|A|R_{n}(|A|)C_{n}=R_{n}(|A|)
, and let
D_{n}=C_{n}U^{*}=R_{n}(|A|)C_{n}R_{n}(|A|)U^{*}
. We note that
D_{n}AR_{n}(|A|)=R_{n}(|A|)
and
AD_{n}=UR_{n}(|A|)U^{*}
. In particular,
AD_{n}
is the final projection of the partial isometry
UR_{n}(|A|)
. Now, suppose that
x,y\in(AD_{n})(\mathcal{H})
and
\langle x,y\rangle=0
. Then, it turns out from

0=\langle x,y\rangle=\langle AD_{n}x,y\rangle

that
\langle BD_{n}x,y\rangle=0
. In particular, if
x,y\in(AD_{n})(\mathcal{H})
,
\|x\|=\|y\|=1
and
\langle x,y\rangle=0
. Then,

\langle x,y\rangle=\langle y,x\rangle=\langle x+y,x-y\rangle=0,

which implies that

\langle BD_{n}x,y\rangle=\langle BD_{n}y,x\rangle=\langle BD_{n}(x+y),x-y\rangle=0.

We obtain
\langle BD_{n}x,x\rangle=\langle BD_{n}y,y\rangle
by

0=\langle BD_{n}(x+y),x-y\rangle=\langle BD_{n}x,x\rangle-\langle BD_{n}y,y\rangle.

Now, let
(e_{\lambda})_{\lambda}
be an orthonormal basis for
(AD_{n})(\mathcal{H})
. Then, we have

\langle(BD_{n}-\langle BD_{n}e_{\lambda_{0}},e_{\lambda_{0}}\rangle AD_{n})e_{\lambda},e_{\lambda}\rangle=\langle BDe_{\lambda},e_{\lambda}\rangle-\langle BD_{n}e_{\lambda_{0}},e_{\lambda_{0}}\rangle=0

for a fixed
\lambda_{0}
. Moreover, we obtain
\langle BD_{n}e_{\lambda},e_{\mu}\rangle=0
and

\langle(BD_{n}-\langle BD_{n}e_{\lambda_{0}},e_{\lambda_{0}}\rangle AD_{n})e_{\lambda},e_{\mu}\rangle=\langle BD_{n}e_{\lambda},e_{\mu}\rangle-\langle BD_{n}e_{\lambda_{0}},e_{\lambda_{0}}\rangle\langle e_{\lambda},e_{\mu}\rangle=0

whenever
\lambda\neq\mu
. Therefore,
AD_{n}BD_{n}AD_{n}=\langle BD_{n}e_{\lambda_{0}},e_{\lambda_{0}}\rangle AD_{n}
in
B(\mathcal{H})
and

\displaystyle AD_{n}BR_{n}(|A|)=(AD_{n}BD_{n}AD_{n})AR_{n}(|A|)

\displaystyle=(\langle BD_{n}e_{\lambda_{0}},e_{\lambda_{0}}\rangle AD_{n})AR_{n}(|A|)

\displaystyle=\langle BD_{n}e_{\lambda_{0}},e_{\lambda_{0}}\rangle AD_{n}AR_{n}(|A|).

Set
c_{n}=\langle BD_{n}e_{\lambda_{0}},e_{\lambda_{0}}\rangle
for each
n
. Since
(R_{n}(|A|))_{n}
converges to
R(|A|)=U^{*}U
with respect to the strong-operator topology, it follows that
\lim_{n}\|R_{n}(|A|)x-R(|A|)x\|=0
and
\lim_{n}\|AD_{n}y-R(|A^{*}|)y\|=0
for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}
. In particular, if
Ax_{0}\neq 0
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{n}\langle AD_{n}AR_{n}(|A|)x_{0},Ax_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{n}\langle AR_{n}(|A|)x_{0},AD_{n}Ax_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle AR(|A|)x_{0},R(|A^{*}|)Ax_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\|Ax_{0}\|^{2}

\displaystyle>0

which together with
\|AD_{n}\|\leq 1
and
R_{n}(|A|)\leq 1
implies that

\displaystyle|c_{n}|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\langle AD_{n}BR_{n}(|A|)x_{0},Ax_{0}\rangle}{\langle AD_{n}AR_{n}(|A|)x_{0},Ax_{0}\rangle}\right|\leq\frac{2\|B\|\|x_{0}\|}{\|Ax_{0}\|}

for sufficiently large
n
. Hence, the sequence
(c_{n})_{n}
is bounded. Taking a subsequence if necessary, we may assume that
(c_{n})_{n}
converges to some
c\in\mathbb{C}
. It follows that

\displaystyle\langle BR(|A|)x,R(|A^{*}|)y\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{n}\langle BR_{n}(|A|)x,AD_{n}y\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{n}c_{n}\langle AR_{n}(|A|)x,AD_{n}y\rangle

\displaystyle=c\langle AR(|A|)x,R(|A^{*}|)y\rangle

for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}
, that is,

B=R(|A^{*}|)BR(|A|)=cR(|A^{*}|)AR(|A|)=cA.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be an irreducible
C^{*}
-algebra acting on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Suppose that
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
. Then,
\sum_{j=1}^{n}\omega_{x_{j},y_{j}}=0
(on
\mathfrak{A}
) if and only if there exists an
n\times n
matrix
C=[c_{jk}]
such that
\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{c_{jk}}x_{j}=0
for each
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{jk}y_{k}=y_{j}
for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Proof.

The proof is almost the same as that of
[
23
, Proposition 2.6.6]
. If the matrix
[c_{jk}]
with the stated properties exists, then it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\omega_{x_{j},y_{j}}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\overline{c_{jk}}\omega_{x_{j},y_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{c_{jk}}\omega_{x_{j},y_{k}}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega_{0,y_{k}}=0.

For the converse, suppose that
\sum_{j=1}^{n}\omega_{x_{j},y_{j}}=0
on
\mathfrak{A}
. If
y_{1}=\cdots=y_{n}=0
, then
C=0
has the desired properties. We assume that
\langle\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\}\rangle\neq\{0\}
. Let
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{r}\}
be an orthonormal basis for
\langle\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\}\rangle
. Then, there exist
n\times r
matrix
A=[a_{jl}]
and
r\times n
matrix
B=[b_{lk}]
such that
y_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{ji}e_{i}
for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
e_{l}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}b_{lk}y_{k}
for each
l\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
. Set
C=AB
. Then,
C
is an
n\times n
matrix and

y_{j}=\sum_{l=1}^{r}a_{jl}\sum_{k=1}^{n}b_{lk}y_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{l=1}^{r}a_{jl}b_{lk}\right)y_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{jk}y_{k}

for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Moreover, setting
z_{l}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{a_{jl}}x_{j}
for each
l\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, we have

\displaystyle 0=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\omega_{x_{j},y_{j}}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{r}\overline{a_{jl}}\omega_{x_{j},e_{l}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{r}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{a_{jl}}\omega_{x_{j},e_{l}}=\sum_{l=1}^{r}\omega_{z_{l},e_{l}}.

Since
\mathfrak{A}
acts irreducibly on
\mathcal{H}
, there exists an element
U
of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
Ue_{l}=z_{l}
for each
l\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
. It follows that

0=\sum_{l=1}^{r}\omega_{z_{l},e_{l}}(U^{*})=\sum_{l=1}^{r}\|z_{l}\|^{2}.

Therefore,
z_{1}=\cdots=z_{r}=0
and

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{c_{jk}}x_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{r}\overline{a_{jl}b_{lk}}x_{j}=\sum_{l=1}^{r}\overline{b_{lk}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{a_{jl}}x_{j}\\
=\sum_{l=1}^{r}\overline{b_{lk}}z_{l}=0

for each
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
∎

Remark 6.8
.

Let
X
and
Y
be vector spaces over the scalar field
\mathbb{K}
, respectively. Suppose that
\sigma
is a field automorphism on
\mathbb{K}
. Then, a mapping
A:X\to Y
is said to be
\sigma
-semilinear
if it satisfies
T(x+y)=Tx+Ty
and
T(cx)=\sigma(c)Tx
for each
x,y\in X
and each
c\in\mathbb{K}
. If
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
, then the identity mapping on
\mathbb{R}
is a unique field automorphism on
\mathbb{R}
. However, in the complex case, there are many other situations. If
\sigma
is the complex conjugation on
\mathbb{C}
, then a
\sigma
-similinear mapping are called
conjugate-linear operator
. The identity mapping and complex conjugation on
\mathbb{C}
are characterized as continuous field automorphism on
\mathbb{C}
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
be Hilbert spaces, and let
A:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{K}
be a continuous conjugate-linear. Then,
A:\mathcal{H}\to\overline{\mathcal{K}}
and
A:\overline{\mathcal{H}}\to\mathcal{K}
can be viewed as bounded linear operators, and hence, the adjoint
A^{*}
of
A
exists as a bounded conjugate-linear operator from
\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{K}
.

Now, we give a wide class of (continuous) finite-homeomorphisms between normal parts of geometric structure spaces of irreducible
C^{*}
-algebras.

Theorem 6.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be irreducible
C^{*}
-algebras acting on Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
. Suppose that either of the following holds:

(i)

U
and
V
are linear bijection from
\mathcal{H}
onto
\mathcal{K}
.

(ii)

U
and
V
are conjugate-linear bijection from
\mathcal{H}
onto
\mathcal{K}
.

Define a mapping
\Phi:\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})\to\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})
by

\Phi(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A})=\ker\omega_{Ux,Vy}\cap\mathfrak{B}

for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. Then,
\Phi
is a finite-homeomorphism. Moreover, provided that
U
and
V
are bounded,
\Phi
is continuous if and only if
V^{*}\mathfrak{B}U\subset\mathfrak{A}
, and
\Phi
is a homeomorphism if and only if
V^{*}\mathfrak{B}U=\mathfrak{A}
.

Proof.

Since
U
and
V
are semilinear, it is straightforward to check that
\Phi
is well-defined. Moreover, setting
\Psi(\ker\omega_{z,w}\cap\mathfrak{B})=\ker\omega_{U^{-1}z,V^{-1}w}\cap\mathfrak{A}
for each
z,w\in\mathcal{K}
yields the inverse mapping of
\Phi
. In particular,
\Phi
is bijective. Suppose that
x,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
, and that

\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in\{\ker\omega_{x_{j},y_{j}}\cap\mathfrak{A}:j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\}^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A}).

Then,
\bigcap_{j=1}^{n}(\ker\omega_{x_{j},y_{j}}\cap\mathfrak{A})\subset\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}
and
\omega_{x,y}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_{j}\omega_{x_{j},y_{j}}
for some
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
. Hence, setting
z_{j}=c_{j}x_{j}
for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
z_{n+1}=-x
and
y_{n+1}=y
, we have
\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\omega_{z_{j},y_{j}}=0
. It follows from Lemma
6.7
that there exists an
(n+1)\times(n+1)
matrix
[c_{jk}]
such that
\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\overline{c_{jk}}z_{j}=0
for each
k\in\{1,\ldots,n+1\}
and
\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}c_{jk}y_{k}=y_{j}
for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,n+1\}
. If
U
and
V
are linear, then
\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\overline{c_{jk}}Uz_{j}=0
and
\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}c_{jk}Vy_{k}=Vy_{j}
, while if
U
and
V
are conjugate-linear, then
\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}c_{jk}Uz_{j}=0
and
\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\overline{c_{jk}}Vy_{k}=Vy_{j}
. Therefore, in either case,
\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\omega_{Uz_{j},Vy_{j}}=0
by Lemma
6.7
, which implies that
\omega_{Ux,Vy}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\omega_{U(c_{j}x_{j}),Vy_{j}}
. This shows that
\bigcap_{j=1}^{n}(\ker\omega_{Ux_{j},Vy_{j}}\cap\mathfrak{B})\subset\ker\omega_{Ux,Vy}\cap\mathfrak{B}
and

\ker\omega_{Ux,Vy}\cap\mathfrak{B}\in\{\ker\omega_{Ux_{j},Vy_{j}}\cap\mathfrak{B}:j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\}^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B}),

that is,
\Psi
is finitely continuous. Applying the same argument as above to
\Psi^{-1}
, we can conclude that
\Psi
is a finite-homeomorphism.

In the rest of this proof, we assume that
U
and
V
are bounded. We note that
U^{-1}
and
V^{-1}
are also bounded by the open mapping theorem. Suppose that
\Phi
is continuous, and that
B\in\mathfrak{B}\setminus\{0\}
. Set

S(B)=\{\ker\omega_{z,w}\cap\mathfrak{B}\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B}):B\in\ker\omega_{z,w}\cap B\}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B}).

Then,
S(B)^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})=S(B)
and
S(B)\neq\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})
. Indeed, if
\ker\omega_{z_{0},w_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{B}\in S(B)^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})
, then

B\in\bigcap\{\ker\omega_{z,w}\cap\mathfrak{B}\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B}):B\in\ker\omega_{z,w}\cap\mathfrak{B}\}\subset\ker\omega_{z_{0},w_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{B},

that is,
\ker\omega_{z_{0},w_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{B}\in S(B)
. Moreover, we realize that
S(B)\neq\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})
by
\ker\omega_{z,Bz}\cap\mathfrak{B}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})\setminus S(B)
whenever
Bz\neq 0
. Now, by the continuity of
\Phi
, we obtain

\Phi(S^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A}))\subset\Phi(S)^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})

for each
S\subset\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
. In particular,
\Phi^{-1}(S(B))^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})=\Phi^{-1}(S(B))\neq\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
. It follows that
\Phi^{-1}(S(B))^{=}\neq\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
, which together with
[
44
, Lemma 4.1]
implies that there exists an
A\in\mathfrak{A}\setminus\{0\}
such that

A\in\bigcap\{\Phi^{-1}(\ker\omega_{z,w}\cap\mathfrak{B}):z,w\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{0\},~\langle Bz,w\rangle=0\}.

Since
\Phi^{-1}=\Psi
, we have
\langle AU^{-1}z,V^{-1}w\rangle=0
whenever
\langle Bz,w\rangle=0
. We note that
(V^{-1})^{*}AU^{-1}\in B(\mathcal{K})
even if
U
and
V
are both conjugate-linear. Therefore, Lemma
6.6
ensures that
(V^{-1})^{*}AU^{-1}=cB
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
, that is,
V^{*}BU=c^{-1}A\in\mathfrak{A}
. This shows that
V^{*}\mathfrak{B}U\subset\mathfrak{A}
.

Conversely, suppose that
V^{*}\mathfrak{B}U\subset\mathfrak{A}
. Let
S\subset\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
, and let
\ker\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in S^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
. If

B\in\bigcap\{\Phi(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}):\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in S\}

and
\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in S
, then
\langle V^{*}BUx,y\rangle=\langle BUx,Vy\rangle=0
, which together with
V^{*}BU\in V^{*}\mathfrak{B}U\subset\mathfrak{A}
implies that
V^{*}BU\in\bigcap\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in S\}
. Since
\ker\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in S^{=}
, it follows that
\langle BUx_{0},Vy_{0}\rangle=\langle V^{*}BUx_{0},y_{0}\rangle=0
and
B\in\ker\omega_{Ux_{0},Vy_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{B}=\Phi(\ker\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A})
. Hence,

\bigcap\{\Phi(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}):\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in S\}\subset\Phi(\ker\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}),

that is,
\Phi(\ker\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A})\in\Phi(S)^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})
. This proves that
\Phi(S^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A}))\subset\Phi(S)^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})
. Thus,
\Phi
is continuous.

Finally, we note that
\Phi
is a homeomorphism if and only if both
\Phi
and
\Psi
are continuous, which occurs if and only if
V^{*}\mathfrak{B}U\subset\mathfrak{A}
and
(V^{-1})^{*}\mathfrak{A}U^{-1}\subset\mathfrak{B}
, that is,
V^{*}\mathfrak{B}U=\mathfrak{A}
.
∎

The preceding theorem gives typical examples of (continuous) finite-homeomorphisms between
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})
for irreducible
C^{*}
-algebras
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
. Our next task is to show that continuous finite-homeomorphisms between
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})
are limited to typical ones at least in the infinite-dimensional case. To this end, we consider the relation “
\rightleftarrows
” in a local setting. Then, as will be shown in the following lemma, it is well-behaved in the context of normal parts of geometric structure spaces of irreducible
C^{*}
-algebras.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be an irreducible
C^{*}
-algebra acting on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
that acts irreducibly on
\mathcal{H}
. Suppose that
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. Then,
\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\rightleftarrows\ker\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}}\cap\mathfrak{A}
in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
if and only if either
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
or
\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
is linearly dependent.

Proof.

First, we note that
\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A}=\ker\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}}\cap\mathfrak{A}
if and only if both
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
and
\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
are linearly dependent. Indeed, if
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
is linearly independent, then there exists an
A\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
Ax_{1}=0
and
Ax_{2}=y_{2}
, in which case,
\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}(A)=0
and
\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}}(A)=1
. Therefore,
\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\neq\ker\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}}\cap\mathfrak{A}
. An argument similar to above also works in the case that
\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
is linearly independent. Conversely, if both
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
and
\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
are linearly dependent, then it is obvious that
\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A}=\ker\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}}\cap\mathfrak{A}
.

Now, suppose that
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
is linearly dependent and
\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
is linearly independent. Then,
\ker\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}}\cap\mathfrak{A}=\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{2}}\cap\mathfrak{A}
, and

\ker\omega_{x_{1},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in\{\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A},\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{2}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\}^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})

whenever
y\in\langle\{y_{1},y_{2}\}\rangle\setminus\{0\}
. Hence,

\{\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A},\ker\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\}^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})\neq\{\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A},\ker\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\},

that is,
\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\rightleftarrows\ker\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}}\cap\mathfrak{A}
in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
. Similarly, we have the same consequence as above in the other case.

For the converse, suppose that both
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
and
\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
are linearly independent. In this case,
\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\neq\ker\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}}\cap\mathfrak{A}
. It may be assumed that
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\in S_{\mathcal{H}}
. Let
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
be such that

\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in\{\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A},\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\}^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A}).

Then, we have
\omega_{x,y}|\mathfrak{A}=(c_{1}\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}+c_{2}\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}})|\mathfrak{A}
for some scalars
c_{1},c_{2}
. If
\{x_{1},x_{2},x\}
is linearly independent, then there exists an element
B\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
Bx_{j}=0
for
j=1,2
and
Bx=y
. It follows that

\|y\|^{2}=\omega_{x,y}(B)=c_{1}\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}(B)+c_{2}\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}}(B)=0,

which contradicts
y\neq 0
. Hence,
\{x_{1},x_{2},x\}
is linearly dependent. By an argument similar to above, we also derive that
\{y_{1},y_{2},y\}
is linearly dependent. Therefore, we have
x=a_{1}x_{1}+a_{2}x_{2}
and
y=b_{1}y_{1}+b_{2}y_{2}
for some scalars
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2}
. It follows that

\displaystyle(c_{1}\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}+c_{2}\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}})|\mathfrak{A}

\displaystyle=\omega_{x,y}|\mathfrak{A}

\displaystyle=(a_{1}b_{1}\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}+a_{1}b_{2}\omega_{x_{1},y_{2}}+a_{2}b_{1}\omega_{x_{2},y_{1}}+a_{2}b_{2}\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}})|\mathfrak{A}.

Meanwhile, since
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
is linearly independent, there exist
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
A_{1}x_{1}=0
,
A_{1}x_{2}=y_{1}-\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle y_{2}
,
A_{2}x_{1}=y_{2}-\langle y_{2},y_{1}\rangle y_{1}
and
A_{2}x_{2}=0
. We note that

\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}(A_{1})=\omega_{x_{1},y_{2}}(A_{1})=\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}}(A_{1})=\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}(A_{2})=\omega_{x_{2},y_{1}}(A_{2})=\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}}(A_{2})=0,

while

\omega_{x_{2},y_{1}}(A_{1})=\omega_{x_{1},y_{2}}(A_{2})=1-|\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle|^{2}>0

since
\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
is linearly independent. Therefore,
a_{2}b_{1}=a_{1}b_{2}=0
by

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(a_{1}b_{1}\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}+a_{1}b_{2}\omega_{x_{1},y_{2}}+a_{2}b_{1}\omega_{x_{2},y_{1}}+a_{2}b_{2}\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}})(A_{1})

\displaystyle=(a_{1}b_{1}\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}+a_{1}b_{2}\omega_{x_{1},y_{2}}+a_{2}b_{1}\omega_{x_{2},y_{1}}+a_{2}b_{2}\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}})(A_{2}).

Combining this with
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
, we obtain
(x,y)=(a_{1}x_{1},b_{1}y_{1})
or
(x,y)=(a_{2}x_{2},b_{2}y_{2})
, which implies that

\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in\{\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A},\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\}.

Thus,

\{\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A},\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\}^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})=\{\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A},\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\}.

This shows that
\ker\omega_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\not\rightleftarrows\ker\omega_{x_{2},y_{2}}\cap\mathfrak{A}
in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
.
∎

In the rest of this section, certain subsets of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
play important roles. We formulate them as follows.

Definition 6.11
.

Let
(K,c)
be a close space, and let
S\subset K
. Then,
S
is called an
R
-set
if
t_{1}\rightleftarrows t_{2}
whenever
t_{1},t_{2}\in S
.

It can be shown that
R
-sets are preserved under finite-homeomorphisms.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
(K,c)
and
(L,d)
be closure spaces, and let
f:K\to L
be a finite-homeomorphism. Suppose that
S\subset K
. Then,
S
is an
R
-set if and only if
f(S)
is.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.28
, for
t_{1},t_{2}\in K
, we have
t_{1}\rightleftarrows t_{2}
if and only if
f(t_{1})\rightleftarrows f(t_{2})
. If
S
is an
R
-set, and if
t_{1},t_{2}\in S
, then
t_{1}\rightleftarrows t_{2}
, which implies that
f(t_{1})\rightleftarrows f(t_{2})
. This shows that
f(S)
is also an
R
-set. Applying the same argument as above to
f^{-1}
, we infer that
S
is an
R
-set if
f(S)
is.
∎

In the following lemma, for an irreducible
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, we identify
R
-sets in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
maximal with respect to inclusion.

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be an irreducible
C^{*}
-algebra acting on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Suppose that
S\subset\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
. Then,
S
is an
R
-set in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
maximal with respect to inclusion if and only if either of the following holds:

(i)

There exists an
x_{0}\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
such that
S=\{\ker\omega_{x_{0},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}\}
.

(ii)

There exists a
y_{0}\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
such that
S=\{\ker\omega_{x,y_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}:x\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}\}
.

Proof.

The lemma is obvious if
\dim\mathcal{H}=1
. We assume that
\dim\mathcal{H}\geq 2
. Suppose that
S
is an
R
-set in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
maximal with respect to inclusion. Then,
S
contains at least two distinct elements. Indeed since each singleton is an
R
-set, it follows that
S\neq\emptyset
. Let
\ker\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in S
, and let
y_{0}^{\perp}\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
be such that
\langle y_{0},y_{0}^{\perp}\rangle=0
. Then,
\ker\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\neq\ker\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}^{\perp}}\cap\mathfrak{A}
and
\{\ker\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A},\ker\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}^{\perp}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\}
is an
R
-set in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
by Lemma
6.10
. Hence,
S\neq\{\ker\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\}
by the maximality of
S
. Let
x^{\prime}_{0},y^{\prime}_{0}\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
be such that
\ker\omega_{x^{\prime}_{0},y^{\prime}_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in S
and
\ker\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\neq\ker\omega_{x^{\prime}_{0},y^{\prime}_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}
. By Lemma
6.10
, either
\{x_{0},x^{\prime}_{0}\}
or
\{y_{0},y^{\prime}_{0}\}
is linearly dependent.

Suppose that
\{x_{0},x^{\prime}_{0}\}
is linearly dependent. Then,
\ker\omega_{x^{\prime}_{0},y^{\prime}_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}=\ker\omega_{x_{0},y^{\prime}_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}
and
\{y_{0},y^{\prime}_{0}\}
is linearly independent by
\ker\omega_{x_{0},y_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\neq\ker\omega_{x^{\prime}_{0},y^{\prime}_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}
. Let
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
be such that
\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in S
. If
\{x_{0},x\}
is linearly independent, then
\{y_{0},y\}
is linearly dependent by Lemma
6.10
, which implies that
\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}=\ker\omega_{x,y_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}
. However, then
\ker\omega_{x_{0},y^{\prime}_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\not\rightleftarrows\ker\omega_{x,y_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}
in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
again by Lemma
6.10
. This contradicts the assumption that
S
is an
R
-set. Therefore,
\{x_{0},x\}
must be linearly dependent, that is,
\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}=\ker\omega_{x_{0},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}
. This shows that
S\subset\{\ker\omega_{x_{0},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}\}
. Since
\{\ker\omega_{x_{0},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:y\in S_{\mathcal{H}}\}
is itself an
R
-set in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
by Lemma
6.10
, we obtain
S=\{\ker\omega_{x_{0},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}\}
by the maximality of
S
.

If
\{y_{0},y_{0}^{\prime}\}
is linearly dependent, then an argument similar to that in the preceding paragraph shows that
S=\{\ker\omega_{x,y_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{A}:x\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}\}
.

Conversely, let
S=\{\ker\omega_{x_{0},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}\}
, and let
S^{\prime}
be an
R
-set in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
that contains
S
. Take an arbitrary pair
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
with
\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle=0
. Then,
\ker\omega_{x_{0},y_{j}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in S\subset S^{\prime}
for
j=1,2
. If
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
satisfies
\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in S^{\prime}
, then
\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\rightleftarrows\ker\omega_{x_{0},y_{j}}\cap\mathfrak{A}
in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
for
j=1,2
. If
\{x_{0},x\}
is linearly independent, then Lemma
6.10
ensures that
\{y,y_{j}\}
is linearly dependent for
j=1,2
. However, this is impossible since
\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
is linearly independent. Thus,
\{x_{0},x\}
must be linearly dependent, in which case,
\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}=\ker\omega_{x_{0},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in S
. This proves the maximality of
S
. An argument similar to that in above also works in the other case.
∎

For each nonzero elements
x
of a Banach space, let
\overline{x}
denote the one-dimensional subspace generated by
x
. Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space, and let
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. Then, we note that
\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}=\ker\omega_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}\cap\mathfrak{A}
whenever
x^{\prime}\in\overline{x}\setminus\{0\}
and
y^{\prime}\in\overline{y}\setminus\{0\}
. Hence, we have

\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}\}=\{\ker\omega_{x^{\prime},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}\}

whenever
\overline{x}=\overline{x^{\prime}}
, and

\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}\}=\{\ker\omega_{x,y^{\prime}}\cap\mathfrak{A}:y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}\}

whenever
\overline{y}=\overline{y^{\prime}}
. Based on this observation, we assign special symbols to these sets.

Definition 6.14
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be an irreducible
C^{*}
-algebra acting on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Then, let

\displaystyle R(\overline{x},\mathcal{H})

\displaystyle=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}\}

\displaystyle R(\mathcal{H},\overline{y})

\displaystyle=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:x\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}\}

for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
.

We need a partial extension of Lemma
6.6
.

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space, and let
A\in B(\mathcal{H})
be a positive operator with
\dim A(\mathcal{H})\geq 2
. Suppose that
B:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
is a bounded linear or conjugate linear operator such that
\langle Bx,y\rangle=0
whenever
\langle Ax,y\rangle=0
and
\langle Bx_{0},x_{0}\rangle\neq 0
for some
x_{0}\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. Then,
B=cA
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.6
, it is sufficient to show that
B
is linear. We note that
\langle Ax_{0},x_{0}\rangle>0
from the assumption. Since
A(\mathcal{H})\subset A^{1/2}(\mathcal{H})
, there exists an element
y_{0}\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
such that
A^{1/2}y_{0}\neq 0
and
\langle A^{1/2}x_{0},A^{1/2}y_{0}\rangle=0
. Namely, we have
x_{0},y_{0}\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
such that
\langle Ax_{0},x_{0}\rangle\langle Ay_{0},y_{0}\rangle>0
,
\langle Ax_{0},y_{0}\rangle=0
and
\langle Bx_{0},x_{0}\rangle\neq 0
.

Let
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
and

c=\sqrt{\frac{\langle Ax_{0},x_{0}\rangle}{\langle Ay_{0},y_{0}\rangle}}>0.

We note that
\langle Ax_{0},y_{0}\rangle=\langle Ay_{0},x_{0}\rangle=0
, which implies that

\langle A(x_{0}+zcy_{0}),x_{0}-\overline{z^{-1}}cy_{0}\rangle=\langle Ax_{0},x_{0}\rangle-c^{2}\langle Ay_{0},y_{0}\rangle=0.

In particular,
\langle A(x_{0}+cy_{0}),x_{0}-cy_{0}\rangle=0
. Combining this with
\langle A(cy_{0}),x_{0}\rangle=\langle A(zcy_{0}),x_{0}\rangle=0
, we obtain
\langle Bx_{0},y_{0}\rangle=\langle B(cy_{0}),x_{0}\rangle=\langle B(zcy_{0}),x_{0}\rangle=0
and

\langle B(x_{0}+cy_{0}),x_{0}-cy_{0}\rangle=\langle B(x_{0}+zcy_{0}),x_{0}-\overline{z^{-1}}cy_{0}\rangle=0.

It follows that

0=\langle B(x_{0}+cy_{0}),x_{0}-cy_{0}\rangle=\langle Bx_{0},x_{0}\rangle-\langle B(cy_{0}),cy_{0}\rangle

and

0=\langle B(x_{0}+zcy_{0}),x_{0}-\overline{z^{-1}}cy_{0}\rangle=\langle Bx_{0},x_{0}\rangle-\langle B(zcy_{0}),\overline{z^{-1}}cy_{0}\rangle,

that is,

\langle B(cy_{0}),cy_{0}\rangle=\langle Bx_{0},x_{0}\rangle=\langle B(zcy_{0}),\overline{z^{-1}}cy_{0}\rangle=\sigma(z)z^{-1}\langle B(cy_{0}),cy_{0}\rangle.

We note that
\langle B(cy_{0}),cy_{0}\rangle=\langle Bx_{0},x_{0}\rangle\neq 0
. Therefore,
\sigma(z)=z
for each
z\in\mathbb{C}
. This proves that
B
is linear.
∎

We present a useful tool for simplifying our argument on (finite-)homeomorphisms between (normal parts of) geometric structure spaces of irreducible
C^{*}
-algebras.

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, and let
\Phi^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a}(\ker\rho)=\ker\rho^{*}
for each
\ker\rho\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
. Then,
\Phi^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a}
is a homeomorphism on
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
with
(\Phi^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a})^{-1}=\Phi^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a}
.

Proof.

Since the mapping
\rho\mapsto\rho^{*}
is a conjugate-linear isometric isomorphism on
\mathfrak{A}^{*}
, it follows that
\ker\rho^{*}\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
whenever
\ker\rho\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
. Moreover, we have

\ker\rho^{*}=\{A\in\mathfrak{A}:\rho(A^{*})=0\}=\{A^{*}:A\in\ker\rho\}

for each
\rho\in\mathfrak{A}
, the mapping
\Phi^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a}:\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})\to\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
is well-defined. Since
\Phi^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a}\circ\Phi^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a}
is the identity mapping on
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
, the mapping
\Phi^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a}
is bijective and
(\Phi^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a})^{-1}=\Phi^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a}
. Now, let
S\subset\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
. Then, we have

\bigcap\{\ker\rho^{*}:\ker\rho\in S\}=\left\{A^{*}:A\in\bigcap\{\ker\rho:\ker\rho\in S\}\right\},

which implies that
\Phi^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a}(S^{=})\subset\Phi^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a}(S)^{=}
. This shows that
\Phi^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a}
(and hence, its inverse) is continuous.
∎

The final ingredient needed for our argument is the following result of Kakutani and Mackey
[
25
, Lemma 2 and Corollary]
. We give its proof for the readers’ convenience; see also
[
13
, Lemma 2]
.

Lemma 6.17

(Kakutani and Mackey, 1946)
.

Let
X,Y
be infinite dimensional complex Banach spaces, and let
T:X\to Y
be a semilinear bijection. If
T(M)
is closed whenever
M
is a closed hyperplane of
X
, then
T
is linear or conjugate linear.

Proof.

Suppose that
T
is
\sigma
-linear. Since
\sigma(1)=1
and
\sigma(i)\in\{-1,1\}
, we have
\sigma(p+iq)=p+iq
for each
p,q\in\mathbb{Q}
, or
\sigma(p+iq)=p-iq
for each
p,q\in\mathbb{Q}
. Hence, it is sufficient to show that
\sigma
is continuous. To this end, suppose to the contrary that
\sigma
is discontinuous. Since
\sigma
is additive, there exists a sequence
(c_{n})_{n}\subset\mathbb{C}
such that
\lim_{n}c_{n}=0
, and
(\sigma(c_{n}))_{n}
does not converge to
0
. We may assume that
|\sigma(c_{n})|\geq\varepsilon>0
for each
n
. Let
q_{n}\in\mathbb{Q}
be such that
q_{n}>0
, and
|q_{n}^{-1}|c_{n}|-1|<n^{-1}
. Then,
\lim_{n}q_{n}^{-1}|c_{n}|=1
,
\lim_{n}q_{n}=0
, and
\lim_{n}|\sigma(q_{n}^{-1}c_{n})|=\lim_{n}q_{n}^{-1}|\sigma(c_{n})|=+\infty
