. Hence, we have a bounded sequence
(d_{n})_{n}\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
such that
\lim_{n}|\sigma(d_{n})|=+\infty
.

Now, let
(x_{n})_{n}
be a basic sequence in
X
, and let
(f_{n})_{n}
be the coordinate functionals of
(x_{n})_{n}
. By the Hahn-Banach theorem, each
f_{n}\in[(x_{n})_{n}]^{*}
extends to an element of
X^{*}
. Replacing
(x_{n},f_{n})
with
(2^{n}\|f_{n}\|x_{n},2^{-n}\|f_{n}\|^{-1}f_{n})
if necessary, we have a biorthogonal sequence
((x_{n},f_{n}))_{n}\subset X\times X^{*}
satisfying
\|f_{n}\|=2^{-n}
. Moreover, taking a subsequence of
(d_{n})_{n}
, it may be assumed that
|\sigma(d_{n})|>n\|Tx_{n}\|
for each
n
. Since
(d_{n})_{n}
is bounded, we can define an element of
X^{*}
by
f=\sum_{n}d_{n}f_{n}
. It follows that
f(x_{n})=d_{n}
for each
n
. From this, we know that
f\neq 0
, and
fx_{0}=1
for some
x_{0}\in X
. Set
y_{n}=x_{0}-d_{n}^{-1}x_{n}
for each
n
. Then,
(y_{n})_{n}\subset\ker f
, and

\|Tx_{0}-Ty_{n}\|=\|T(x_{0}-y_{n})\|=\|T(d_{n}^{-1}x_{n})\|=\frac{\|Tx_{n}\|}{|\sigma(d_{n})|}<\frac{1}{n}\to 0,

which implies that
Tx_{0}\in\overline{T(\ker f)}
. Meanwhile, from the assumption,
T(\ker f)
is closed as
\ker f
is a closed hyperplane of
X
. Therefore,
Tx_{0}\in T(\ker f)
. However, this leads to
x_{0}\in\ker f
since
T
is bijective, which contradicts
f(x_{0})=1
. Thus,
\sigma
is continuous.
∎

Now, we give the main result in this section which describes the form of (continuous) finite-homeomorphisms between
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})
, where
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are irreducible
C^{*}
-algebras.

Theorem 6.18
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be irreducible
C^{*}
-algebras acting on Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
, respectively. Suppose that
\Phi:\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})\to\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})
is a finite-homeomorphism. Then,
\dim\mathcal{H}=\dim\mathcal{K}
provided that either
\mathcal{H}
or
\mathcal{K}
is finite-dimensional. Moreover, if
\dim\mathcal{H}=\dim\mathcal{K}\geq 3
, then there exists a pair of semilinear bijections
U
and
V
from
\mathcal{H}
onto
\mathcal{K}
such that either of the following holds:

(i)

\Phi(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A})=\ker\omega_{Ux,Vy}\cap\mathfrak{B}
for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
.

(ii)

\Phi(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A})=\ker\omega_{Vy,Ux}\cap\mathfrak{B}
for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
.

If further
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
are infinite-dimensional and
\Phi
is continuous, then there exist bounded linear or conjugate-linear isomorphisms
U
and
V
that satisfy either (i) or (ii), in which case,
U
is linear if and only if
V
is, and
V^{*}\mathfrak{B}U\subset\mathfrak{A}
. In particular,
\dim\mathcal{H}=\dim\mathcal{K}
. If
\Phi
is a homeomorphism, then
V^{*}\mathfrak{B}U=\mathfrak{A}
.

Proof.

If
\dim\mathcal{H}=1
or
\dim\mathcal{K}=1
, then
\mathfrak{A}=\mathbb{C}I
or
\mathfrak{B}=\mathbb{C}I
, which implies that

\mathop{\mathrm{card}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A}))=\mathop{\mathrm{card}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A}))=\mathop{\mathrm{card}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B}))=\mathop{\mathrm{card}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B}))=1.

Hence,
\dim\mathfrak{A}=\dim\mathfrak{B}=1
by
[
44
, Propositions 4.4 and 4.5]
, in which case,
\dim\mathcal{H}=\dim\mathcal{K}=1
. In the following, we assume that
\dim\mathcal{H}\geq 2
and
\dim\mathcal{K}\geq 2
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}_{R}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{M}_{R}(\mathfrak{B})
be the collections of all
R
-sets in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})
that are maximal with respect to inclusion, respectively. Then, Lemma
6.13
ensures that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{R}(\mathfrak{A})

\displaystyle=\{R(\overline{x},\mathcal{H}),R(\mathcal{H},\overline{y}):x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}\}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{R}(\mathfrak{B})

\displaystyle=\{R(\overline{z},\mathcal{K}),R(\mathcal{K},\overline{w}):z,w\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{0\}\}.

Since
\Phi
is a finite-homeomorphism, it preserves
R
-sets by Lemma
6.12
. Hence,
S\in\mathfrak{M}_{R}(\mathfrak{A})
if and only if
\Phi(A)\in\mathfrak{M}_{R}(\mathfrak{B})
. Fix an arbitrary
x_{0}\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. Then,
\Phi(R(\overline{x_{0}},\mathcal{H}))\in\mathfrak{M}_{R}(\mathfrak{B})
. If
\Phi(R(\overline{x_{0}},\mathcal{H}))=R(\mathcal{K},\overline{w_{0}})
for some
w_{0}\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{0\}
, we consider
\Phi^{\mathfrak{B}}_{a}\circ\Phi
instead of
\Phi
, where
\Phi^{\mathfrak{B}}_{a}
is a homeomorphism on
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})
defined in Lemma
6.16
. We note that
\omega_{z,w}^{*}=\omega_{w,z}
for each
z,w\in\mathcal{K}
, and that
\Phi^{\mathfrak{B}}_{a}(R(\mathcal{K},\overline{w_{0}}))=R(\overline{w_{0}},\mathcal{K})
. It should also be noted that
\Phi^{\mathfrak{B}}_{a}
maps
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})
onto itself. Therefore, it is sufficient to prove the existence of mappings
U
and
V
with desired properties under the additional assumption that
\Phi(R(\overline{x_{0}},\mathcal{H}))=R(\overline{z_{0}},\mathcal{K})
for some
z_{0}\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{0\}
.

Let
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. We claim that there exist
z,w\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{0\}
such that
\Phi(R(\overline{x},\mathcal{H}))=R(\overline{z},\mathcal{K})
and
\Phi(R(\mathcal{H},\overline{y}))=R(\mathcal{K},\overline{w})
. To show this, we note that if
R(\overline{x_{0}},\mathcal{H})\cap R(\overline{x},\mathcal{H})\neq\emptyset
then
\overline{x_{0}}=\overline{x}
. Indeed, if
\ker\omega_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in R(\overline{x_{0}},\mathcal{H})\cap R(\overline{x},\mathcal{H})
, then
x^{\prime}\in\overline{x_{0}}\cap\overline{x}
, which implies that
\overline{x_{0}}=\overline{x}
. Now, suppose that
\Phi(R(\overline{x},\mathcal{H}))=R(\mathcal{K},\overline{w})
for some
w\in\mathcal{K}
. Then, it follows from

\displaystyle\Phi(R(\overline{x_{0}},\mathcal{H})\cap R(\overline{x},\mathcal{H}))

\displaystyle=\Phi(R(\overline{x_{0}},\mathcal{H}))\cap\Phi(R(\overline{x},\mathcal{H})

\displaystyle=R(\overline{z_{0}},\mathcal{K})\cap R(\mathcal{K},\overline{w})

\displaystyle=\{\ker\omega_{z_{0},w}\cap\mathfrak{B}\}

that
\overline{x_{0}}=\overline{x}
. However, this means that
R(\overline{x_{0}},\mathcal{H})=R(\overline{x},\mathcal{H})
and

\Phi(R(\overline{x_{0}},\mathcal{H}))=\{\ker\omega_{z_{0},w}\cap\mathfrak{B}\},

which is impossible since
R(\overline{x_{0}},\mathcal{H})
contains infinitely many elements by
\dim\mathcal{H}\geq 2
. Therefore,
\Phi(R(\overline{x},\mathcal{H}))=R(\overline{z},\mathcal{K})
for some
z\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{0\}
. Similarly since

\displaystyle R(\overline{z_{0}},\mathcal{K})\cap\Phi(R(\mathcal{H},\overline{y}))

\displaystyle=\Phi(R(\overline{x_{0}},\mathcal{H}))\cap\Phi(R(\mathcal{H},\overline{y}))

\displaystyle=\Phi(R(\overline{x_{0}},\mathcal{H})\cap R(\mathcal{H},\overline{y}))

\displaystyle=\{\Phi(\ker\omega_{x_{0},y}\cap\mathfrak{A})\},

it follows that
\Phi(R(\mathcal{H},\overline{y}))=R(\mathcal{K},\overline{w})
; for otherwise,
R(\overline{z_{0}},\mathcal{K})=\{\Phi(\omega_{x_{0},y}\cap\mathfrak{A})\}
, which is also impossible since
\dim\mathcal{K}\geq 2
.

For each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
, let
\varphi(\overline{x})
and
\psi(\overline{y})
be the unique one-dimensional subspaces of
\mathcal{K}
that satisfy
\Phi(R(\overline{x},\mathcal{H}))=R(\varphi(\overline{x}),\mathcal{K})
and
\Phi(R(\mathcal{H},\overline{y}))=R(\mathcal{K},\psi(\overline{y}))
, respectively. Then, both
\varphi
and
\psi
are bijection between the collections of one-dimensional subspaces of
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
. Moreover, for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
, we have

\displaystyle\Phi(\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\})

\displaystyle=\Phi(R(\overline{x},\mathcal{H})\cap R(\mathcal{H},\overline{y}))

\displaystyle=\Phi(R(\overline{x},\mathcal{H}))\cap\Phi(R(\mathcal{H},\overline{y}))

\displaystyle=R(\varphi(\overline{x}),\mathcal{K})\cap R(\mathcal{K},\psi(\overline{y})).

Hence,
\Phi(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A})=\ker\omega_{z,w}\cap\mathfrak{B}
for any choice of
z\in\varphi(\overline{x})\setminus\{0\}
and
w\in\psi(\overline{y})\setminus\{0\}
. For each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
, choose
z(x),w(y)\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{0\}
such that
z(x)\in\varphi(\overline{x})
and
w(y)\in\psi(\overline{y})
. It follows that

\Phi(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A})=\ker\omega_{z(x),w(y)}\cap\mathfrak{B}

for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
.

The next step was inspired by
[
13
, Lemma 1]
. Let
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{K})
be the collections of all subspaces of
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
. For each
M\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H})
, let

\varphi_{0}(M)=\langle\{z(x):x\in M\setminus\{0\}\}\rangle.

Then, we note that
\varphi_{0}(\{0\})=\langle\emptyset\rangle=\{0\}
and
\varphi_{0}(\overline{x})=\varphi(\overline{x})
for each
x\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. We claim that

M\setminus\{0\}=\{x\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}:z(x)\in\varphi_{0}(M)\}

for each
M\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H})
. Indeed, if
x\in M\setminus\{0\}
, then
z(x)\in\varphi_{0}(M)
by the definition of
\varphi_{0}(M)
. Conversely, suppose that
x\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
and
z(x)\in\varphi_{0}(M)
. Then, there exist
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in M\setminus\{0\}
and
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
such that
z(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_{j}z(x_{j})
. Fix an arbitrary
y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. Since
\omega_{z(x),w(y)}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_{j}\omega_{z(x_{j}),w(y)}
, it follows that
\bigcap_{j=1}^{n}\ker\omega_{z(x_{j}),w(y)}\cap\mathfrak{B}\subset\ker\omega_{z(x),w(y)}\cap\mathfrak{B}
, that is,
\Phi(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A})\in\Phi(\{\ker\omega_{x_{j},y}:j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\})^{=}
. Combining this with

\displaystyle\Phi(\{\ker\omega_{x_{j},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\})^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})

\displaystyle=\Phi(\{\ker\omega_{x_{j},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\}^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})),

we obtain
\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in\{\ker\omega_{x_{j},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\}^{=}
. Let
E
be the projection from
\mathcal{H}
onto
\langle\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\rangle
. If
x\not\in\langle\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\rangle
, then
(I-E)x\neq 0
. Since
\mathfrak{A}
acts irreducibly on
\mathcal{H}
, there exists an element
A\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
Ay=(I-E)x
. It follows that
\omega_{x_{j},y}(A^{*})=0
for each
j
and
\omega_{x,y}(A^{*})=\|(I-E)x\|^{2}>0
, which contradicts
\bigcap_{j=1}^{n}\ker\omega_{x_{j},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\subset\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}
. Therefore,
x\in\langle\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\rangle\subset M
. This proves the claim, which shows that
\varphi_{0}
is injective.

Next, let
M_{N}=\{x\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}:z(x)\in N\}\cup\{0\}
for each
N\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{K})
. We claim that
M_{N}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H})
and
N=\varphi_{0}(M_{n})
. To show this, take an arbitrary
x\in\langle M_{N}\rangle\setminus\{0\}
and an arbitrary
y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. Then,
x\in\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
for some
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in M_{N}\setminus\{0\}
, which implies that

\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in\{\ker\omega_{x_{j},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\}^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A}).

It follows from

\displaystyle\Phi(\{\ker\omega_{x_{j},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\}^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A}))

\displaystyle=\Phi(\{\ker\omega_{x_{j},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\})^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})

that
\ker\omega_{z(x),w(y)}\cap\mathfrak{B}\in\{\ker\omega_{z(x_{j}),w(y)}\cap\mathfrak{B}:j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\}^{=}
. Let
F
be the projection from
\mathcal{K}
onto
\langle z(x_{1}),\ldots,z(x_{n})\rangle
. If
z(x)\not\in\langle z(x_{1}),\ldots,z(x_{n})\rangle
, then
(I-F)z(x)\neq 0
. Since
\mathfrak{B}
acts irreducibly on
\mathcal{K}
, there exists an element
B\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
Bw(y)=(I-F)z(x)
. However, this means that

B^{*}\in\bigcap_{j=1}^{n}(\ker\omega_{z(x_{j}),w(y)}\cap\mathfrak{B})\setminus(\ker\omega_{z(x),w(y)}\cap\mathfrak{B}),

which contradicts
\ker\omega_{z(x),w(y)}\cap\mathfrak{B}\in\{\ker\omega_{z(x_{j}),w(y)}\cap\mathfrak{B}:j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\}^{=}
. Hence,
z(x)\in\langle z(x_{1}),\ldots,z(x_{n})\rangle\subset N
, that is,
x\in M_{N}
. This shows that
\langle M_{N}\rangle\setminus\{0\}=M_{N}\setminus\{0\}
, which implies that
M_{N}=\langle M_{N}\rangle\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H})
. Since
\{z(x):x\in M_{N}\setminus\{0\}\}\subset N
, it follows that
\varphi_{0}(M_{N})\subset N
. Conversely, if
z\in N\setminus\{0\}
, then
\varphi(\overline{x})=\overline{z}
for some
x\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
, in which case,
z(x)=cz
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. In particular, we have
x\in M_{N}\setminus\{0\}
and
z=c^{-1}z(x)\in\varphi_{0}(M_{N})
. Therefore,
N=\varphi_{0}(M_{N})
. We note that
M_{\mathcal{K}}=\mathcal{H}
and
\varphi_{0}(\mathcal{H})=\mathcal{K}
.

By the preceding two paragraphs, we know that
\varphi_{0}:\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H})\to\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{K})
is bijective. Moreover, it is in fact an order isomorphism. From this,
\dim\mathcal{H}=2
if and only if
\dim\mathcal{K}=2
. Indeed, if
\dim\mathcal{H}=2
, then
\{0\}\subsetneq\overline{x}\subsetneq\mathcal{H}
is a maximal chain in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H})
for each
x\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. Since
\varphi_{0}
is an order isomorphism,
\{0\}=\varphi_{0}(\{0\})\subsetneq\varphi_{0}(\overline{x})\subsetneq\varphi_{0}(\mathcal{H})=\mathcal{K}
is also a maximal chain in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{K})
. This shows that
\dim\mathcal{K}=2
. The converse also follows from the same argument as above. Now, suppose that
\dim\mathcal{H}\geq 3
(and
\dim\mathcal{K}\geq 3
). Then, by the fundamental theorem of projective geometry, there exists a semilinear bijection
U:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{K}
such that
\varphi_{0}(M)=U(M)
for each
M\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H})
. In particular,
\varphi(\overline{x})=\varphi_{0}(\overline{x})=U(\overline{x})
for each
x\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. Since
U
preserves linear independence of finite subsets of
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
, it follows that
\mathcal{H}
is finite-dimensional if and only if
\mathcal{K}
is, and
\dim\mathcal{H}=\dim\mathcal{K}
provided that either
\mathcal{H}
or
\mathcal{K}
is finite-dimensional.

Suppose that
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
are infinite-dimensional and
\Phi
is continuous. Let
N
be a proper closed subspace of
\mathcal{K}
, let
y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
, and let
(x_{n})_{n}
be a sequence in
\varphi_{0}^{-1}(N)=M_{N}
that converges to
x\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. It may be assumed that
x_{n}\neq 0
for each
n
. Then, we obtain
\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\in\{\ker\omega_{x_{n},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
, which together with

\Phi(\{\ker\omega_{x_{n},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A}))\subset\Phi(\{\ker\omega_{x_{n},y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:n\in\mathbb{N}\})^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})

implies that
\ker\omega_{z(x),w(y)}\cap\mathfrak{B}\in\{\ker\omega_{z(x_{n}),w(y)}\cap\mathfrak{B}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}^{=}
. Let
F_{N}
be the projection from
\mathcal{K}
onto
N
. If
z(x)\not\in N
, then an element
B_{N}\in\mathfrak{B}
with
B_{N}w(y)=(I-F_{N})z(x)
satisfies

B_{N}^{*}\in\bigcap_{n}(\ker\omega_{z(x_{n}),w(y)}\cap\mathfrak{B})\setminus(\ker\omega_{z(x),w(y)}\cap\mathfrak{B})

by
(z(x_{n}))_{n}\subset N
, a contradiction. Therefore,
z(x)\in N
, that is,
x\in M_{N}=\varphi_{0}^{-1}(N)
. This shows that
U^{-1}(N)=\varphi_{0}^{-1}(N)
is closed whenever
N
is a closed subspace of
\mathcal{K}
, which together with Lemma
6.17
implies that
U^{-1}
(and
U
) is linear or conjugate-linear. Considering
U
as a mapping from
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
onto
\mathcal{K}
if necessary, we may assume that
U
is linear. If
g\in\mathcal{K}^{*}
, then
\ker g
is a closed subspace of
\mathcal{K}
, which implies that
\ker(g\circ U)=U^{-1}(\ker g)
is a closed subspace of
\mathcal{H}
(or
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
). This means that
g\circ U\in\mathcal{H}^{*}
(or
g\circ U\in\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{*}
) whenever
g\in\mathcal{K}^{*}
, that is,
U
is weak-to-weak continuous. Therefore,
U
is bounded, and
U^{-1}
is also bounded by the open mapping theorem. Similarly (or applying the same argument as above to the mapping
\Psi=\Phi^{\mathfrak{B}}_{a}\circ\Phi\circ\Phi^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a}
), provided that
\dim\mathcal{H}\geq 3
(and
\dim\mathcal{K}\geq 3
), we can show that there exists a semilinear bijection
V:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{K}
(which is bounded linear or conjugate-linear isomorphism if
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
are infinite-dimensional and
\Phi
is continuous) such that
\psi(\overline{y})=V(\overline{y})
for each
y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. Consequently, we have

\Phi(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A})=\ker\omega_{Ux,Vy}\cap\mathfrak{B}

and

(\Phi^{\mathfrak{B}}_{a}\circ\Phi)(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A})=\ker\omega_{Vy,Ux}\cap\mathfrak{B}

for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. This completes the proof of the existence of
U
and
V
that satisfy (i) or (ii).

Finally, suppose that both
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
are infinite-dimensional and
\Phi
is continuous. In this case, we can choose bounded linear or conjugate-linear isomorphisms
U
and
V
that satisfy (i) or (ii). Since
\mathfrak{B}
acts irreducibly on
\mathcal{K}
, either
K(\mathcal{K})\subset\mathfrak{B}
or
K(\mathcal{K})\cap\mathfrak{B}=\{0\}
by
[
10
, Corollary IV.1.2.5]
. If
K(\mathcal{K})\subset\mathfrak{B}
, let
B_{0}
be any finite-rank projection on
\mathcal{K}
with
\dim B_{0}(\mathcal{K})\geq 2
. In the case that
K(\mathcal{K})\cap\mathfrak{B}=\{0\}
, take an arbitrary nonzero positive element
B_{0}\in\mathfrak{B}
. Then,
B_{0}(\mathcal{H})
is infinite-dimensional since
B_{0}
cannot be finite-rank. Let
z_{0}\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{0\}
be such that
\langle B_{0}z_{0},z_{0}\rangle>0
, and let
S(B_{0})=\{\ker\omega_{z,w}\cap\mathfrak{B}\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B}):B_{0}\in\ker\omega_{z,w}\cap\mathfrak{B}\}
. Since
S(B_{0})^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})=S(B_{0})
and
\Phi
is continuous, we obtain

\Phi^{-1}(S(B_{0}))^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})=\Phi^{-1}(S(B_{0}))

and in particular

\Phi^{-1}(\ker\omega_{z_{0},z_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{B})\not\in\Phi^{-1}(S(B_{0}))^{=}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})

by
\ker\omega_{z_{0},z_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{B}\not\in S(B_{0})
. From this and
\Phi^{-1}(\ker\omega_{z_{0},z_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{B})\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
, there exists an element
A_{0}\in\mathfrak{A}
such that

A_{0}\in\bigcap\{\Phi^{-1}(\ker\omega_{z,w}\cap\mathfrak{A}):z,w\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{0\},~\langle B_{0}z,w\rangle=0\}\setminus\Phi^{-1}(\ker\omega_{z_{0},z_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{B}).

If
\Phi(\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A})=\omega_{Ux,Vy}\cap\mathfrak{B}
for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
, then

\Phi^{-1}(\ker\omega_{z,w}\cap B)=\ker\omega_{U^{-1}z,V^{-1}w}\cap\mathfrak{A}

for each
z,w\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{0\}
, which implies that
\langle A_{0}U^{-1}z,V^{-1}w\rangle=0
whenever
\langle B_{0}z,w\rangle=0
and
\langle A_{0}U^{-1}z_{0},V^{-1}z_{0}\rangle\neq 0
. Since
(V^{-1})^{*}A_{0}U^{-1}
is a bounded linear or conjugate-linear operator on
\mathcal{K}
, Lemma
6.15
ensures that
(V^{-1})^{*}A_{0}U^{-1}=cB_{0}
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. In particular,
V^{*}B_{0}U
is nonzero and linear. From this,
U
is linear if and only if
V
is. Meanwhile, if
\Phi(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A})=\ker\omega_{Vy,Ux}\cap\mathfrak{B}
for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
, then

\Phi^{-1}(\ker\omega_{z,w}\cap\mathfrak{B})=\ker\omega_{U^{-1}w,V^{-1}z}\cap\mathfrak{A}

for each
z,w\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{0\}
. In this case, we have
\langle(U^{-1})^{*}A_{0}^{*}V^{-1}z,w\rangle=0
whenever
\langle B_{0}z,w\rangle=0
and
\langle(U^{-1})^{*}A_{0}^{*}V^{-1}z_{0},z_{0}\rangle\neq 0
, which together with Lemma
6.15
implies that
(U^{-1})^{*}A_{0}^{*}V^{-1}=cB_{0}
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. In particular,
U^{*}B_{0}V
is nonzero and linear. Therefore, also in this case,
U
is linear if and only if
V
is. We note that if
\Phi(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A})=\ker\omega_{Vy,Ux}\cap\mathfrak{B}
for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
, then
(\Phi^{\mathfrak{B}}\circ\Phi)(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A})=\ker\omega_{Ux,Vy}
for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. Hence, in either case, the mapping
\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}\mapsto\ker\omega_{Ux,Vy}\cap\mathfrak{B}
is continuous if
\Phi
is. Combining this with Theorem
6.9
, we have
V^{*}\mathfrak{B}U\subset\mathfrak{A}
(and
V^{*}\mathfrak{B}U=\mathfrak{A}
if
\Phi
is a homeomorphism), as desired.
∎

7.
The Naimark problem revisited

The main objective of this section is to present a characterization elementary
C^{*}
-algebras in terms of their geometric structure spaces. In this direction, the separable case has been already completed by Rosenberg
[
34
]
as was mentioned in the introduction, since the spectrum of a
C^{*}
-algebra can be recognized as a substructure of its geometric structure space. To be precise, a separable
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
is elementary if and only if
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
is a singleton. Meanwhile, by the result of Akemann and Weaver
[
2
]
, we cannot show in ZFC that non-separable
C^{*}
-algebras with singleton spectra are always elementary. Under these circumstances, we try to find a property
P
for geometric structure spaces of (non-separable)
C^{*}
-algebras
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\mathfrak{A}
is elementary if and only if
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
is a singleton and
\mathfrak{A}
has the property
P
as well as the property
P
does not imply that
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
is a singleton. To this end, we first characterize
C^{*}
-algebras with singleton spectra in terms of their geometric structure spaces. The following theorem gives some conditions equivalent to the reverse inclusion of Corollary
5.18
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, and let
\rho
be a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
. Then, the following are equivalent:

(i)

C(\ker\rho)^{=}=C(\ker\rho)
.

(ii)

\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}))=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}\}
.

(iii)

\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi(\mathfrak{A}):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}}\}
for each
\pi\in\overline{\pi_{\rho}}
.

(iv)

\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi(\mathfrak{A}):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}}\}
for some
\pi\in\overline{\pi_{\rho}}
.

Proof.

Let
\Phi(\ker\tau)=\ker\pi_{\rho}^{*}(\tau)
for each
\ker\tau\in\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}))
. Since
\ker\pi_{\rho}=J_{C(\ker\rho)}
by Lemma
5.10
, it follows from Theorem
5.17
that
\Phi
is a homeomorphism from
\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}))
onto
C(\ker\rho)^{=}
. In particular,
\Phi(\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})))=C(\ker\rho)^{=}
. Meanwhile, we have

\displaystyle C(\ker\rho)

\displaystyle=\{\ker\pi_{\rho}^{*}(\omega_{x,y}|\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}\}

\displaystyle=\Phi(\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}\})

by Lemma
6.1
. Hence, we have
C(\ker\rho)^{=}=C(\ker\rho)
if and only if
\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}))=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}\}
. This proves (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii).

Suppose that (i) holds. Let
\pi:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathcal{H}
be an irreducible representation of
\mathfrak{A}
that is unitarily equivalent to
\pi_{\rho}
, and let
U:\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\to\mathcal{H}
be an isometric isomorphism such that
\pi(A)=U\pi_{\rho}(A)U^{*}
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. If
\ker\tau\in\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))
, then we obtain
\pi^{*}(\tau)\in\mathop{\mathrm{ext}}\nolimits(B_{\mathfrak{A}^{*}})
and

J_{C(\ker\rho)}=\ker\pi_{\rho}=\ker\pi\subset\ker\pi^{*}(\tau),

which together with Lemmas
4.13
and
6.1
implies that

\pi^{*}(\tau)\in C(\ker\rho)^{=}=C(\ker\rho)=\{\ker\pi_{\rho}^{*}(\omega_{x,y}|\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\tau}}\}.

Hence,
\pi^{*}(\tau)=\pi_{\rho}^{*}(\omega_{x,y}|\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}))
for some
x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}
, in which case, we have

\tau(\pi(A))=\omega_{x,y}(\pi_{\rho}(A))=\langle\pi_{\rho}(A)x,y\rangle=\langle U^{*}\pi(A)Ux,y\rangle

for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. This shows that
\tau=\omega_{Ux,Uy}|\pi(\mathfrak{A})
. Combining this with Corollary
5.18
, we derive that
\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi(\mathfrak{A}):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}}\}
. Therefore, (i)
\Rightarrow
(iii). The implication (iii)
\Rightarrow
(iv) is obvious.

Finally, suppose that
\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi(\mathfrak{A}):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}}\}
for some
\pi\in\overline{\pi_{\rho}}
. Let
V:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
be an isometric isomorphism such that
\pi(A)=V^{*}\pi_{\rho}(A)V
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, and let
\psi(\pi(A))=\pi_{\rho}(A)
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
. Then,
\psi:\pi(\mathfrak{A})\to\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})
is a
*
-isomorphism. By Theorem
4.19
, setting
\Psi(I)=\psi(I)
for each
I\in\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))
, we have a homeomorphism
\Psi:\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))\to\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}))
. Since

\displaystyle\Psi(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi(\mathfrak{A}))=\psi(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi(\mathfrak{A}))

\displaystyle=\ker(\omega_{x,y}\circ\psi^{-1})\cap\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})

\displaystyle=\ker\omega_{Vx,Vy}\cap\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}),

it follows that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}))=\Psi(\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A})))

\displaystyle=\{\Psi(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi(\mathfrak{A})):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}}\}

\displaystyle=\{\ker\omega_{Vx,Vy}\cap\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}}\}

\displaystyle=\{\ker\omega_{z,w}\cap\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}):z,w\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}\}.

Thus, (iv)
\Rightarrow
(ii).
∎

Corollary 7.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Then,
C(I)^{=}=C(I)
for each
I\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
if and only if
\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi(\mathfrak{A}):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}}\}
for each irreducible representation
\pi:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
C(I)^{=}=C(I)
for each
I\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
, and that
\pi:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H})
is an irreducible representation of
\mathfrak{A}
. Then, there exists a pure state
\rho
of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\pi
and
\pi_{\rho}
are unitarily equivalent. Since
C(\ker\rho)^{=}=C(\ker\rho)
, it follows from Theorem
7.1
that
\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi(\mathfrak{A}):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}}\}
.

Conversely, suppose that
\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi(\mathfrak{A}):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}}\}
for each irreducible representation
\pi:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathcal{H}
. Let
I\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
. Then, by Lemma
5.9
, there exists a pure state
\rho
such that
C(I)=C(\ker\rho)
. Since
\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}))=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}\}
, it follows from Theorem
7.1
that
C(I)^{=}=C(\ker\rho)^{=}=C(\ker\rho)=C(I)
.
∎

Corollary 7.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Then,
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
(or equivalently,
\mathfrak{GS}(\mathfrak{A})
) is a singleton if and only if there exists a faithful irreducible representation
\pi:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H})
of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi(\mathfrak{A}):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}}\}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{GS}(\mathfrak{A})
is a singleton. Then,
C(I)=\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{A})
for any choice of
I\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
. Hence, by Corollary
7.2
,
\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi(\mathfrak{A}):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}}\}
for each irreducible representation
\pi:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H})
. We note that since
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
is also a singleton, each irreducible representation
\pi:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H})
is faithful.

Conversely, suppose that there exists a faithful irreducible representation
\pi:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H})
of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi(\mathfrak{A}):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}}\}
. Let
\rho
be a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\pi
and
\pi_{\rho}
are unitarily equivalent. Then, we obtain
C(\ker\rho)^{=}=C(\ker\rho)
by Theorem
7.1
. Meanwhile, since
\pi
is faithful, it follows from Lemma
5.10
that
J_{C(\ker\rho)}=\ker\pi_{\rho}=\ker\pi=\{0\}
. Combining this with Lemma
4.13
, we infer that
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})=C(\ker\rho)^{=}=C(\ker\rho)
. Thus,
\mathfrak{GS}(\mathfrak{A})
is a singleton.
∎

This completes our first task. Next, we introduce an important subcategory of
\mathbb{C}
-
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Gss}}
.

Definition 7.4
.

The full subcategory of
\mathbb{C}
-
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Gss}}
whose objects are homeomorphs of geometric structure spaces of nontrivial
C^{*}
-algebras is denoted by
\mathop{C^{*}\textrm{-}\textrm{\bf Gss}}
.

Remark 7.5
.

From the definition,
\mathop{C^{*}\textrm{-}\textrm{\bf Gss}}
is a strictly full subcategory of
\mathbb{C}
-
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Gss}}
. We note that if
(K,c)
is an object of
\mathop{C^{*}\textrm{-}\textrm{\bf Gss}}
, then
(K^{\mathfrak{G}},c^{\mathfrak{G}})
and
(K^{\mathfrak{P}},c^{\mathfrak{P}})
are both topologizable by Proposition
3.14
, Theorems
3.29
and
4.16
, and Corollary
5.12
.

We make use of a characterization of norm-closed ideals of
B(\mathcal{H})
by Luft
[
27
, Corollary 6.1]
; see also
[
16
]
. Recall that, for a cardinal number
\alpha\geq\aleph_{0}
, an element
A\in B(\mathcal{H})
is said to be
\alpha
-compact
if
\dim M<\alpha
whenever
M
is a closed subspace of
A(\mathcal{H})
. Let
K_{\alpha}(\mathcal{H})
be the set of all
\alpha
-compact operators on
B(\mathcal{H})
. Then,
K_{\aleph_{0}}(\mathcal{H})=K(\mathcal{H})
and
K_{\alpha}(\mathcal{H})\subset K_{\beta}(\mathcal{H})
whenever
\alpha\leq\beta
.

Theorem 7.6

(Luft, 1968)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space. Then,
\mathcal{I}
is a norm-closed ideal of
B(\mathcal{H})
if and only if
\mathcal{I}=K_{\alpha}(\mathcal{H})
for some cardinal number
\alpha\geq\aleph_{0}
.

If
U
and
V
are bounded conjugate-linear isomorphism from
\mathcal{H}
onto
\mathcal{K}
, and if
A\in K_{\alpha}(\mathcal{K})
, then
V^{*}AU\in K_{\alpha}(\mathcal{H})
. Indeed, if
M
is a closed subspace of
V^{*}AU(\mathcal{H})
, then
(V^{-1})^{*}(M)
is a closed subspace of
AU(\mathcal{H})=A(\mathcal{K})
, which implies that
\dim M=\dim(V^{-1})^{*}(M)<\alpha
. This shows that
V^{*}AU\in K_{\alpha}(\mathcal{H})
. In summary, we have the identity
V^{*}K_{\alpha}(\mathcal{K})U=K_{\alpha}(\mathcal{H})
.

The following is the main result in this paper.

Theorem 7.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a non-separable
C^{*}
-algebra. Then,
\mathfrak{A}
is elementary if and only if it satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
is a singleton.

(ii)

There exists an element
I\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
with the following property: If
(K,c)
is an object of
\mathop{C^{*}\textrm{-}\textrm{\bf Gss}}
, and if there exists a continuous finite-homeomorphism
f:C(t)\to C(I)
for an element
t\in K
, then
f\circ g\circ f^{-1}
is a homeomorphism on
C(I)
whenever
g
is a continuous finite-homeomorphism on
C(t)
.

Proof.

Each elementary
C^{*}
-algebra has the singleton spectrum by Rosenberg’s theorem. We assume that
\mathfrak{A}
is
*
-isomorphic to a norm-closed ideal
\mathcal{I}
of
B(\mathcal{H})
for some infinite-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Then, there exists a homeomorphism
\Phi:\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})\to\mathfrak{S}(\mathcal{I})
by Theorem
4.19
, and
\mathcal{I}
acts irreducibly on
\mathcal{H}
since it contains
K(\mathcal{H})
. Let
I=\Phi^{-1}(\ker\omega_{x,x}\cap\mathcal{I})
for some
x\in S_{\mathcal{H}}
. We note that
\Phi(C(I))=C(\Phi(I))=\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathcal{I})
by Lemmas
3.28
and
6.3
.

Let
(K,c)
be an object of
\mathop{C^{*}\textrm{-}\textrm{\bf Gss}}
, and let
f:C(t)\to C(I)
be a continuous finite-homeomorphism. Then, there exist a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{B}
and a homeomorphism
f_{0}:\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})\to K
. Define another mapping
\Phi_{0}=\Phi\circ f\circ f_{0}
from
C(f_{0}^{-1}(t))
onto
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathcal{I})
. Proposition
3.18
ensures that
\Phi_{0}
is a continuous finite-homeomorphism. If
g
is a continuous finite-homeomorphism on
C(t)
, then
\Psi=f_{0}^{-1}\circ g\circ f_{0}
is a continuous finite-homeomorphism on
C(f_{0}^{-1}(t))
. We will know that
f\circ g\circ f^{-1}
is a homeomorphism on
C(I)
once it is proved that
\Phi_{0}\circ\Psi\circ\Phi_{0}^{-1}=\Phi\circ(f\circ g\circ f^{-1})\circ\Phi^{-1}
is a homeomorphism on
C(\Phi(I))
.

By Lemma
5.9
, there exists a pure state
\rho
of
\mathfrak{B}
such that
C(f_{0}^{-1}(t))=C(\ker\rho)
, in which case, by Theorem
5.17
, the mapping
\Psi_{0}:\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{B}))\to C(\ker\rho)^{=}
given by
\Psi(\ker\tau)=\ker\pi_{\rho}^{*}(\tau)
for each
\ker\tau\in\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{B}))
is a homeomorphism. Moreover,
\Psi_{0}(\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{B})))=C(\ker\rho)
by Lemma
6.1
. Hence, the mapping
\Phi_{0}\circ\Psi_{0}
gives a continuous finite-homeomorphism from
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{B}))
onto
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathcal{I})
. Applying Theorem
6.18
, we obtain
\dim\mathcal{H}=\dim\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
and bounded linear or conjugate-linear isomorphisms
U
and
V
from
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
onto
\mathcal{H}
such that
U
is linear if and only if
V
is,
V^{*}\mathcal{I}U\subset\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{B})
, and either

(\Phi_{0}\circ\Psi_{0})(\ker\omega_{z,w}\cap\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{B}))=\ker\omega_{Uz,Vw}\cap\mathcal{I}

for each
z,w\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\setminus\{0\}
or

(\Phi_{0}\circ\Psi_{0})(\ker\omega_{z,w}\cap\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{B}))=\ker\omega_{Vw,Uz}\cap\mathcal{I}

for each
z,w\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\setminus\{0\}
. Replacing
\Phi_{0}\circ\Psi_{0}
with
\Phi^{\mathcal{I}}_{a}\circ\Phi_{0}\circ\Psi_{0}
if necessary, we may assume that the former case occurs.

Let
\Psi^{\prime}=\Psi_{0}^{-1}\circ\Psi\circ\Psi_{0}
. Then,
\Psi^{\prime}
is a continuous finite-homeomorphism on
\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{B})
and

\Psi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\pi(\mathfrak{B})))=(\Psi_{0}^{-1}\circ\Psi)(C(\ker\rho))=\Psi_{0}^{-1}(C(\ker\rho))=\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\pi(\mathfrak{B})).

Hence, by Proposition
3.18
and Theorem
6.18
, there exist bounded linear or conjugate-linear isomorphisms
U^{\prime},V^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
such that
U^{\prime}
is linear if and only if
V^{\prime}
is,
V^{\prime*}\pi(\mathfrak{B})U^{\prime}=\pi(\mathfrak{B})
, and either

\Phi^{\prime}(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{B}))=\ker\omega_{U^{\prime}x,V^{\prime}y}\cap\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{B})

for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\setminus\{0\}
or

\Phi^{\prime}(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{B}))=\ker\omega_{V^{\prime}y,U^{\prime}x}\cap\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{B})

for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\setminus\{0\}
. It follows from

\Phi_{0}\circ\Psi\circ\Phi_{0}^{-1}=(\Phi_{0}\circ\Psi_{0})\circ\Psi^{\prime}\circ(\Phi_{0}\circ\Psi_{0})^{-1}

that

(\Phi_{0}\circ\Psi\circ\Phi_{0}^{-1})(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathcal{I})=\ker\omega_{UU^{\prime}U^{-1}x,VV^{\prime}V^{-1}y}\cap\mathcal{I}

for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
in the former case, and

(\Phi_{0}\circ\Phi\circ\Phi_{0}^{-1})(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathcal{I})=\ker\omega_{UV^{\prime}V^{-1}y,VU^{\prime}U^{-1}x}\cap\mathcal{I}

for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
in the latter case. We note that

UU^{\prime}U^{-1},VV^{\prime}V^{-1},UV^{\prime}V^{-1},VU^{\prime}U^{-1}

are all bounded linear or conjugate-linear isomorphisms on
\mathcal{H}
. Moreover,
UU^{\prime}U^{-1}
is linear if and only if
U^{\prime}
is linear, while
VV^{\prime}V^{-1}
is linear if and only if
V^{\prime}
is linear. This shows that
UU^{\prime}U^{-1}
is linear if and only if
VV^{\prime}V^{-1}
is. Similarly since
U
is linear if and only
V
is, it follows that
UV^{\prime}V^{-1}
is linear if and only if
VU^{\prime}U^{-1}
is. Combining these with the fact that
\mathcal{I}
is an ideal of
B(\mathcal{H})
, in the light of Theorems
6.9
and
7.6
, we infer that
\Phi_{0}\circ\Psi\circ\Phi_{0}^{-1}
is a homeomorphism on
C(\Phi(I))
. Thus,
\mathfrak{A}
satisfies (ii) if it is
*
-isomorphic to a norm-closed ideal of some infinite-dimensional
B(\mathcal{H})
.

For the converse, suppose that (i) and (ii) hold. By Corollary
7.3
, it may be assumed that there
\mathfrak{A}
is an irreducible
C^{*}
-algebra acting on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})=\{\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}:x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}\}
. In particular, we have
C(I)=\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})=\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
for any choice of
I\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
. Let
U
be a unitary operator on
\mathcal{H}
. Define mappings
\Phi_{0}:\mathfrak{S}_{n}(B(\mathcal{H}))\to\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\Phi_{U}:\mathfrak{S}(B(\mathcal{H}))\to\mathfrak{S}(B(\mathcal{H}))
by
\Phi_{0}(\ker\omega_{x,y})=\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}
for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
and
\Phi_{U}(\ker\rho)=\ker\rho U
for each
\ker\rho\in\mathfrak{S}(B(\mathcal{H}))
. Then,
\Phi_{0}
is a continuous finite-homeomorphism by Theorem
6.9
. Moreover,
\Phi_{U}
is a homeomorphism on
\mathfrak{S}(B(\mathcal{H}))
with
\Phi_{U}(\mathfrak{S}_{n}(B(\mathcal{H})))=\mathfrak{S}_{n}(B(\mathcal{H}))
. Indeed, let
\varphi_{U^{*}}(A)=U^{*}A
for each
A\in B(\mathcal{H})
. Then,
\varphi
is an isometric isomorphism on
B(\mathcal{H})
, which together with Theorem
4.19
implies that the mapping
\ker\rho\mapsto\varphi_{U^{*}}(\ker\rho)=\Phi_{U}(\ker\rho)
is a homeomorphism on
\mathfrak{S}(B(\mathcal{H}))
. We note that
\ker\rho\sim\ker\rho U
by Theorem
5.8
, which implies that
\Phi_{U}(\ker\rho)\in C(\ker\rho)
for each
\ker\rho\in\mathfrak{S}(B(\mathcal{H}))
. Therefore,
\Phi_{U}(C(I))=C(\Phi_{U}(I))=C(I)
for each
I\in\mathfrak{S}(B(\mathcal{H}))
. In particular,
\Phi_{U}
restricts to
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(B(\mathcal{H}))
. From the assumption, the mappings
\Phi_{0}\circ\Phi_{U}\circ\Phi_{0}^{-1}
and
\Phi_{0}\circ\Phi_{U^{*}}\circ\Phi_{0}^{-1}
are homeomorphisms on
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
. Since

(\Phi_{0}\circ\Phi_{U}\circ\Phi_{0}^{-1})(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A})=\ker\omega_{x,U^{*}y}\cap\mathfrak{A}

and

(\Phi_{0}\circ\Phi_{U^{*}}\circ\Phi_{0}^{-1})(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A})=\ker\omega_{x,Uy}\cap\mathfrak{A}

for each
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
, it follows from Theorem
6.9
that
U\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{A}
and
U^{*}\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{A}
. By the self-adjointness of
\mathfrak{A}
, we also have
\mathfrak{A}U=\mathfrak{A}
. From this and the fact that each element of
B(\mathcal{H})
can be represented as a linear combination of at most four unitary elements, it turns out that
B\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{A}B=\mathfrak{A}
for each
B\in B(\mathcal{H})
, that is,
\mathfrak{A}
is a norm-closed ideal of
B(\mathcal{H})
. If
\mathfrak{A}\neq K(\mathcal{H})
, then
K(\mathcal{H})
is a proper norm-closed ideal of
\mathfrak{A}
. In the light of
[
32
, Corollary 3.13.8]
, each norm-closed ideal of a
C^{*}
-algebra is the intersection of those primitive ideals that contain it, which ensures that there exists an irreducible representation
\pi
of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
K(\mathcal{H})\subset\ker\pi
. However, this is impossible since each irreducible representation of
\mathfrak{A}
is faithful by (i). Thus,
\mathfrak{A}=K(\mathcal{H})
.
∎

Remark 7.8
.

The condition (ii) in the preceding theorem is written in the language of closure spaces, that is, it is preserved under closure space homeomorphisms. To show this, let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be
C^{*}
-algebras, and let
\Phi:\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})\to\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})
be a homeomorphism. If
\mathfrak{A}
satisfies the condition (ii), then there exists an element
I\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
with the stated property. Let
(K,c)
be an object of
\mathop{C^{*}\textrm{-}\textrm{\bf Gss}}
, and if there exists a continuous finite-homeomorphism
f:C(t)\to C(\Phi(I))
for an element
t\in K
, then
\Phi^{-1}\circ f
is a continuous finite-homeomorphism from
C(t)
onto
C(I)
. Therefore,
(\Phi^{-1}\circ f)\circ g\circ(\Phi^{-1}\circ f)^{-1}
is a homeomorphism on
C(I)
whenever
g
is a continuous finite-homeomorphism on
C(t)
. It follows that
f\circ g\circ f^{-1}
is a homeomorphism on
C(\Phi(I))
. This shows that
\mathfrak{B}
also satisfies the condition (ii).

We cannot show any implication between (i) and (ii) in the preceding theorem. Indeed, as was shown in its proof, if
\mathcal{H}
is a non-separable Hilbert space, then each proper norm-closed ideal of
B(\mathcal{H})
other than
K(\mathcal{H})
provides a counterexample to (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i). Meanwhile, the implication (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) is equivalent to the Naimark problem, which cannot be proved in ZFC and have counterexamples, for example, in ZFC
+

\diamondsuit_{\aleph_{1}}
.

Theorem
7.7
provides a characterization of elementary
C^{*}
-algebras in terms of their geometric structure spaces. In particular,
being elementary
can be recognized in the context of closure spaces. Starting with this point, we give characterizations of type I (or CCR, or (sub)homogeneous)
C^{*}
-algebras. Recall that a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
is said to be
n
-homogeneous
(or
n-subhomogeneous
) if every irreducible representation of
\mathfrak{A}
is of dimension
n
(or of dimension less than or equal to
n
).

Theorem 7.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Then, the following hold:

(i)

\mathfrak{A}
is of type I if and only if, for each
I\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
, there exist a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{I}
and a continuous finite-homeomorphism
\Phi_{I}:C(I)\to\mathfrak{S}(K(\mathcal{H}_{I}))
.

(ii)

\mathfrak{A}
is CCR if and only if, for each
I\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
, there exist a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{I}
and a homeomorphism
\Phi_{I}:C(I)\to\mathfrak{S}(K(\mathcal{H}_{I}))
.

(iii)

\mathfrak{A}
is
n
-homogeneous if and only if, for each
I\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
, there exist a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{I}
with
\dim\mathcal{H}_{I}=n
and a (finite-)homeomorphism
\Phi_{I}:C(I)\to\mathfrak{S}(K(\mathcal{H}_{I}))
.

(iv)

\mathfrak{A}
is
n
-subhomogeneous if and only if, for each
I\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
, there exist a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{I}
with
\dim\mathcal{H}_{I}\leq n
and a (finite-)homeomorphism
\Phi_{I}:C(I)\to\mathfrak{S}(K(\mathcal{H}_{I}))
.

Proof.

Let
\rho
be a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
. Then, by Lemma
5.10
and Theorem
5.17
, the mapping
\Phi:\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}))\to C(\ker\rho)^{=}
given by
\Phi(\ker\tau)=\ker\pi_{\rho}^{*}(\tau)
is a homeomorphism. Meanwhile, we have
C(\ker\rho)=\{\ker\pi_{\rho}^{*}(\omega_{x,y}|\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})):x,y\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}\}
by Lemma
6.1
. Hence,
\Phi|\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}))
is a homeomorphism from
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}))
onto
C(\ker\rho)
.

Let
I\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
. Then, by Corollary
5.9
, there exists a pure state
\rho
of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
C(I)=C(\ker\rho)
. Suppose that
\mathfrak{A}
is of type I, then
K(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})\subset\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})
, which together with Theorem
6.9
(for the case
U=V=\mathbf{1}
) implies that the mapping
\Psi_{\rho}:\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}))\to\mathfrak{S}(K(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})
given by
\Psi_{\rho}(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}))=\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap K(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})
is a continuous finite-homeomorphism, which induces a continuous finite-homeomorphism
\Phi_{I}:C(I)\to K(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})
by composing with
\Phi^{-1}|C(I)
. If
\mathfrak{A}
is CCR, then
\Psi_{I}
and
\Phi_{I}
are homeomorphisms by
K(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})=\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})
. In the
n
-homogeneous (or
n
-subhomogemeous) case, we further have
\dim\mathcal{H}_{\rho}=n
(or
\dim\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\leq n
).

Conversely, let
\pi:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H})
be an irreducible representation of
\mathfrak{A}
. Then, there exists a pure state
\rho
such that
\pi
and
\pi_{\rho}
are unitarily equivalent. Let
U_{\rho}:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
be an isometric isomorphism. If
\Psi:C(\ker\rho)\to\mathfrak{S}(K(\mathcal{K}))
is a finite-homeomorphism, then so is
\Psi\circ(\Phi|\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})))
. By Theorem
6.18
, we obtain
\dim\mathcal{H}_{\rho}=\dim\mathcal{K}
provided that
\mathcal{K}
is finite-dimensional, in which case,
K(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})=\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})=U_{\rho}\pi(\mathfrak{A})U_{\rho}^{*}
and
K(\mathcal{H})=\pi(\mathfrak{A})
. In particular,
\mathfrak{A}
is
n
-homogeneous (or
n
-subhomogeneous) if
\mathcal{K}
can be always chosen to satisfy
\dim\mathcal{K}=n
(or
\dim\mathcal{K}\leq n
). If
\mathcal{K}
is infinite-dimensional and
\Psi
is continuous, then Theorem
6.18
generates bounded linear or conjugate-linear isomorphisms
U
and
V
from
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
onto
\mathcal{K}
such that
U
is linear if and only if
V
is, and
V^{*}K(\mathcal{K})U\subset\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})
. It follows from
K(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})=V^{*}K(\mathcal{H})U
that
K(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})\subset\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})=U_{\rho}\pi(\mathfrak{A})U_{\rho}^{*}
. Therefore,
K(\mathcal{H})=U_{\rho}^{*}K(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})U_{\rho}\subset\pi(\mathfrak{A})
. This shows that
\mathfrak{A}
is of type I. If
\Psi
can be always chosen to be homeomorphism, then
K(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})=V^{*}K(\mathcal{H})U=\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})
and
K(\mathcal{H})=\pi(\mathfrak{A})
. Thus,
\mathfrak{A}
is CCR in this case.
∎

What is important in the preceding theorem is that each property of
C^{*}
-algebras is explained by closure space properties of geometric structure spaces.

8.
Nonlinear classification of
C^{*}
-algebras

Here, we consider nonlinear classification of
C^{*}
-algebras in terms of their geometric structure spaces.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category. Then, the
core
of
\mathcal{C}
, denoted by
\mathop{\mathrm{core}}\nolimits(\mathcal{C})
, is the subcategory consisting of all objects of
\mathcal{C}
but with morphisms only the isomorphisms of
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 8.2
.

The category of Banach spaces over
\mathbb{K}
and linear contraction, denoted as
\mathbb{K}
-
Ban
1
, is the category whose objects are Banach spaces over
\mathbb{K}
. The morphisms between Banach spaces
X
and
Y
are linear operator
T:X\to Y
with
\|T\|\leq 1
, and the composition of morphisms is the composition of mappings. The full subcategory of
\mathbb{C}
-
Ban
1
whose objects are
C^{*}
-algebras is denoted by
C^{*}
-
Ban
1
.

Remark 8.3
.

The isomorphisms in
\mathbb{K}
-
Ban
1
are isometric isomorphisms. If
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are
C^{*}
-algebras, then
T:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is an isometric isomorphism if and only if there exist a Jordan
*
-isomorphism
J:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
and a unitary element of the multiplier algebra of
\mathfrak{B}
such that
T(A)=UJ(A)
for each
A\in\mathfrak{A}
, where
J:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is called a
Jordan
*
-isomorphism
if
J
is bijective, linear,
J(A^{*})=J(A)^{*}
and
J(AB+BA)=J(A)J(B)+J(A)J(B)
for each
A,B\in\mathfrak{A}
. The fact that Jordan
*
-isomorphisms between unital
C^{*}
-algebras are isometric isomorphisms (and preserves commutativity) was shown by Kadison
[
22
, Theorem 5]
, which was extended naturally by a result of Paterson and Sinclair
[
31
, Theorem 2]
. Namely, each Jordan
*
-isomorphism between (possible non-unital)
C^{*}
-algebras extends uniquely to a Jordan
*
-isomorphism between their multiplier algebras. Meanwhile, the powerful fact that isometric isomorphisms between
C^{*}
-algebras generate Jordan
*
-isomorphisms between them was shown in
[
22
, Theorem 7]
for unital
C^{*}
-algebras and in
[
31
, Theorem 1]
for arbitrary
C^{*}
-algebras.

From the viewpoint of nonlinear classification, it is appropriate to define
\mathfrak{S}(\{0\})
as
\emptyset
. Remark that there exists a unique (trivial) closure operator on
\emptyset
. Since morphisms in
\mathop{\mathrm{core}}\nolimits(\mathbb{K}
-
Ban
{}_{1})
are isometric isomorphisms, we can define an invariant in
\mathop{\mathrm{core}}\nolimits(\mathbb{K}
-
Ban
{}_{1})
as a consequence of Theorem
4.19
.

Definition 8.4
.

Define a
\mathbb{K}
-
\mathop{\textrm{\bf Gss}}
-valued invariant
\mathfrak{S}
in
\mathop{\mathrm{core}}\nolimits(\mathbb{K}
-
Ban
{}_{1})
by
X\mapsto\mathfrak{S}(X)
and
T\mapsto\Phi_{T}
, where
\Phi_{T}
is a homeomorphism in Theorem
4.19
.

A natural question concerning the preceding definition is as follows: Is the invariant
\mathfrak{S}
complete? In this direction, it is already known that there exists a pair of Banach spaces
(X,Y)
over
\mathbb{K}
such that
X
is not (isometrically) isomorphic to
Y
but
\mathfrak{S}(X)
is homeomorphic to
\mathfrak{S}(Y)
. The complex case was settled in uniform algebras, see
[
45
, Example 4.14]
, while the real case was recently studied in
[
47
]
. Therefore, the invariant
\mathfrak{S}
is not complete in the whole category
\mathop{\mathrm{core}}\nolimits(\mathbb{K}
-
Ban
{}_{1})
. Meanwhile, it was shown in
[
42
, Theorem 5.2]
and
[
44
, Theorem 3.16]
that
\mathfrak{S}
is complete in the context of abelian
C^{*}
-algebras. At least, there is still no counterexample in
C^{*}
-algebras. Motivated by these circumstances, we attack the following problem in the rest of this paper, and give some partial answers to it.

Problem 8.5
.

Is the invariant
\mathfrak{S}
complete in
\mathop{\mathrm{core}}\nolimits(C^{*}
-
Ban
{}_{1})
?

Remark 8.6
.

By Remark
8.3
, the preceding problem is equivalently reformulated as follows: If
C^{*}
-algebras
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
have homeomorphic geometric structure spaces, are they Jordan
*
-isomorphic?

We begin with understanding the effects of the existence of homeomorphisms between geometric structure spaces of
C^{*}
-algebras.

Theorem 8.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be
C^{*}
-algebras. Suppose that
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})
are homeomorphic. Then, there exists a homeomorphism
\varphi:\hat{A}\to\hat{B}
such that
\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))
and
\mathfrak{S}(\pi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{B}))
are homeomorphic whenever
\varphi(\overline{\pi})=\overline{\pi^{\prime}}
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{A}
is of type I or CCR or
n
-homogeneous or
n
-subhomogeneous if and only if
\mathfrak{B}
has the same corresponding property.

Proof.

Let
\Phi:\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})\to\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})
be a homeomorphism. Then, by Lemma
3.28
, we have
\Phi(C(I))=C(\Phi(I))
and
\Phi(C(I)^{=})=C(\Phi(I))^{=}
for each
I\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})
. In particular,
\Phi|C(I)
is a homeomorphism from
C(I)
onto
C(\Phi(I))
by Proposition
3.18
. Hence, the proof of the latter half is completed by combining this with Theorem
7.9
.

To prove the former half, recall that Theorem
4.16
generates the homeomorphism
\Phi^{\mathfrak{GS}}:\mathfrak{GS}(\mathfrak{A})\to\mathfrak{GS}(\mathfrak{B})
given by
\Phi^{\mathfrak{GS}}(C(I))=C(\Phi(I))
for each
I\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
. Moreover, by Theorem
5.11
, natural homeomorphisms
\Phi_{\mathfrak{A}}:\hat{\mathfrak{A}}\to\mathfrak{GS}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\Phi_{\mathfrak{B}}:\hat{\mathfrak{B}}\to\mathfrak{GS}(\mathfrak{B})
are given by
\Phi_{\mathfrak{A}}(\overline{\pi_{\rho}})=C(\ker\rho)
and
\Phi_{\mathfrak{B}}(\overline{\pi_{\tau}})=C(\ker\tau)
for each pure state
\rho
and
\tau
of
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
, respectively. Therefore, we can define a homeomorphism
\varphi:\hat{\mathfrak{A}}\to\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
by
\varphi=\Phi_{\mathfrak{B}}^{-1}\circ\Phi^{\mathfrak{GS}}\circ\Phi_{\mathfrak{A}}
. If
\rho
and
\tau
are pure states of
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
, then
\varphi(\overline{\pi_{\rho}})=\overline{\pi_{\tau}}
if and only if
C(\Phi(\ker\rho))=C(\ker\tau)
. Now, suppose that
\varphi(\overline{\pi_{\rho}})=\overline{\pi_{\tau}}
. Then,
\Phi(C(\ker\rho)^{=})=C(\Phi(\ker\rho))^{=}=C(\ker\tau)^{=}
, which implies that
C(\ker\rho)^{=}
and
C(\ker\tau)^{=}
are homeomorphic. Meanwhile, by Lemma
5.10
and Theorem
5.17
,
\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}))
is homeomorphic to
C(\ker\rho)^{=}
, while
\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\tau}(\mathfrak{B}))
is homeomorphic to
C(\ker\tau)^{=}
. Therefore,
\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A}))
and
\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\tau}(\mathfrak{B}))
are homeomorphic. This completes the proof since
\pi(\mathfrak{A})
and
\pi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{B})
are
*
-isomorphic to
\pi_{\rho}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\pi_{\tau}(\mathfrak{B})
, respectively, whenever
\pi\in\overline{\pi_{\rho}}
and
\pi^{\prime}\in\overline{\pi_{\tau}}
.
∎

To obtain results related to the preceding theorem with higher resolution, we have to focus on each of irreducible representations of
C^{*}
-algebras. Below we present a basic tool for this.

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be
C^{*}
-algebras. Suppose that
\mathfrak{A}
acts irreducibly on a Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}
, and that
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})
are homeomorphic. Then, there exist a faithful irreducible representation
\pi
of
\mathfrak{B}
and a homeomorphism
\Phi:\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})\to\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{B}))
such that
\Phi(\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A}))=\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\pi(\mathfrak{B}))
.

Proof.

Let
\Phi:\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})\to\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})
be a homeomorphism. By Lemma
6.3
, we have
\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})=C(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A})
for any choice of
x,y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. In particular, since
J_{\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})}=\{0\}
, it follows from Lemma
4.13
that
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})=\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})^{=}
. Hence,
\Phi(\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A}))=C(\Phi(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}))
and

C(\Phi(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A}))^{=}=\Phi(\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A}))^{=}=\Phi(\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})^{=})=\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B}).

Set
I^{\prime}=\Phi(\ker\omega_{x,y}\cap\mathfrak{A})
for short. Since
C(I^{\prime})^{=}=\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})
, we obtain
J_{C(I^{\prime})}=\{0\}
by
[
44
, Lemma 4.1]
. It follows from Corollary
5.9
that there exists a pure state
\tau
of
\mathfrak{B}
such that
C(I^{\prime})=C(\ker\tau)
. We note that
\ker\pi_{\tau}=J_{C(\ker\tau)}=\{0\}
by Lemma
5.10
, that is,
\pi_{\tau}
is faithful. Moreover, we derive that

\Phi_{\pi_{\tau}^{-1}}(\ker\rho)=\pi_{\tau}^{-1}(\ker\rho)=\ker\pi_{\tau}^{*}(\rho)

for each
\ker\rho\in\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\tau}(\mathfrak{B}))
. Since
\Phi_{\pi_{\tau}}
is a homeomorphism from
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})
onto
\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\tau}(\mathfrak{B}))
with
\Phi_{\pi_{\tau}^{-1}}=\Phi_{\pi_{\tau}}^{-1}
by Theorem
4.19
and
C(\ker\tau)=\Phi_{\pi_{\tau}^{-1}}(\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\pi_{\tau}(\mathfrak{B})))
by Lemma
6.1
, it follows that the mapping
\Phi_{\pi_{\tau}}\circ\Phi
is a homeomorphism from
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
onto
\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\tau}(\mathfrak{B}))
with
(\Phi_{\pi_{\tau}}\circ\Phi)(\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A}))=\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\pi_{\tau}(\mathfrak{B}))
.
∎

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, and let
\pi:\mathfrak{A}\to B(\mathcal{H})
be an irreducible representation. Recall that
\pi
is called a GCR representation if
K(\mathcal{H})\subset\pi(\mathfrak{A})
. A primitive ideal
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathfrak{A}
is said to be
\mathfrak{A}
-GCR
if there exists a GCR representation
\pi
of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\mathcal{I}=\ker\pi
.

Theorem 8.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be are irreducible
C^{*}
-algebras acting on Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
, respectively. Suppose that
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})
are homeomorphic. Then,
K(\mathcal{H})=\mathfrak{A}
or
K(\mathcal{H})\subsetneq\mathfrak{A}
or
K(\mathcal{H})\cap\mathfrak{A}=\{0\}
if and only if
\mathfrak{B}
has the same corresponding property. Moreover, if
K(\mathcal{H})\subset\mathfrak{A}
or
K(\mathcal{K})\subset\mathfrak{B}
, then
\dim\mathcal{H}=\dim\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
K(\mathcal{K})\subset\mathfrak{B}
. Then, the identity representation of
\mathfrak{B}
is (primitive and) GCR, and
\{0\}
is a
\mathfrak{B}
-GCR ideal in
\mathfrak{B}
. Hence, by
[
10
, Corollary IV.1.3.6]
, any faithful irreducible representation or
\mathfrak{B}
is unitarily equivalent to the identity representation of
\mathfrak{B}
. Meanwhile, by Lemma
8.8
, there exist a faithful irreducible representation
\pi:\mathfrak{B}\to B(\mathcal{K}_{0})
and a homeomorphism
\Phi:\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})\to\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{B}))
such that
\Phi(\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\mathfrak{A}))=\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\pi(\mathfrak{B}))
. Let
W:\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{K}_{0}
be an isometric isomorphism such that
\pi(B)=WBW^{*}
for each
B\in\mathfrak{B}
. We note that
\dim\mathcal{K}=\dim\mathcal{K}_{0}
.

If
\mathcal{K}
is finite-dimensional, then
\dim\mathcal{H}=\dim\mathcal{K}_{0}=\dim\mathcal{K}
by Theorem
6.18
, in which case,
K(\mathcal{H})=\mathfrak{A}
and
K(\mathcal{K})=\mathfrak{B}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{K}
is infinite-dimensional. Then, again by Theorem
6.18
,
\mathcal{H}
is necessarily infinite-dimensional and there exist bounded linear or conjugate-linear isomorphisms
U
and
V
from
\mathcal{H}
onto
\mathcal{K}_{0}
such that
U
is linear if and only if
V
is, and
V^{*}\pi(\mathfrak{B})U=\mathfrak{A}
. Hence, it follows from
K(\mathcal{K})\subset\mathfrak{B}
that

K(\mathcal{H})=V^{*}WK(\mathcal{K})W^{*}U\subset V^{*}W\mathfrak{B}W^{*}U=\mathfrak{A}.

In particular, if
K(\mathcal{K})=\mathfrak{B}
, then
K(\mathcal{H})=\mathfrak{A}
. This shows that
K(\mathcal{K})\subset\mathfrak{B}
(or
K(\mathcal{K})=\mathfrak{B}
) implies that
\dim\mathcal{H}=\dim\mathcal{K}
and
K(\mathcal{H})\subset\mathfrak{A}
(or
K(\mathcal{H})=\mathfrak{A}
). The converse to these implications also follow from an argument similar to above. Combining this with
[
10
, IV.1.2.5]
, we can conclude that
\mathfrak{A}\cap K(\mathcal{H})=\{0\}
if and only if
\mathfrak{B}\cap K(\mathcal{K})=\{0\}
.
∎

As an application of Theorems
8.7
and
8.9
, we give the first example of a class of non-abelian
C^{*}
-algebras that are completely determined by their geometric structure spaces. This answers Problem
8.5
partially and affirmatively.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Then,
\mathfrak{A}
is said to be
compact
if it is
*
-isomorphic to a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
K(\mathcal{H})
for some Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. For compact
C^{*}
-algebras, we know a reasonable characterization by direct sums of elementary
C^{*}
-algebras; see
[
8
, Theorem 1.4.5]
. Namely, a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
is compact if and only if there exists a family of Hilbert spaces
(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
such that
\mathfrak{A}
is
*
-isomorphic to
\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}K(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})
, where
\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}K(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})
denotes the
c_{0}
-direct sum of the family
(K(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}))_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
. The following lemma translates this into the language of geometric structure spaces. It should be mentioned that the lemma was due to Huruya
[
18
]
if
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
is a finite set.

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Then,
\mathfrak{A}
is compact if and only if
\mathfrak{A}
is CCR and
\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{A})
is discrete, in which case,
\pi(\mathfrak{A})=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\pi_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{A})
, where
\pi=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\pi_{\lambda}
is the reduced atomic representation of
\mathfrak{A}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{A}
is compact. Then, it may be assumed that
\mathfrak{A}=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}K(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})
for a family of Hilbert space
(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
. Let
\pi_{\mu}
be a representation of
\mathfrak{A}
given by
\pi_{\mu}((A_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda})=A_{\mu}
for each
(A_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\in\mathfrak{A}
. Since
\pi_{\mu}(\mathfrak{A})=K(\mathcal{H}_{\mu})
, it follows that
\pi_{\mu}
is an irreducible representation of
\mathfrak{A}
. Moreover, each irreducible representation
\pi
of
\mathfrak{A}
is unitarily equivalent to some
\pi_{\mu}
. Indeed, since
\mathfrak{A}=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}K(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})
, it follows that
\mathfrak{A}^{*}
is the
\ell_{1}
-direct sum of the family
(K(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})^{*})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
. From this, if
\rho
is a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\pi_{\rho}
is unitarily equivalent to
\pi
, then there exists a unique index
\mu
such that
\rho|\mathfrak{A}P_{\mu}\neq 0
, where
P_{\mu}
is the projection from the
\ell_{2}
-direct sum
\mathcal{H}
of the family
(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
onto the subspace naturally identified with
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}
. In particular,
\rho|\mathfrak{A}P_{\mu}
is a pure state of
\mathfrak{A}P_{\mu}
which is the natural embedding of
K(\mathcal{H}_{\mu})
. Hence, we have an element
x\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}}
such that

\rho((A_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda})=\langle A_{\mu}x,x\rangle=\langle\pi_{\mu}((A_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda})x,x\rangle

for each
(A_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\in\mathfrak{A}
. Meanwhile, since
x
is cyclic in
\pi_{\mu}(\mathfrak{A})
, the representations
\pi_{\rho}
and
\pi_{\mu}
are unitarily equivalent. Therefore,
\pi\in\overline{\pi_{\mu}}
. This shows that
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}=\{\overline{\pi_{\lambda}}:\lambda\in\Lambda\}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{A})=\{\ker\pi_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\Lambda\}
. In particular, it follows that
\mathfrak{A}
is CCR. The discreteness of
\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{A})
follows from
\bigcap_{\lambda\neq\mu}\ker\pi_{\lambda}=\mathfrak{A}P_{\mu}\not\subset\ker\pi_{\mu}
for each
\mu
.

Conversely, suppose that
\mathfrak{A}
is CCR and
\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{A})
is discrete. Set
\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{A})=\{\mathcal{I}_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\Lambda\}
, where
\mathcal{I}_{\lambda}\neq\mathcal{I}_{\mu}
whenever
\lambda\neq\mu
. Then, for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
, there exists an irreducible representation
\pi_{\lambda}
of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\mathcal{I}_{\lambda}=\ker\pi_{\lambda}
. Since
\mathfrak{A}
is CCR, by the Glimm-Sakai theorem, the mapping
\pi\mapsto\ker\pi
is a bijection from
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
onto
\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{A})
. Therefore,
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}=\{\overline{\pi_{\lambda}}:\lambda\in\Lambda\}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{A})=\{\ker\pi_{\rho}:\lambda\in\Lambda\}
. Moreover,
\overline{\pi_{\lambda}}\neq\overline{\pi_{\mu}}
and
\ker\pi_{\lambda}\neq\ker\pi_{\mu}
whenever
\lambda\neq\mu
.

By the discreteness of
\mathop{\mathrm{Prim}}\nolimits(\mathfrak{A})
, we have
\bigcap_{\lambda\neq\mu}\ker\pi_{\lambda}\not\subset\ker\pi_{\mu}
for each
\mu
. Let
A_{\mu}\in\bigcap_{\lambda\neq\mu}\ker\pi_{\lambda}\setminus\ker\pi_{\mu}
, and let
\mathcal{J}_{\mu}=\bigcap_{\lambda\neq\mu}\ker\pi_{\lambda}
for each
\mu
. Then,
\mathcal{J}_{\mu}
is a norm-closed ideal of
\mathfrak{A}
and
0\neq\pi_{\mu}(A_{\mu})\in\pi_{\mu}(\mathcal{J}_{\mu})
. Remark that
\pi_{\mu}
is faithful on
\mathcal{J}_{\mu}
since
\mathcal{J}_{\mu}\cap\ker\pi_{\mu}=\{0\}
, which ensures that
\pi_{\mu}(\mathcal{J}_{\mu})
is a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
\pi_{\mu}(\mathfrak{A})=K(\mathcal{H}_{\mu})
. Meanwhile since
\pi_{\mu}(A_{\mu})\neq 0
, there exists an
x_{0}\in S_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}}
such that
y_{0}=\pi_{\mu}(A_{\mu})x_{0}\neq 0
. Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu}
be such that
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
is linearly independent. Then, for each
j
, there exist
A_{j},B_{j}\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
\pi_{\mu}(A_{j})y_{0}=y_{j}
and
\pi_{\mu}(B_{j})x_{i}=\delta_{ij}x_{0}
. It follows that
A_{j}A_{\mu}B_{j}\in\mathcal{J}_{\mu}
and
\pi_{\mu}(A_{j}A_{\mu}B_{j})x_{i}=\delta_{ij}y_{j}
, which implies that
\sum_{j=1}^{n}\pi_{\mu}(A_{j}A_{\mu}B_{j})x_{i}=y_{i}
for each
i
. This shows that
\pi_{\mu}(\mathcal{J}_{\mu})
also acts irreducibly on
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}
. Combining this with
\pi_{\mu}(\mathcal{J}_{\mu})\subset K(\mathcal{H}_{\mu})
, we obtain
\pi_{\mu}(\mathcal{J}_{\mu})=K(\mathcal{H}_{\mu})
by
[
10
, Proposition IV.1.2.4]
.

Let
\pi=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\pi_{\lambda}
. Then,
\pi
is the reduced atomic representation of
\mathfrak{A}
which is known to be faithful. If
P_{\mu}
is the projection from the
\ell_{2}
-direct sum
\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
of the family
(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
onto the subspace naturally identified with
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}
, and if
(B_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\in\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}K(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})
, then for each
\varepsilon>0
, there exist
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\in\Lambda
such that
\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}(B_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}P_{\lambda_{j}}-(B_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\|<\varepsilon
. Moreover, by the preceding paragraph,
B_{\lambda_{j}}=\pi_{\lambda_{j}}(A_{\lambda_{j}})
for some
A_{\lambda_{j}}\in\mathcal{J}_{\lambda_{j}}
, which implies that
\pi(A_{\lambda_{j}})=(\pi_{\lambda}(A_{\lambda_{j}}))_{\lambda\in\Lambda}=(B_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}P_{\lambda_{j}}
for each
j
. Hence,
\sum_{j=1}^{n}(B_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}P_{\lambda_{j}}=\pi(\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{\lambda_{j}})\in\pi(\mathfrak{A})
and
(B_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\in\pi(\mathfrak{A})
. It follows that
\pi(\mathfrak{A})\supset\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}K(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})
.

We claim that
\pi(\mathfrak{A})=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}K(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})
. If
\pi(\mathfrak{A})\supsetneq\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}K(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})
, then
\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}K(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})
is a proper norm-closed ideal of
\pi(\mathfrak{A})
. Since each closed ideal of a
C^{*}
-algebra is the intersection of those primitive ideals that contain it (
[
32
, Corollary 3.13.8]
), there exists an irreducible representation
\varphi
of
\pi(\mathfrak{A})
such that
\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}K(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})\subset\ker\varphi
. We note that
\varphi\circ\pi
is an irreducible representation of
\mathfrak{A}
, which implies that
\varphi\circ\pi\in\overline{\pi_{\mu}}
for some
\mu\in\Lambda
. In particular,
\ker(\varphi\circ\pi)=\ker\pi_{\mu}
. Meanwhile since
\pi_{\mu}(\mathcal{J}_{\mu})=K(\mathcal{H}_{\mu})
, there exists an
A\in\mathcal{J}_{\mu}
such that
\pi_{\mu}(A)\neq 0
. However, then
(\varphi\circ\pi)(A)=0
by
\pi(A)\in\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}K(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})
. This contradicts
\ker(\varphi\circ\pi)=\ker\pi_{\mu}
. Thus,
\pi(\mathfrak{A})=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}K(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})
.
∎

Remark 8.11
.

The fact that compact
C^{*}
-algebras are always CCR was already noted in
[
8
, the paragraph following Definition 1.5.1]
.

Now, we prove that compact
C^{*}
-algebras are completely determined by their geometric structure spaces.

Theorem 8.12
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be
C^{*}
-algebras. Suppose that either
\mathfrak{A}
or
\mathfrak{B}
is compact. Then,
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are
*
-isomorphic if and only if
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})
are homeomorphic.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the “if” part. Suppose that
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})
are homeomorphic. If either
\mathfrak{A}
or
\mathfrak{B}
is compact, then both of them are compact by Corollary
5.13
, Theorem
8.7
and Lemma
8.10
. Moreover, we have
\pi(\mathfrak{A})=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\pi_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{A})
for the reduced atomic representation of
\mathfrak{A}
. We note that
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}=\{\overline{\pi_{\lambda}}:\lambda\in\Lambda\}
by the definition of
\pi
. Meanwhile, Theorem
8.7
generates a homeomorphism
\varphi:\hat{\mathfrak{A}}\to\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
such that
\mathfrak{S}(\pi_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{A}))=\mathfrak{S}(\pi^{\prime}_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{B}))
whenever
\varphi(\overline{\pi_{\lambda}})=\overline{\pi^{\prime}_{\lambda}}
. Since
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are CCR, it follows from Theorem
8.9
that irreducible
C^{*}
-algebras
\pi_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\pi^{\prime}_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{B})
are
*
-isomorphic. Now, again by Lemma
8.10
, the reduced atomic representation
\pi^{\prime}=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\pi^{\prime}_{\lambda}
of
\mathfrak{B}
satisfies
\pi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{B})=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\pi^{\prime}_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{B})
. Thus,
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are
*
-isomorphic.
∎

Corollary 8.13
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be
C^{*}
-algebras. Suppose that either
\mathfrak{A}
or
\mathfrak{B}
is elementary. Then,
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are
*
-isomorphic if and only if
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})
are homeomorphic.

Corollary 8.14
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be
C^{*}
-algebras. Suppose that either
\mathfrak{A}
or
\mathfrak{B}
is finite-dimensional. Then,
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are
*
-isomorphic if and only if
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})
are homeomorphic.

Recall that the algebras of the form
B(\mathcal{H})
are recognized as
type I factors
in the theory of operator algebras, although
B(\mathcal{H})
is not a type I
C^{*}
-algebra if
\mathcal{H}
is infinite-dimensional: see, for example,
[
10
, IV.1.1.5]
. Hence, the following theorem gives an example of a non-compact
C^{*}
-algebra that is completely determined by its geometric structure space.

Theorem 8.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be
C^{*}
-algebras. Suppose that either
\mathfrak{A}
or
\mathfrak{B}
is a type I factor. Then,
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are
*
-isomorphic if and only if
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})
are homeomorphic.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the “if” part. Suppose that
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{B})
are homeomorphic. It may be assumed that
\mathfrak{A}=B(\mathcal{H})
for some Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Since
\mathfrak{A}
acts irreducibly on
\mathcal{H}
, by Lemma
8.8
, there exist a faithful irreducible representation
\pi:\mathfrak{B}\to B(\mathcal{K})
and a homeomorphism
\Phi:\mathfrak{S}(B(\mathcal{H}))\to\mathfrak{S}(\pi(\mathfrak{B}))
such that
\Phi(\mathfrak{S}_{n}(B(\mathcal{H})))=\mathfrak{S}_{n}(\pi(\mathfrak{B}))
. By Theorem
6.18
, we have
\dim\mathcal{H}=\dim\mathcal{K}
, which implies that
\pi(\mathfrak{B})=B(\mathcal{K})
if either
\mathcal{H}
or
\mathcal{K}
is finite-dimensional. If
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
are infinite-dimensional, there exists bounded linear or conjugate-linear isomorphisms
U
and
V
from
\mathcal{H}
onto
\mathcal{K}
such that
U
is linear if and only if
V
is, and
V\*\pi(\mathfrak{B})U=B(\mathcal{H})
. This shows that
\pi(\mathfrak{B})=(V^{-1})^{*}B(\mathcal{H})U^{-1}=B(\mathcal{K})
. Thus,
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are
*
-isomorphic via
B(\mathcal{H})
and
\pi(\mathfrak{B})
.
∎

Finally, we introduce two classification problems related to Problem
8.5
. The first one is based on the structure determined by Birkhoff-James orthogonality. Recall that a (possibly nonlinear) mapping
T
between Banach spaces is called a Birkhoff-James orthogonality preserver if is bijective and
x\perp_{BJ}y
is equivalent to
Tx\perp_{BJ}Ty
. A nonlinear equivalence of Banach spaces is naturally defined in terms of these mappings.

Definition 8.16
.

Let
X
and
Y
be Banach spaces. Then,
X
and
Y
are
isomorphic with respect to the structure determined by Birkhoff-James orthogonality
, denoted by
X\sim_{BJ}Y
, if there exists a Birkhoff-James orthogonality preserver
T:X\to Y
.

This notion was first introduced in
[
41
, Definition 1.3]
. It is known that if
X
and
Y
are smooth Banach spaces, then
X\sim_{BJ}Y
if and only if
X
is isometrically isomorphic to
Y
(or
\overline{Y}
in the complex case); see
[
6
, Theorem 3.11]
and also
[
19
,
43
]
for interim results. Moreover, it was shown in
[
42
, Theorems 5.5 and 5.14]
that spaces of continuous functions and classical sequence spaces are isometrically classified by their structure determined by Birkhoff-James orthogonality. We remark that these results on spaces of continuous functions and classical sequence spaces are also valid in terms of geometric structure spaces; see
[
44
, Theorems 3.16 and 6.14]
.

Meanwhile, there is a graph theoretical approach to studying the structure determined by Birkhoff-James orthogonality. The following definition was given in
[
6
]
.

Definition 8.17
.

Let
X
be a Banach space. Then, the
di-orthograph

\mathop{\hat{\Gamma}}(X)
of
X
consists of the vertices
V(\mathop{\hat{\Gamma}}(X))=\{\overline{x}:x\in X\setminus\{0\}\}
and arrows
E(\mathop{\hat{\Gamma}}(X))=\{(\overline{x},\overline{y}):x\perp_{BJ}y\}
, where
\overline{x}
is the one-dimensional subspace of
X
generated by
x
, and
(\overline{x},\overline{y})\in E(\mathop{\hat{\Gamma}}(X))
means that
\mathop{\hat{\Gamma}}(X)
contains the directed arrow
\overline{x}\to\overline{y}
.

An advantage of di-orthographs is that smoothness of finite-dimensional Banach spaces as well as rotundity of Banach spaces are characterized in terms of them; see
[
6
, Lemmas 2.5 and 2.6]
. It is worth mentioning that a non-directed version was also considered in
[
5
]
earlier.

Using graph isomorphisms between di-orthographs, we can introduce another geometric nonlinear equivalence of Banach spaces. Recall that a mapping
f
from a directed graph
G
to another
H
is called a
graph isomorphism
if it is a bijection from the vertices of
G
onto that of
H
and
x\to y
is equivalent to
f(x)\to f(y)
.

Definition 8.18
.

Let
X
and
Y
be Banach spaces. Then,
X
and
Y
said to
isomorphic with respect to di-orthographs
, denoted by
X\sim_{\mathop{\hat{\Gamma}}}Y
if there exists a graph isomorphism
f:\mathop{\hat{\Gamma}}(X)\to\mathop{\hat{\Gamma}}(Y)
.

Remark 8.19
.

Let
X
be a Banach space. Then,
X
can be itself viewed as a directed graph by setting
V(X)=X
and
E(X)=\{(x,y):x\perp_{BJ}y\}
. The graph isomorphisms with respect to this graph are just Birkhoff-James orthogonality preservers.

We note that an isometric isomorphism
T
from a Banach space
X
onto another
Y
induces graph isomorphisms
f_{T}:\mathop{\hat{\Gamma}}(X)\to\mathop{\hat{\Gamma}}(Y)
and
T:X\to Y
, where
f_{T}
is given by
f_{T}(\overline{x})=\overline{Tx}
for each
x\in X\setminus\{0\}
. Hence, we can define two invariants in
\mathop{\mathrm{core}}\nolimits(\mathbb{K}
-
Ban
{}_{1})
. Let
Quiv
denote the category of directed graphs.

Definition 8.20
.

Define
Quiv
-valued invariants
\hat{F}
and
F_{BJ}
in
\mathop{\mathrm{core}}\nolimits(\mathbb{K}
-
Ban
{}_{1})
by
\hat{F}(X)=\mathop{\hat{\Gamma}}(X)
,
\hat{F}(T)=f_{T}
,
F_{BJ}(X)=X
, and
F_{BJ}(T)=T
, respectively.

Now, we formulate two problems related to Problem
8.5
.

Problem 8.21
.

Are the invariants
\hat{F}
and
F_{BJ}
complete in
\mathop{\mathrm{core}}\nolimits(C^{*}
-
Ban
{}_{1})
?

Remark 8.22
.

Let
X
be a Banach space, and let
x,y\in X
. Then,
x\perp_{BJ}y
in
X
if and only if
x\perp_{BJ}y
in
\overline{X}
. Moreover, we have
V(\mathop{\hat{\Gamma}}(X))=V(\mathop{\hat{\Gamma}}(\overline{X}))
. Hence,
\mathop{\hat{\Gamma}}(X)=\mathop{\hat{\Gamma}}(\overline{X})
, that is,
X\sim_{\mathop{\hat{\Gamma}}}\overline{X}
. From this, we can at most expect from
X\sim_{\mathop{\hat{\Gamma}}}Y
or
X\sim_{BJ}Y
that
X
is isometrically isomorphic to
either

Y
or
\overline{Y}
for general complex Banach spaces
X
and
Y
. Meanwhile, for a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, we always have the isometric isomorphic
A\mapsto A^{*}
from
\mathfrak{A}
onto
\overline{\mathfrak{A}}
. This justify the formulations of Problems
8.5
and
8.21
.
